| `FC11-C06-U053` | Example / comparison | Chapter 6, source L4413 | Example 6.5.1. Consider a non-degenerate quadratic form $q$ on a vector space $V$ over a field of characteristic 2 such that $q$ has an isotropic vector, e.g. if $q$ is an orthogonal direct sum of hyperbolic planes. The so-called symplectic transvection $t ( x ) = x + b _ { q } ( u , x ) u$ , $u \in V$ isotropic, preserves $b _ { q }$ as one directly checks. However, since we are in characteristic two, $q ( t ( x ) ) = q ( x ) + b _ { q } ( u , x ) ^ { 2 }$ and so $t \not \in { \cal O } ( q )$ . The simplest such example is the hyperbolic plane $U _ { \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } }$ over $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ . Then $\mathrm { O } ( U _ { \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } } ) .$ ) consists of the six transformations $\mathrm { i d } , t : = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { 1 } \\ { 1 } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) , s : = \left( \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & { 1 } \\ { 1 } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) , s ^ { 2 } , s t = t s ^ { 2 }$ and $s t ^ { 2 } = t s$ , while ${ \sf O } ( q ) = \{ { \mathrm { i d } } , t \}$ . | — |
| `FC11-C06-U054` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 6, source L4415 | An isometry of determinant $1$ is called a rotation; an isometry of determinant $-1$ is called a reflection. | — |
| `FC11-C06-U055` | Definition 6.5.2 | Chapter 6, source L4417 | Definition 6.5.2. Suppose $2 \neq 0$ in $R$ . The special orthogonal group (or the group of rotations of $L$ ) is the group $\operatorname { S O } ( L ) = \{ g \in \operatorname { O } ( L ) \mid \operatorname* { d e t } ( \varphi ) = 1 \}$ , a normal subgroup of $\mathrm { O } ( L )$ of index at most 2. An isometry $\varphi$ with $\operatorname* { d e t } \varphi = - 1$ will be called a reflection. | — |
| `FC11-C06-U056` | Example / comparison | Chapter 6, source L4419-L4447 | Example 6.5.3. If a non-isotropic $x$ in an $R$-lattice satisfies $2b(x,L)\subset b(x,x)R$, then $\sigma_x(y)=y-2b(x,y)b(x,x)^{-1}x$ is the hyperplane reflection in $x$ and has determinant $-1$. For a quadratic lattice this is $\sigma_x(y)=y-b_q(x,y)q(x)^{-1}x$. | — |
| `FC11-C06-U057` | Remark 6.5.4 | Chapter 6, source L4449-L4451 | Remark 6.5.4. 1. One can define the determinant for an isometry with values in any commutative ring $A$ . However, if $A$ has zero-divisors, this is a less useful concept, especially if all elements are zero-divisors since then $1 = 0$ . This applies for example to the ring $Q ( R ) / R$ and hence to $R$ -torsion modules. 2. There are lattices $( L , b )$ that do not admit reflections, i.e., for which ${ \sf O } ( L ) =$ $\mathsf { S O } ( L )$ . See Example 6.5.5.4 below. | — |
| `FC11-C06-U058` | Definition / construction | Chapter 6, source L4453-L4467 | Every lattice isometry acts on $L^*/L$ and yields reduction homomorphisms $r_L^b:O(L,b)\to O(b_L^\#)$ and, for a quadratic lattice, $r_L^q:O(L,b_q)\to O(q_L^\#)\hookrightarrow O(b_L^\#)$. These maps need not be injective or surjective; scaling a lattice by a non-zero scalar does not change its isometry group. | — |
| `FC11-C06-U059` | Example / comparison | Chapter 6, source L4469-L4477 | Rank one lattices. The isometry group of $\langle a \rangle$ , $a \in R - \{ 0 \}$ is the kernel $R _ { [ 2 ] } ^ { \times }$ of the squaring map on $R ^ { \times }$ . If $( R , \mathfrak { m } )$ is a local ring with 2 a unit, this group only consists of , as we shall see in Remark 7.2.2. The isometry group of the discriminant bilinear form $\langle a ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ is the kernel $( R / a R ) _ { [ 2 ] } ^ { \times }$ of the squaring map on $( R / a R ) ^ { \times }$ . | `FC11-C06-U060` | Example / comparison | Chapter 6, source L4479-L4507 | # 2. Binary forms. (a) We first consider the hyperbolic plane $U _ { R } = R e \oplus R f$ , $e \cdot e = f \cdot f = 0$ , $e \cdot f = 1$ Assume $2 \neq 0$ . Up to units $e$ and $f$ are the only primitive isotropic vectors. Since these are preserved under isometries, the only isometries are the rotations $i _ { a } : = \left( \begin{array} { c c } { { a } } & { { 0 } } \\ { { 0 } } & { { a ^ { - 1 } } } \end{array} \right)$ , $a \in R ^ { \times }$ , and the reflections $j _ { b } : = \binom { 0 } { b } \begin{array} { r } { b ^ { - 1 } } \\ { 0 } \end{array}$ , $b \in R ^ { \times }$ . Observe that $j _ { b }$ is the hyperplane reflection in $e - b f$ . Since $j _ { 1 } \circ j _ { a } = i _ { a }$ , all isometries of $U _ { R }$ are products of at most two hyperplane reflections. Note that these isometries are also the isometries for the corresponding quadratic form. This implies $a _ { \mathrm { 1 1 } } a _ { \mathrm { 2 1 } } = a _ { \mathrm { 1 2 } } a _ { \mathrm { 2 2 } } = 0$ and $a _ { 1 1 } a _ { 2 2 } + a _ { 1 2 } a _ { 2 1 } = 1$ so that the only isometries preserving the quadratic form are just the $j _ { a }$ and the $i _ { b }$ for suitable units $a$ , $b$ . For a non-zero $s$ which is not a unit, the lattice $U _ { R } ( s )$ is not unimodular and its discriminant torsion module $[ s ^ { - 1 } R / R ] ^ { \oplus 2 }$ inherits the hyperbolic form. Its isometry group consists of the isometries $i _ { \bar { a } }$ and $j _ { \bar { b } }$ where now $\bar { a } , \bar { b } \in ( R / s R ) ^ { \times }$ . Hence the reduction homomorphism ?????? is surjective. (b) Next, we return to the situation (see Section 6.3.B) where $k = Q ( R )$ , $K = Q ( S )$ $S = R [ \xi ]$ , a quadratic extension with $\xi ^ { 2 } + u \xi + v = 0$ , and associated quadratic form $\mathsf { N } _ { K / k } ( x + y \xi ) = x ^ { 2 } - u x y + v y ^ { 2 }$ on $K$ (i.e., $x ^ { 2 } - u x y + v y ^ { 2 }$ on $\Phi ^ { 2 } k$ , where $( x , y )$ is identified with $x + y \xi$ ). (b1) Determining the rotations in case $2 \neq 0$ . Multiplication with any $U \in S$ with $N _ { K / k } ( U ) = 1$ induces a $k$ -linear map from $K$ to $K$ that preserves the quadratic form since $N _ { K / k }$ is multiplicative. The matrix of multiplication by $U = a + b \xi$ , $a , b , \in R$ , in the basis $\{ 1 , \xi \}$ is given by $$ M _ { a , b } = \left( \begin{array} { c c } { { a } } & { { - b v } } \\ { { b } } & { { a - u b } } \end{array} \right) . $$ Since $\operatorname* { d e t } \bigl ( M _ { a , b } \bigr ) = a ^ { 2 } - u a b + v b ^ { 2 } = N _ { K / k } ( U ) = 1$ , this is a rotation. We claim that all rotations occur this way. Let $\rho$ be any rotation and let $\rho ( 1 ) = a \cdot 1 + b \xi$ . Then $N _ { K / k } \big ( a + b \xi \big ) = 1$ and $\rho ^ { - 1 } \cdot M _ { a , b } ( 1 ) = 1$ . A small matrix computation shows that the only rotation which fixes 1 is the identity. It follows that $\rho = M _ { a , b }$ . (b2) On reflections in case $2 \neq 0$ . The reflection in $a - 1 + b \xi$ with $N _ { K / k } ( a + b \xi ) =$ $a ^ { 2 } - u a b + v b ^ { 2 } = 1$ has matrix $$ M _ { a , b } ^ { \prime } = \left( \begin{array} { c c } { { a } } & { { - u a + v b } } \\ { { b } } & { { - a } } \end{array} \right) . $$ As in the case of rotations, any orthogonal transformation with determinant $^ { - 1 }$ is of this shape. Observe also that $M _ { a , b } ^ { \prime } \circ M _ { 1 , 0 } ^ { \prime } = M _ { a , b }$ , and so all isometries are products of at most two reflections. (b3) The case $2 = 0$ . We still have the preceding wo types of orthogonal transformations despite both having determinant 1. The first type $M _ { a , b }$ comes from multiplication with norm 1 elements. However, a matrix for a reflection cannot come from multiplication with norm 1 vectors, since if this were the case, we would have $u b = 0 = u a$ and so $u = 0$ but then the extension $R ( \xi ) / R$ would not be separable. We shall see later (cf. Example 16.2.5.3) that in this case the two types of orthogonal transformations are distinguished by their Dickson invariants. | `FC11-C06-U049`, `FC11-C06-U053`, `FC11-C06-U058` |

| `FC11-C06-U062` | Example / comparison | Chapter 6, source L4511-L4517 | Consider the quadratic form $q ( x , y ) = a x ^ { 2 } + 2 x y + c y ^ { 2 }$ on $\mathbb { Z } ^ { 2 }$ with $a , c \in \mathbb { Z }$ and $a \ge 2$ and $c > a$ . The only integral solution for $q ( x , y ) = a$ is $( x , y ) = ( \pm 1 , 0 )$ . | `FC11-C07-U003` | Example / comparison | Chapter 7, source L4553-L4557 | Let $R = \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ where $p$ is any prime. Quadratic modules over this ring are the quadratic $p$ -adic lattices. We claim that every (non-degenerate) quadratic $p$ -adic lattice $( L , q )$ admits reflections and so here also $\mathsf { S O } ( L )$ is of index 2 in $\mathrm { O } ( L )$ . We see this as follows. Since the valuation $v _ { p }$ is non-archimedean and since for all $z , y \in L$ , $b _ { q } ( z , y ) = q ( z + y ) - q ( z ) - q ( y )$ , equation (A.4) implies $v _ { p } ( b _ { q } ( z , y ) ) \geq \operatorname* { i n f } _ { u \in L , q ( u ) \neq 0 } v _ { p } ( q ( u ) )$ . That the (non-zero) infimum is attained, say at $x$ , is clear since the valuation is discrete. It follows that $v _ { p } \left( b _ { q } ( x , y ) / q ( x ) \right) \geq 0$ , which translates as $b _ { q } ( x , y ) \in q ( x ) \cdot \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ and so (6.6) holds. Hence the reflection $\sigma _ { x }$ in $x$ is a well-defined isometry. We set this result apart since it will be used later: $$ \operatorname* { i n f } _ { z \in L . a ( z ) \neq 0 } v _ { p } ( q ( z ) ) = v _ { p } ( q ( x ) ) \implies \sigma _ { x } { \mathrm { ~ i s ~ a ~ h y p e r p l a n e ~ r e f l e c t i o n ~ o f ~ } } L . $$ | — |

The condition (6.6) implies that a reflection is of the form $\sigma _ { c }$ with $c = u e + v f$ for some units $u$ , $v$ . Then $\sigma _ { c } ( e ) = - v u ^ { - 1 } \cdot f$ and $\sigma _ { c } ( f ) = - v ^ { - 1 } u \cdot e$ , i.e., $\sigma _ { c } ~ = ~ j _ { b }$ with $b \ = \ - v u ^ { - 1 }$ . | `FC11-C07-U005` | Example / comparison | Chapter 7, source L4561-L4563 | Consider the unimodular dyadic lattice $V _ { 0 }$ , i.e., $\mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } ^ { \oplus 2 }$ with quadratic form $x =$ $( x _ { 1 } , x _ { 2 } ) \mapsto q ( x ) = x _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 1 } x _ { 2 } + x _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ . It contains no isotropic vectors (such an isotropic vector would lead to a solution of the equation $x ^ { 2 } = - 3$ in $\mathbb { Q } _ { 2 }$ which is impossible by Theorem A.2.1-2) and all reflections are of the form $\sigma _ { x }$ , $q ( x ) \in \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } ^ { * }$ . This concerns every primitive vector since then, as we readily see, $q ( x )$ is a unit due to the special shape of the quadratic form. We end this sections with a few words about the case $2 = 0$ . Here the situation is quite different. For example, if $V = W \oplus k e$ is a quadratic space over a field of characteristic 2 such that $q ( e ) = 1$ , then $\sigma _ { e }$ is the identity. More generally, if $( V , q )$ is quadratic $k$ -space and $x$ satisfies $q ( x ) \neq 0$ and $x \in \operatorname { r a d } ( b _ { q } )$ , then $\sigma _ { x }$ is the identity on $V$ . If $q ( x ) \neq 0$ , then the corresponding map $\sigma _ { x }$ is an example of a so-called transvection w.r.t. the bilinear form $b _ { q }$ , see Appendix A.5. | — |

| `FC11-C07-U015` | Corollary 7.2.8 | Chapter 7, source L4621-L4622 | Corollary 7.2.8 (Witt’s extension theorem). Let $( V , b )$ be an inner product space over $R$ and $W _ { 1 } , W _ { 2 } \subset V$ two $R$ -submodules such that $b \| _ { W _ { 1 } } , \ b \| _ { W _ { 2 } }$ are unimodular. Any isometry $W _ { 1 } \stackrel { \simeq } { \to } W _ { 2 }$ can be extended to an isometry of $V$ . In particular, any two primitive embeddings $W \hookrightarrow V$ , $W$ unimodular, are equivalent. In other words, $\operatorname { O } ( V )$ acts transitively on such primitive embeddings. | — |

| `FC11-C07-U018` | Definition 7.2.11 | Chapter 7, source L4659 | Definition 7.2.11. Let $( V , q )$ be an inner-product space over a local ring. The Witt index ${ \sf W } _ { \tau } ( V , q )$ of $( V , q )$ is the dimension of a maximal isotropic submodule of $V$ . | — |

| `FC11-C07-U020` | Remark 7.2.12 | Chapter 7, source L4665 | Remark 7.2.12. The Witt index is not stable under ring extension since a form which does not represents zero over $R$ may very well do so over a larger ring. However, if $R$ is an integral domain with quotient field $Q ( R )$ , the Witt indices over $R$ and $Q ( R )$ are the same. This applies to integral lattices $L$ and shows that for those the Witt index is an invariant of $L _ { \mathbb { Q } }$ and $\mathsf { W } _ { \tau } ( L _ { \mathbb { Q } } ) \leq \mathsf { W } _ { \tau } ( L _ { \mathbb { R } } )$ . The equality may be strict. We have seen this already in Example 1.10.6 where we showed that $x ^ { 2 } + y ^ { 2 } - 3 z ^ { 2 }$ has no isotropic vector over $\mathbb { Q }$ and so has Witt index 0 while over the reals it has Witt index 1. Note that the latter is directly related to the signature. Indeed, if $L$ is non-degenerate with signature $( s , t )$ one has $\mathsf { W } _ { \tau } ( L _ { \mathbb { R } } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } ( s , t )$ . | `FC11-C01-U140`, `FC11-C07-U018` |

Every non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form over a field $k$ of characteristic different from 2 is isometric to a diagonal form $$ \langle a _ { 1 } \rangle \oplus \cdots \oplus \langle a _ { n } \rangle , w i t h a _ { j } \in k ^ { \times } , j = 1 , \ldots , n . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C08-U002` | Remark 8.1.2 | Chapter 8, source L4729-L4733 | Remark 8.1.2. 1. For the field $\mathbb { R }$ we may further simplify this: since every positive number is a square, we may assume that $a _ { j } \in \{ 1 , - 1 \}$ . The number of times 1 or $^ { - 1 }$ appears is unique as stated by Sylvester’s theorem. Its proof is recalled below (see Corollary 8.1.3). 2. For an algebraically closed field such as the field $\mathbb { C }$ we may assume that the $a _ { j }$ are all equal to 1, i.e., every form is equivalent to a sum of squares. What about uniqueness of the above representations? The Witt decomposition $V = U ^ { \circledast m } \circledast V ^ { \prime }$ , where $V ^ { \prime }$ is totally anisotropic, has the merit of being unique. Over $\mathbb { R }$ and $\mathbb { C }$ the diagonal decomposition of $V ^ { \prime }$ is also unique. It is instructive to compare this with what we said about the Witt ring of $\mathbb { C }$ and $\mathbb { R }$ in Example 7.3.2. In Section 8.3 we show uniqueness over finite fields. However, in general we have no uniqueness, as explained in Chapter 3 where we investigated this for the field $\mathbb { Q }$ . | `FC11-C07-U022` |
| `FC11-C08-U003` | Corollary 8.1.3 | Chapter 8, source L4735-L4742 | Corollary 8.1.3 (Sylvester’s Law). Let $( V , b )$ be a real inner product space. There is a subspace $V ^ { + }$ (respectively $V ^ { - }$ ) of maximal dimension $d ^ { + }$ (respectively $d ^ { - }$ ) on which $^ { b }$ is positive (negative) such that $$ V = V ^ { + } \oplus V ^ { - } . $$ Moreover, all such spaces $V ^ { \pm }$ have the same dimension $d ^ { \pm }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C08-U004` | Definition 8.1.4 | Chapter 8, source L4753 | Definition 8.1.4. Referring to (8.1), the pair $( \dim V ^ { + } , \dim V ^ { - } )$ is called the signature of the inner product space $V$ over $\mathbb { R }$ . The difference $\tau ( V ) = \dim V ^ { + } - \dim V ^ { - }$ is called the index . If $V = V ^ { + }$ , the inner product space is called positive definite, if $V = V ^ { - }$ we say that $V$ is negative definite. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U005` | Theorem 8.1.5 | Chapter 8, source L4757 | Theorem 8.1.5 (Classification of real forms). Two real inner product spaces $( V , b )$ and $( V ^ { \prime } , b ^ { \prime } )$ are isometric if and only if $\dim V = \dim V ^ { \prime }$ and $\tau ( V ) = \tau ( V ^ { \prime } )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C08-U006` | Proposition 8.2.1 | Chapter 8, source L4763-L4764 | Proposition 8.2.1. Let $( V , b )$ be a symmetric inner product space over a field $k$ of characteristic 2. Then $V$ is isometric to an orthogonal direct sum $V _ { 1 } \odot V _ { 2 }$ , where $V _ { 1 }$ is a diagonal form and $V _ { 2 }$ is a direct sum of hyperbolic planes. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U007` | Definition / construction | Chapter 8, source L4781-L4787 | The Arf invariant of a non-degenerate quadratic form in characteristic $2$ is the class obtained from a symplectic basis by the standard sum $\sum q(e_i)q(f_i)$ modulo the Artin--Schreier subgroup. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U008` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 8, source L4787 | no longer depends on the particular symplectic basis. It is called the Arf invariant of $q$ : | `FC11-C08-U007` |
| `FC11-C08-U009` | Theorem 8.2.2 | Chapter 8, source L4789-L4796 | Theorem 8.2.2. A non-degenerate quadratic form on $V$ in characteristic 2 is equivalent to a form $\begin{array} { r } { q = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } x _ { i } x _ { n + i } + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { 2 n } a _ { j } x _ { j } ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ , for some $a _ { j } \in k$ . 1. The Arf invariant of such a form is well defined. 2. Isometric non-degenerate quadratic forms have the same Arf invariant. 3. If $k$ is a perfect field of characteristic 2, then two non-degenerate quadratic forms of the same rank and the same Arf invariant are isometric. | `FC11-C08-U007` |
| `FC11-C08-U010` | Corollary 8.2.3 | Chapter 8, source L4875-L4881 | Corollary 8.2.3. Let $( V , q )$ be a quadratic inner product space of rank $2 n$ over a perfect field of characteristic 2. Then coordinates may be chosen so that $q$ is equivalent to a form with Arf invariant $c ^ { 2 } \equiv c$ mod $\wp ( k )$ given by $$ q ^ { ( c ) } ( x ) : = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n } x _ { i } x _ { n + i } + c ( x _ { n } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 2 n } ^ { 2 } ) \ = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { n - 1 } x _ { i } x _ { n + i } + x _ { n } x _ { 2 n } + c ( x _ { n } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 2 n } ^ { 2 } ) , \quad c \in k . $$ This form is isometric to $\mathbb { Q } ^ { n - 1 } U \mathbb { Q } P ^ { ( c ) }$ where the binary form on $P ^ { ( c ) }$ is given by $x y + c ( x ^ { 2 } + y ^ { 2 } )$ . | `FC11-C08-U007` |
| `FC11-C08-U011` | Example / comparison | Chapter 8, source L4883 | Example 8.2.4. The cyclic group $\mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ can also be viewed as the field $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ and the ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \mathbb { Z } / \mathbb { Z }$ -valued torsion forms $u _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 1 }$ from Examples 1.9.5 give $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ -valued forms $U$ and $V$ with Arf invariants $0$ , $^ { 1 }$ , respectively. So for $k = \mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ the only two types of rank $2 n$ non-degenerate quadratic forms are $U ^ { \left( \perp \right) n }$ and $U ^ { \oplus ( n - 1 ) } \oplus V$ . Below, in Section 8.3, we generalize this for all finite fields. | `FC11-C01-U121`, `FC11-C08-U007` |
| `FC11-C08-U012` | Proposition 8.2.5 | Chapter 8, source L4887-L4899 | Proposition 8.2.5. Let $k$ be a field of characteristic 2. 1. A $k$ -vector space $V$ with a symmetric bilinear form $b$ is isometric to $\mathbb { O } _ { i = 1 } ^ { s } \langle a _ { i } \rangle \mathbb { O }$ $U ^ { \oplus t } \oplus \operatorname { r a d } ( b )$ , $a _ { i } \in k ^ { \times }$ , $i = 1 , \dots , s$ . 2. A $k$ -vector space $V$ with a quadratic form $q$ is isometric to $q ^ { \prime } \textcircled { \div } \mathrm { r a d } ( b _ { q } )$ where (a) $q ^ { \prime }$ n-degenera, for some dratic form equivalent to a form . $\textstyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } x _ { i } x _ { m + i } +$ $\textstyle \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { 2 m } a _ { j } x _ { j } ^ { 2 }$ $a _ { j } \in k$ (b) $) \mathrm { ~ r a d } ( b _ { q } ) = V _ { q } \textcircled { \div } \mathrm { r a d } ( q )$ , with $q \| _ { V _ { q } }$ diagonal of rank equal to $\dim V _ { q } = s$ , $s \leq [ k : k ^ { 2 } ]$ . If $k$ is a perfect field (e.g. if $k = \mathbb { F } _ { 2 ^ { r } }$ , a finite field, or $k$ is algebraically closed), then (a) $q ^ { \prime } \simeq U ^ { \oplus ( m - 1 ) } \oplus P ^ { ( c ) }$ . (b) $s = 0$ or $s = 1$ . Hence $\operatorname { r a d } ( b _ { q } ) = \operatorname { r a d } ( q )$ or $\operatorname { r a d } ( b _ { q } ) = \langle a \rangle \oplus \operatorname { r a d } ( q )$ , $a \in k ^ { \times }$ . In the last case, $i f \operatorname { r a d } ( q ) = 0$ , $q$ is semi-unimodular. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U013` | Remark 8.2.6 | Chapter 8, source L4917 | Remark 8.2.6. Note that if $\operatorname { r a d } ( q ) = 0$ and $\dim V = s$ is odd, the semi-discriminant sdisc(??) | `FC11-C08-U014` | Example / comparison | Chapter 8, source L4919-L4921 | Example 8.2.7. Over $\mathbf F_2$, non-degenerate even-dimensional quadratic spaces have the two isometry types distinguished by Arf invariant $0$ or $1$; in odd dimension, a semi-unimodular form is the corresponding even-dimensional normal form plus one square term. | `FC11-C08-U007` |
| `FC11-C08-U015` | Example / comparison | Chapter 8, source L4941-L4965 | Example 8.3.1. Over a finite field $k$, every totally anisotropic binary quadratic form is isometric to a scalar norm form for the unique quadratic extension $K/k$. In characteristic $2$, writing $K=k(\xi)$ with $\xi^2+\xi+c=0$, irreducibility is equivalent to $\operatorname{Tr}_{k/\mathbf F_2}(c)=1$, and the norm form is $x^2+xy+cy^2$. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U016` | Remark 8.3.2 | Chapter 8, source L4967-L4974 | Remark 8.3.2. We just explained that to classify quadratic spaces over a finite field of characteristic 2, one needs to specify an element with absolute trace equal to 1 and that for $k = \mathbb { F } _ { 2 ^ { r } }$ with $r$ odd, one can take $c = 1$ . To see what happens for $r$ even, let us first consider the case of $k$ iterated quadratic extensions, say $k _ { 0 } = \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } \subset k _ { 1 } = \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ( s _ { 1 } ) \subset \cdots \subset k _ { m } = k _ { m - 1 } ( s _ { m } )$ , where $s _ { j }$ is a root of an Artin– Schreier polynomial $X ^ { 2 } + X + c _ { j }$ . We claim that the product $c _ { 1 } \cdots c _ { m }$ has absolute trace 1. To show this, note that $\mathsf { T r } _ { k _ { j } / k _ { j - 1 } } ( c _ { j } ) = 1$ by the choice of $c _ { j }$ so that recursively $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \mathsf { T r } _ { k _ { m } / k _ { 0 } } ( c _ { 1 } \cdot \cdot \cdot c _ { m } ) = \mathsf { T r } _ { k _ { m } / k _ { m - 1 } } ( c _ { m } ) \cdot \mathsf { T r } _ { k _ { m - 1 } / k _ { 0 } } ( c _ { 1 } \cdot \cdot \cdot c _ { m - 1 } ) } \\ & { \phantom { \frac { 1 } { 1 } } = \mathsf { T r } _ { k _ { m - 1 } / k _ { 0 } } ( c _ { 1 } \cdot \cdot \cdot c _ { m - 1 } ) = 1 . } \end{array} $$ For the general case $r = 2 ^ { m } s$ with $s$ odd, we simply write $k$ as an extension of $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 ^ { m } }$ . Since $\deg ( k / \mathbb { F } _ { 2 ^ { m } } ) = s$ is odd, $\mathsf { T r } _ { k / \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } } ( c ) = s \mathsf { T r } _ { \mathbb { F } _ { 2 ^ { m } } / \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } } ( c ) = 1$ as well. | — |
| `FC11-C08-U017` | Theorem 8.3.3 | Chapter 8, source L4976-L4981 | Theorem 8.3.3 (Classification of quadratic inner product spaces over finite fields). Let $k$ be a finite field of characteristic $p$ , $K$ the unique quadratic extension field of $k$ and let $V$ be a quadratic inner product space over $k$ . $p \neq 2$ • $\dim V$ even. Then $V \simeq U ^ { \oplus m }$ or $V \simeq U ^ { \oplus m } \oplus \mathsf { N } _ { K / k }$ . The form $\mathsf { N } _ { K / k }$ is isometric to a diagonal form $\left. 1 \right. \ @ \left. - \varepsilon \right.$ with ?? a non-square. • $\dim V$ odd. Then $V \simeq U ^ { \oplus m } \oplus \langle c \rangle$ and there are two isometry classes according to whether $c \neq 0$ is a square or not. In both cases, the given decomposition is the Witt decomposition and so the Witt index takes only the values ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \dim V$ , ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \left( \dim V - 1 \right)$ or ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } ( \dim V - 2 )$ . $p = 2$ Then dim ?? is even, and either $V \simeq U ^ { \oplus m }$ with zero Arf invariant, or $V \simeq$ $U ^ { \oplus m } \oplus \mathsf { N } _ { K / k }$ with non-zero Arf invariant. More precisely, if $K = k ( \xi )$ with $\xi ^ { 2 } + \xi + c = 0$ , then $\mathsf { N } _ { K / k }$ is isometric to the binary form $c x ^ { 2 } + x y + c y ^ { 2 }$ with Arf invariant $c ^ { 2 } \equiv c$ mod $\wp ( k )$ . | `FC11-C08-U007` |

| `FC11-C08-U019` | Remark 8.3.5 | Chapter 8, source L4992 | Remark 8.3.5. | `FC11-C09-U001` | Lemma 9.1.1 | Chapter 9, source L5032-L5038 | Lemma 9.1.1. The torsion module $G$ is isomorphic to its dual $G _ { Q ( R ) / R } ^ { * }$ . Let $$ G \simeq \oplus _ { j = 1 } ^ { r } R / d _ { j } R , \quad d _ { 1 } \| d _ { 2 } \| \cdot \cdot \cdot \| d _ { r } $$ be the invariant factor decomposition with corresponding generators $e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { r }$ . Then the isomorphism $G \stackrel { \simeq } { \to } G _ { Q ( R ) / R } ^ { * }$ can be chosen such that $e _ { j }$ maps to the function $u _ { j } ^ { \ast } \in G _ { Q ( R ) / R } ^ { \ast }$ for which $u _ { j } ^ { \ast } ( e _ { i } ) = d _ { j } ^ { - 1 } \delta _ { i j } \in Q ( R ) / R$ . | — |
| `FC11-C09-U002` | Definition 9.1.2 | Chapter 9, source L5042 | Definition 9.1.2. An ordered basis of $G$ is a system of generators $e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { r }$ of $G$ adapted to the elementary divisors $\{ d _ { 1 } , \ldots , d _ { r } \}$ ordered in such a way that $d _ { 1 } \| d _ { 2 } \| \cdots \| d _ { r }$ as in the above lemma. | — |
| `FC11-C09-U003` | Lemma 9.1.3 | Chapter 9, source L5046-L5050 | Lemma 9.1.3. Let $B ^ { \prime } = ( B _ { i j } )$ be a symmetric $r \times r$ matrix with entries in the field $Q ( R )$ , Then $B = B ^ { \prime }$ mod $R$ is the Gram matrix of a symmetric torsion form on $G$ relative to an ordered basis for $G$ adapted to the elementary divisors $\{ d _ { 1 } , \ldots , d _ { r } \}$ if and only if $d _ { i } B _ { i j } \in R$ and $d _ { j } B _ { i j } \in R$ for $i , j = 1 , \dots , r$ . In other words, using that $d _ { 1 } \| d _ { 2 } \| \cdots \| d _ { r }$ , this is the case if and only if $B ^ { \prime }$ has the form $$ B ^ { \prime } = ( B _ { i j } ) = \left( \begin{array} { c c c c } { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 1 1 } } { d _ { 1 } } } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 1 2 } } { d _ { 1 } } } } & { { \cdots } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 1 r } } { d _ { 1 } } } } \\ { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 2 1 } } { d _ { 1 } } } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 2 2 } } { d _ { 2 } } } } & { { \cdots } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { 2 r } } { d _ { 2 } } } } \\ { { \vdots } } & { { \ddots } } & { { \vdots } } & { { \vdots } } \\ { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { r 1 } } { d _ { 1 } } } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { r 2 } } { d _ { 2 } } } } & { { \cdots } } & { { \displaystyle \frac { A _ { r r } } { d _ { r } } } } \end{array} \right) \qquad \boldsymbol { w i t h } \ A _ { i j } = A _ { j i } \in { \cal R } . $$ | — |

| `FC11-C09-U005` | Definition / construction | Chapter 9, source L5086 | The reduced discriminant $\delta(b)$ is the discriminant invariant attached to a non-degenerate symmetric torsion form. | — |

| `FC11-C09-U007` | Lemma 9.1.5 | Chapter 9, source L5096-L5101 | Lemma 9.1.5. Let $( G , b )$ , $( G ^ { \prime } , b ^ { \prime } )$ be two non-degenerate $p$ -primary bilinear torsion forms 1 and let $p ^ { a }$ , $p ^ { a ^ { \prime } }$ be the first elementary divisors of $G$ , respectively $G ^ { \prime }$ . Then the reduced discriminant of $G \oplus G ^ { \prime }$ is the product $\delta ( b ) \cdot \delta ( b ^ { \prime } )$ considered in $\mathsf { D } ( R / p ^ { c } R )$ , $c = \operatorname* { m i n } ( a , a ^ { \prime } )$ , under the homomorphism which is induced by multiplication $$ R / p ^ { a } R \times R / p ^ { a ^ { \prime } } R \longrightarrow R / p ^ { c } R . $$ | `FC11-C09-U006` |

| `FC11-C09-U010` | Proposition 9.2.1 | Chapter 9, source L5133-L5151 | Proposition 9.2.1. 1. There is a commutative diagram $$ \begin{array} { l } { G \times G \xrightarrow { b \quad \quad \quad } \displaystyle > { \mathbb Q } / { \mathbb Z } } \\ { \displaystyle \int _ { G _ { p } \times G _ { p } } ^ { \textstyle \int \quad \quad } \displaystyle \iint _ { 0 } ^ { } } \\ { G _ { p } \times G _ { p } \xrightarrow { b \| _ { G _ { p } } \quad \quad > { \mathbb Q } ^ { ( p ) } / { \mathbb Z } } \xrightarrow { \quad \quad \quad } \displaystyle { \mathbb Q } _ { p } / { \mathbb Z } _ { p } . } \end{array} $$ In particular, $( G _ { p } , b \| _ { G _ { p } } )$ is in a natural way a $p$ -primary symmetric torsion group. 2. The Sylow decomposition is orthogonal, i.e. $( G , b ) = \oplus _ { p } ( G _ { p } , b \| _ { G _ { p } } )$ . Similar considerations hold for quadratic torsion forms $( G , q )$ , where the polar form $b _ { q }$ is used. Since $q ( x ) = { \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } } b _ { q } ( x , x )$ , the values $q ( x )$ for $x \in G _ { p }$ belong to $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathbb { Q } ^ { ( p ) } / \mathbb { Z } \simeq \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathbb { Q } _ { p } / \mathbb { Z } _ { p } } \end{array}$ which is isomorphic to $\mathbb { Q } _ { p } / \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ if $p$ is odd, since then 2 is a unit in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ . For $p = 2$ we have $\begin{array} { r } { \frac { 1 } { 2 } \mathbb { Q } ^ { ( 2 ) } / \mathbb { Z } = \mathbb { Q } ^ { ( 2 ) } / \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } \simeq \mathbb { Q } _ { 2 } / \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . So for quadratic forms the diagram of Proposition 9.2.1 becomes $$ \begin{array} { l } { G \xrightarrow { q } \mathbb { Q } / \mathbb { Z } } \\ { \displaystyle \int _ { G _ { p } } \omega \xrightarrow [ q ] { \mathcal { q } } \mathbb { \bigcup } _ { \mathbb { Q } ^ { ( p ) } / \mathbb { Z } \xrightarrow [ \infty ] { } \mathbb { Q } _ { p } / \mathbb { Z } _ { p } . } } \end{array} $$ Hence also $( G _ { p } , q \| _ { G _ { p } } )$ is in a natural way a $p$ -primary quadratic torsion group and one has an orthogonal Sylow decomposition $( G , q ) = \oplus _ { p } ( G _ { p } , q \| _ { G _ { p } } )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C09-U011` | Lemma 9.3.1 | Chapter 9, source L5163-L5172 | Lemma 9.3.1. 1. Let $( H , b )$ be a symmetric form on a homogeneous $p$ -primary torsion group of exponent $k$ . Then the Gram matrix of $^ { b }$ with respect to an ordered basis is represented by $$ B = p ^ { - k } A , \quad A = ( A _ { i j } ) , A _ { i j } = A _ { j i } \in \mathbb { Z } , $$ and $b$ is non-degenerate if and only if $\operatorname* { d e t } ( A )$ is relatively prime to $p$ . 2. | `FC11-C09-U014` | Lemma 9.3.3 | Chapter 9, source L5191-L5194 | Lemma 9.3.3. If $G$ is a homogeneous $p$ -primary group of exponent $k$ equipped with a symmetric $p$ -primary torsion form $^ { b }$ , then $( G , b )$ is non-degenerate if and only if $\rho ( b )$ is. More precisely, $b \simeq p ^ { - k } \cdot b ^ { \prime }$ where $b ^ { \prime }$ is unimodular and is (under suitable identifications) isometric to $\rho ( b )$ . In that case, if $H$ splits off orthogonally, then so does $\rho ( H )$ and conversely. | — |

If $G = \oplus H _ { m }$ with $H _ { m }$ homogeneous of exponent ??, then $\rho _ { k } ( G ) = \rho _ { k } ( H _ { k } ) =$ $\rho ( H _ { k } )$ for every $k$ . | — |
| `FC11-C09-U017` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 9, source L5237-L5241 | analogous to the one in (9.5). Explicitly, if $[ x ]$ is the coset containing $x \in G _ { k }$ we put for $x , y \in G _ { k }$ $$ \rho _ { k } ( b ) ( [ x ] , [ y ] ) : = p ^ { k - 1 } b ( x , y ) . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C09-U018` | Lemma 9.3.6 | Chapter 9, source L5251-L5256 | Lemma 9.3.6. Let $( G , b )$ be a $p$ -primary symmetric torsion group with Jordan decomposition $G = \oplus H _ { k }$ . 1. If $b$ is non-degenerate, then $\rho _ { k } ( b )$ is non-degenerate for all $k$ . 2. If $\rho _ { k } ( b )$ is non-degenerate, then $b \| _ { H _ { k } }$ is non-degenerate. | — |
| `FC11-C09-U019` | Proposition 9.3.7 | Chapter 9, source L5263-L5264 | Proposition 9.3.7. A non-degenerate symmetric $p$ -primary torsion group $( G , b )$ admits a Jordan splitting, that is, an orthogonal Jordan decomposition2 $G =$ $\mathbb { O } _ { k = 1 } ^ { s } H _ { k }$ , where, we recall, each $H _ { k }$ is a homogeneous $p$ -primary torsion group of exponent $k$ equipped with a non-degenerate symmetric form. Moreover, each of the symmetric torsion forms $\rho _ { k } ( b )$ are non-degenerate and the reduced discriminant3 $\delta ( b )$ is invertible.4 | `FC11-C09-U006` |
| `FC11-C09-U020` | Definition 9.3.8 | Chapter 9, source L5271-L5275 | Definition 9.3.8. The basic invariants under isometry of a non-degenerate $p$ -primary symmetric torsion group $( G , b )$ are: • the number of elementary divisors of $G$ equal to $p ^ { k }$ $=$ the length of $H _ { k }$ in any Jordan splitting $G = \mathbb { Q } H _ { k }$ of $G$ = the rank of $\rho _ { k } ( G )$ as a $\mathbb { Z } / p \mathbb { Z }$ -module; • if $p$ is odd, for each $k$ as above, $\mathrm { d i s c } ( \rho _ { k } ( b ) ) \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { F } _ { p } )$ . For $p$ -primary quadratic torsion groups $( G , q )$ the invariants are those of the associated polar form $b _ { q }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C09-U021` | Lemma 9.3.9 | Chapter 9, source L5281-L5288 | Lemma 9.3.9. Let $p$ be an odd prime and let $G$ be a $p$ -primary torsion equipped with a quadratic form $q$ . Define $\rho _ { k } ( q )$ on $\rho _ { k } ( G )$ by $$ \rho _ { k } ( q ) [ x ] : = \ c l a s s \ o f \ p ^ { k - 1 } q ( x ) \ i n \ \mathbb { Q } ^ { ( p ) } / \mathbb { Z } . $$ This form is a well-defined $p ^ { - 1 } \mathbb { Z } / \mathbb { Z }$ -valued quadratic form on $\rho _ { k } ( G )$ . It is nondegenerate if and only if $q$ is. | — |
| `FC11-C09-U022` | Definition 9.3.10 | Chapter 9, source L5297-L5301 | Definition 9.3.10. Let $G$ be a 2-primary torsion group equipped with a symmetric form $^ { b }$ ). Its halving , $( \pmb { G } , \pmb { b } ) = ( \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } \pmb { G } , \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } \pmb { b } )$ , is the pair consisting of a 2-primary torsion group $\textbf { G }$ of exponent $\geq 2$ together with a symmetric form $\pmb { b }$ on $\textbf { G }$ constructed as follows: For each summand $G _ { k }$ of exponent $k$ in a chosen decomposition of $G$ . choose generators, $e _ { k 1 } , \ldots , e _ { k r }$ (with $r$ depending on $k$ ). Define $\mathbf { G } _ { k }$ by generators ${ \pmb e } _ { k j }$ , $j = 1 , \dotsc , r$ , of order precisely $2 ^ { k + 1 }$ and set $\pmb { G } = \bigoplus \pmb { G } _ { k }$ . The form $\mathbf { b } = { \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } } b$ is given by $$ { \pmb b } ( { \pmb e } _ { k i } , { \pmb e } _ { \ell j } ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } b ( { \pmb e } _ { k i } , { \pmb e } _ { \ell j } ) \in { \mathbb { Q } } ^ { ( 2 ) } / { \mathbb { Z } } . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C09-U023` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 9, source L5311-L5315 | For a non-degenerate symmetric $2$-primary torsion group $(G,b)$ without order-$2$ summands, the doubling construction puts a quadratic form on $2G=G/G_1$ by $q(\bar x)=b(x,x)$. | — |
| `FC11-C09-U024` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 9, source L5319-L5327 | The doubling construction carries a non-degenerate symmetric $2$-primary torsion form without order-$2$ summands to a non-degenerate quadratic form on $2G$: its polar form satisfies $b_q(\bar x,\bar y)=2b(x,y)$, so a vector in its radical lifts to $G_1$. | `FC11-C09-U023` |
| `FC11-C09-U025` | Lemma 9.3.11 | Chapter 9, source L5329-L5332 | Lemma 9.3.11. Let $( G , q )$ be a 2-primary quadratic torsion group. Then the halving $( \pmb { G } , \pmb { b } )$ of $( G , b _ { q } )$ gives a symmetric torsion group (without cyclic summands of order 2) such that we retrieve $( G , q )$ via $2 ( G , \pmb { b } )$ as above. Also $( G , q )$ is non-degenerate if and only if $( G , b )$ is non-degenerate. | `FC11-C09-U022` |
| `FC11-C09-U026` | Theorem 9.3.12 | Chapter 9, source L5342-L5346 | Theorem 9.3.12. A non-degenerate $p$ -primary quadratic form $( G , q )$ admits $a$ Jordan splitting ?? = ⦹????=1????. Moreover, 1. For each $k = 1 , \dots , s$ , the restriction $q \| _ { H _ { k } }$ is non-degenerate if and only if the quadratic torsion form $\rho _ { k } ( q )$ is non-degenerate. Under suitable identifications $q \vert H _ { k }$ is isometric to $p ^ { - k } \rho _ { k } ( q )$ . 2. The reduced discriminant $\delta ( b _ { q } )$ is invertible if and only if the quadratic torsion forms $\rho _ { k } ( q )$ , $k = 1 , \dots , s$ , are non-degenerate. | `FC11-C09-U006` |
| `FC11-C09-U027` | Proposition 9.4.1 | Chapter 9, source L5361-L5362 | Proposition 9.4.1. Let $p$ be odd. $A$ non-degenerate homogeneous $p$ -primary symmetric torsion form $^ { b }$ of exponent $k$ and length $r$ is isometric to $\mathbb { O } ^ { r } \langle p ^ { - k } \rangle$ if $\mathrm { d i s c } ( \rho _ { k } ( b ) ) = 1$ and to ${ \mathbb { O } ^ { r - 1 } \langle p ^ { - k } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle \epsilon p ^ { - k } \rangle }$ if d $\mathrm { l i s c } ( \rho _ { k } ( b ) ) = \epsilon$ , a non-square modulo $p$ . A similar assertion holds for quadratic forms. | — |
| `FC11-C09-U028` | Proposition 9.4.2 | Chapter 9, source L5367-L5383 | Proposition 9.4.2. • A non-degenerate symmetric (respectively quadratic) form on a homogeneous 2-primary group of exponent $k \geq 2$ and length $r$ is isometric to $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \quad \mathbb { O } _ { j = 1 } ^ { a } \langle u ^ { ( j ) } 2 ^ { - k } \rangle \mathbb { O } ^ { b } u _ { k } \mathbb { O } ^ { c } v _ { k } \quad a + 2 ( b + c ) = r , \quad u ^ { ( j ) } \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) } \\ & { \quad \mathbb { O } _ { j = 1 } ^ { a } [ u ^ { ( j ) } 2 ^ { - k - 1 } ] \mathbb { O } ^ { b } u _ { k } \mathbb { O } ^ { c } v _ { k } \quad r e s p e c t i v e l y . } \end{array} $$ • A non-degenerate symmetric form on a homogeneous 2-primary group of exponent 1 and length $r$ is isometric to $$ \mathbb { O } _ { j = 1 } ^ { a } \langle u ^ { ( j ) } 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \mathbb { O } ^ { b } u _ { 1 } , \quad a + 2 b = r , \quad u ^ { ( j ) } \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) . $$ • A non-degenerate quadratic form on a homogeneous 2-primary group of exponent 1 and length $r$ is isometric to $$ \begin{array} { r } { \mathbb { Q } ^ { a } [ u \cdot 2 ^ { - 2 } ] \ \mathbb { Q } ^ { b } \ u _ { 1 } \ \mathbb { Q } ^ { c } \ v _ { 1 } , \quad a + 2 b + 2 c = r , \quad u \in 1 , 3 = \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z } ) . } \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC11-C10-U001` | Example / comparison | Chapter 10, source L5405-L5407 | The dyadic rank-two lattices $U_k=U_{\mathbf Z_2}(2^k)$ are indecomposable; $U_0$ is the symmetric hyperbolic lattice and its associated quadratic form is $(x_1,x_2)\mapsto x_1x_2$. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U002` | Example / comparison | Chapter 10, source L5409-L5413 | The dyadic rank-two lattices $V_k=V(2^k)$, with $V$ having Gram matrix $\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}$, are indecomposable and are not isometric to $U_k$; $\operatorname{disc}(V_k)=3\cdot2^{2k}$ modulo unit squares. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U003` | Lemma 10.1.1 | Chapter 10, source L5417-L5418 | Lemma 10.1.1. The lattices $U _ { k }$ are the only indecomposable rank two symmetric dyadic lattices which represent zero. There are no indecomposable symmetric rank 2 dyadic lattices other than $U _ { k }$ and $V _ { k }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C10-U004` | Lemma 10.1.2 | Chapter 10, source L5473-L5485 | Lemma 10.1.2 (Recognising binary dyadic forms). For a Gram matrix $\binom { \alpha } { \beta } \begin{array} { l } { \beta } \\ { \gamma } \end{array}$ of a unimodular dyadic symmetric form $b$ , setting $d = \operatorname { d i s c } ( b )$ , the following possibilities occur: 1. The form is decomposable if and only if either $\alpha$ or $\gamma$ is odd, i.e., a unit. More precisely, (a) if $\alpha$ is odd, $b \simeq \left. \alpha \right. \textcircled { \pm } \left. \alpha \cdot d \right.$ . (b) if ?? is odd, $b \simeq \langle \gamma \rangle \oplus \langle \gamma \cdot d \rangle$ . 2. If ?? and ?? are both even (hence $b$ is even), then $\beta$ is odd, ?? is indecomposable, and (a) if ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { 4 } } } \alpha \gamma$ is even, $b \simeq U$ (b) and if ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { 4 } } } \alpha \gamma$ is odd, $b \simeq V$ . For a unimodular binary dyadic quadratic lattice, only case 2 occurs. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U005` | Lemma 10.1.3 | Chapter 10, source L5496-L5497 | Lemma 10.1.3. A $p$ -adic unimodular quadratic form of rank two represents all $p$ -adic units. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U006` | Corollary 10.1.4 | Chapter 10, source L5504-L5505 | Corollary 10.1.4. Let $( L , q )$ be a unimodular binary quadratic $p$ -adic lattice and ?? a $p$ -adic unit. Then ?? admits a reflection $\sigma _ { x }$ in a vector $x \in L$ with $q ( x ) = u$ . | — |
| `FC11-C10-U007` | Definition / construction | Chapter 10, source L5512 | For a $p$-adic lattice $L$, $(q(L))$ and $(b(L,L))$ denote the ideals generated by its quadratic and bilinear values. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U008` | Lemma 10.2.1 | Chapter 10, source L5520-L5521 | Lemma 10.2.1. Let $( L , q )$ be a non-degenerate $p$ -adic quadratic lattice. Write $b$ for the polar form of $q$ . Let $( q ( L ) )$ , $( b ( L , L ) )$ and $x _ { 0 } \in L$ be as above. Suppose that $2 ( q ( L ) ) = ( b ( L , L ) )$ (which is the case for $p$ odd, but not necessarily for $p = 2$ ). Then $\mathbb { Z } _ { p } \cdot x _ { 0 }$ splits off orthogonally. Consequently, if there is no rank one sublattice of $L$ which splits off, then $p = 2$ and $\mathfrak { Z } ( q ( L ) ) \subsetneq ( b ( L , L ) ) \subset \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C10-U009` | Proposition 10.2.2 | Chapter 10, source L5526-L5529 | Proposition 10.2.2 (Classification (I)). 1. For $p$ odd, any (non-degenerate) symmetric (quadratic) $p$ -adic lattice is isometric to an orthogonal direct sum of rank one $p$ -adic lattices of the form $\langle u p ^ { k } \rangle$ , where $u \in \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times }$ and $k \geq 0$ . 2. Dyadic symmetric, respectively quadratic lattices split into an orthogonal direct sum of rank one lattices of the form $\langle u \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle$ , $u \in \{ \pm 1 , \pm 3 \}$ , where $k \geq 0$ , respectively $k \geq 1$ , together with copies of $U _ { k }$ and of $V _ { k }$ , $k \geq 0$ . | — |
| `FC11-C10-U010` | Example / comparison | Chapter 10, source L5556 | Example 10.2.3. Since $\mathrm { d i s c } ( U ) = - 1$ , $\operatorname { d i s c } ( V ) = 3$ , the lattice $E _ { 8 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ , being even with discriminant 1, is isometric to either $\mathbb { O } ^ { 4 } U$ , $\mathbb { O } ^ { 4 } V$ or to $\mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } U \mathbb { O } \mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } V$ . We shall see (Lemma 11.2.1) that these 3 dyadic lattices are isometric. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U011` | Corollary 10.2.4 | Chapter 10, source L5560-L5561 | Corollary 10.2.4. A $p$ -adic unimodular quadratic form of rank $\geq 2$ represents all $p$ -adic units. For $p \neq 2$ the lattice admits isotropic vectors if its rank is at least 3, for $p = 2$ this is the case if the rank is at least 4. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U012` | Remark 10.2.5 | Chapter 10, source L5564 | Remark 10.2.5. The existence of isotropic vectors is used in the proof of Corollary A.3.7. This result in particular states that the occurrence of one root in an indefinite lattice of rank $\geq 4$ implies that there are infinitely many. This, in turn, is a crucial ingredient in the proof of Theorem 17.2.11 which roughly states that for ”most” lattices with Witt index $\geq 2$ the Weyl group is as big as possible. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U013` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 10, source L5566 | A Jordan splitting of a $p$-adic lattice is an orthogonal decomposition into summands $L(p^k)$ with $L$ unimodular; such a summand is homogeneous of exponent $k$. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U014` | Proposition 10.2.6 | Chapter 10, source L5568-L5575 | Proposition 10.2.6. A non-degenerate symmetric $p$ -adic lattice $( L , b )$ has a Jordan splitting, i.e., $$ L = L _ { 0 } \oplus L _ { 1 } ( p ) \oplus \cdots \oplus L _ { r } ( p ^ { r } ) , $$ where each $L _ { j }$ is unimodular. The rank of $L _ { j }$ as well as its discriminant, $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { j } ) \in$ $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } )$ , is uniquely determined by $( L , b )$ . | `FC11-C10-U013` |
| `FC11-C10-U015` | Remark 10.2.7 | Chapter 10, source L5600 | Remark 10.2.7. For $p = 2$ a Jordan splitting need not be unique. We discuss this extensively in Appendix C. See in particular Lemma C.3.3 where relation (V) as well as (VI) exhibit two distinct Jordan splittings for a non-degenerate rank 3 dyadic lattice. The summands on the left and on the right side of the equivalence are indeed non-isometric since these have different discriminants. For uniqueness for odd $p$ , see Proposition 11.1.3. | `FC11-C10-U013` |
| `FC11-C10-U016` | Corollary 10.2.8 | Chapter 10, source L5604-L5605 | Corollary 10.2.8 (Suggested by M. Sch¨utt). An even integral lattice of odd rank has even discriminant. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U017` | Lemma 10.3.1 | Chapter 10, source L5620-L5623 | Lemma 10.3.1. Let $( G , q )$ be a non-degenerate $p$ -adic torsion quadratic group. Write ?? for the polar form of $q$ . Let $( q ( G ) ) \subset \mathbb { Q } _ { p } / \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ , $( b ( G , G ) ) \subset \mathbb { Q } _ { p } / \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ be the $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ -submodule generated by the values of $q ( x ) , x \in G$ , respectively $b ( x , y ) , x , y \in G$ , and let $x _ { 0 } \in G$ such that $q ( x _ { \mathrm { 0 } } )$ generates $( q ( G ) )$ . Suppose that $2 ( q ( G ) ) = ( b ( G , G ) )$ (which is the case for $p$ odd). Then the cyclic group generated by $x _ { 0 }$ splits off orthogonally. Consequently, if there is no non-degenerate cyclic torsion subgroup of $G$ which splits off, then $p = 2$ and $2 ( q ( G ) ) \subsetneq ( b ( G , G ) ) \subset \mathbb { Q } _ { 2 } / \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C10-U018` | Proposition 10.3.2 | Chapter 10, source L5629-L5661 | Proposition 10.3.2 (Compatibility of splittings). 1. Let $L$ be a non-degenerate symmetric or quadratic $p$ -adic lattice with Jordan splitting $$ L = L _ { 0 } \oplus L _ { 1 } ( p ) \oplus \cdots \oplus L _ { n } ( p ^ { n } ) . $$ Then its discriminant form admits a Jordan splitting $$ \begin{array} { r } { \deg _ { L } = \deg _ { L _ { 1 } ( p ) } \oplus \deg _ { L _ { 2 } ( p ^ { 2 } ) } \oplus \cdot \cdot \cdot \oplus \deg _ { L _ { n } ( p ^ { n } ) } . } \end{array} $$ 2. Given a basis for the homogeneous components of the discriminant form of $L$ , there is a corresponding basis for the homogeneous components of $L$ . More precisely, $p$ odd. For all $j \geq 1$ , let $\{ g _ { 1 } ^ { ( j ) } , \ldots , g _ { s } ^ { ( j ) } \}$ be an orthogonal basis for $\mathsf { d g } _ { L _ { j } ( p ^ { j } ) }$ with sis $b _ { j } ^ { \# } ( g _ { k } ^ { ( j ) } , g _ { k } ^ { ( j ) } ) = u _ { k , j } p ^ { - j }$ $\{ e _ { 1 } ^ { ( j ) } , \ldots , e _ { s } ^ { ( j ) } \}$ for the summand , a $p$ -adic unit. Then there is an orthogonal $L _ { j } ( p ^ { j } )$ of $L$ with $b _ { j } ( e _ { k } ^ { ( j ) } , e _ { k } ^ { ( j ) } ) = u _ { k , j } p ^ { j }$ . $A$ similar result is true for quadratic $p$ -adic lattices. $p = 2$ . For all $j \geq 1$ , let $$ \begin{array} { r } { \small { \mathrm { d } } \mathrm { g } _ { L _ { j } ( 2 ^ { j } ) } = \mathbb { G } _ { k = 1 } ^ { a _ { j } } \langle u _ { j , k } \cdot 2 ^ { - j } \rangle \ \mathbb { G } ^ { b _ { j } } v _ { j } \ \mathbb { G } ^ { c _ { j } } u _ { j } , \quad u _ { j , k } \in \mathrm { D } ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { j + 1 } \mathbb { Z } ) , } \end{array} $$ be an orthogonal direct sum splitting. Then there exists a compatible orthogonal direct sum splitting $$ L _ { j } ( 2 ^ { j } ) = \mathbb { \oplus } _ { k = 1 } ^ { a _ { j } } \langle \widetilde { u } _ { j , k } \cdot 2 ^ { j } \rangle \ \mathbb { \oplus } ^ { b _ { j } } V _ { j } \ \mathbb { \oplus } ^ { c _ { j } } U _ { j } , \quad \widetilde { u } _ { j , k } \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } ) . $$ Here $\widetilde { u } _ { j , k } = u _ { j , k } \in \{ \pm 1 , \pm 3 \}$ if $j \geq 2$ , while for $j = 1$ the representative $\widetilde { u } _ { 1 , k } \in$ $\{ \pm 1 , \pm 3 \}$ coincides up to multiplication by $^ { - 3 }$ mod 8 with the representative $u _ { 1 , k } \in \{ 1 , 3 \}$ . In other words, such non-isometric rank one lattices have the same discriminant form. For $j \geq 2$ , $a _ { j } = 0$ if and only if the ideal in $\mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ generated by $b _ { q } ( L _ { j } , L _ { j } )$ differs from the ideal generated by $2 \cdot q _ { j } ( L _ { j } )$ – or – equivalently, if and only the submodule of $\mathbb { Q } _ { 2 } / \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ generated by $b _ { q } ( G _ { j } , G _ { j } )$ , $G _ { j } = \mathsf { d g } _ { L _ { j } ( 2 ^ { j } ) }$ , differs from the submodule generated by $2 \cdot q _ { j } ( G _ { j } )$ . In particular, the vanishing or not of $a _ { j }$ is an intrinsic property of the lattice. | `FC11-C10-U013` |
| `FC11-C10-U019` | Remark 10.3.3 | Chapter 10, source L5678 | Remark 10.3.3. In Chapter 11 we return to the nature of the discriminant form map on lattices whose rank equals the length of its discriminant quadratic form. See Proposition 11.1.3 and Proposition 11.2.4. | — |
| `FC11-C10-U020` | Lemma 10.4.1 | Chapter 10, source L5686-L5691 | Lemma 10.4.1. Let $f , g$ be two non-degenerate quadratic forms over $\mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ . Then $$ \varepsilon _ { p } ( f \oplus g ) = \varepsilon _ { p } ( f ) \cdot \varepsilon _ { p } ( g ) \cdot ( \operatorname { d i s c } ( f ) , \operatorname { d i s c } ( g ) ) _ { p } . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C10-U021` | Lemma 10.4.2 | Chapter 10, source L5706-L5725 | Lemma 10.4.2. 1. Let $p$ be an odd prime and suppose that $f = p ^ { k } \cdot g$ with $g$ unimodular of rank $r$ . Then we have $$ \varepsilon _ { p } ( f ) = ( - 1 ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } r ( r - 1 ) k \cdot \varepsilon ( p ) } \left( { \frac { \operatorname { d i s c } ( g ) } { p } } ^ { k ( r - 1 ) } \right) , \quad \varepsilon ( p ) = \left\{ { \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { { \mathrm { ~ } } i f \ p \equiv 1 { \bmod { 4 } } } \\ { 1 } & { { \mathrm { ~ } } i f \ p \equiv 3 { \bmod { 4 } } . } \end{array} } \right. $$ 2. Let $f$ be a unimodular dyadic quadratic lattice of rank $r _ { f }$ and discriminant $d _ { f }$ , and set $$ \mu ( f ) = - r _ { f } - d _ { f } + 1 , \quad \delta _ { f } = \frac 1 2 ( d _ { f } - 1 ) . $$ Then $\mu ( f )$ is divisible by 4 and $$ \varepsilon _ { 2 } ( f ) = ( - 1 ) ^ { { \frac { 1 } { 4 } } \mu ( f ) } = ( - 1 ) ^ { { \frac { 1 } { 4 } } ( - r _ { f } - d _ { f } + 1 ) } . $$ If $g$ is also a unimodular form, then $\mu ( f \circledast g ) = \mu ( f ) + \mu ( g ) - 4 \delta _ { f } \cdot \delta _ { g }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C11-U001` | Definition 11.1.1 | Chapter 11, source L5754-L5766 | Definition 11.1.1. Let $p$ be an odd prime. • A homogeneous symmetric $p$ -adic lattice of rank $r \geq 1$ and exponent $k$ is in homogeneous normal form if it is of the form $L _ { r , u } ( p ^ { k } )$ , where $$ L _ { r , u } = \langle u \rangle \oplus \oplus ^ { r - 1 } \langle 1 \rangle , \quad u \in \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } , $$ a unimodular lattice. • A symmetric $p$ -adic lattice is in normal form if it is an orthogonal direct sum of homogeneous normal forms. • A su $p$ -primary symmetric torsion form is in normal form if it is an orthogonal of forms $b _ { r , u } ^ { \# } ( p ^ { - k } )$ , the symmetric discriminant form of $L _ { r , u } ( p ^ { k } )$ , $k \geq 1$ | — |
| `FC11-C11-U002` | Lemma 11.1.2 | Chapter 11, source L5770-L5771 | Lemma 11.1.2. 1. For fixed $r$ there are exactly two isometry classes for $L _ { r , u }$ distinguished by $u$ being a square or a non-square modulo $p$ . 2. Every non-degenerate $p$ -adic lattice admits a normal form of the above shape. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U003` | Definition / construction | Chapter 11, source L5786-L5790 | For a $p$-primary torsion form in the normal form of Proposition 9.4.1, associate the $p$-adic lattice $L_{b^\#}=\bigoplus_i L_{r_i,u_i}(p^{k_i})$. | `FC11-C09-U027` |
| `FC11-C11-U004` | Proposition 11.1.3 | Chapter 11, source L5794-L5804 | Proposition 11.1.3. Let $p$ be an odd prime and let $( G , b ^ { \# } )$ be a non-degenerate $p$ -primary torsion symmetric group of length $\ell ( G )$ with $b ^ { \# }$ in normal form (11.1). Then: 1. The lattice $L _ { b ^ { \# } }$ given by (11.2) is up to isometry the unique non-degenerate $p$ -adic lattice of rank $\ell ( G )$ and discriminant form $b ^ { \# }$ . Its discriminant equals $\begin{array} { r } { \| G \| \cdot \prod _ { k = 1 } ^ { s } u _ { k } = \| G \| \cdot \delta ( b ^ { \# } ) } \end{array}$ , where $\delta ( b ^ { \# } )$ is the reduced discriminant of $b ^ { \# }$ . 2. The discriminant form map $$ \left\{ \begin{array} { c c } { { i s o m . ~ c l a s s e s ~ o f ~ p / - a d i c } } \\ { { s y m m e t r i c ~ l a t t i c e s ~ o f ~ r a n k ~ r } } \end{array} \right\} \longrightarrow \left\{ \begin{array} { c c } { { i s o m . ~ c l a s s e s ~ o f ~ p / - p r i m a r y ~ t o r s i o n } } \\ { { s y m m e t r i c ~ f o r m s ~ o f ~ l e n g t h ~ r } } \end{array} \right\} $$ is injective (and hence bijective). | — |
| `FC11-C11-U005` | Definition / construction | Chapter 11, source L5806 | For a non-degenerate symmetric $p$-primary torsion form $b^\#$, $L_{b^\#}$ denotes the uniquely determined rank-$\ell(G)$ $p$-adic lattice in the associated normal-form isometry class; this notation is used even when $b^\#$ itself is not displayed in normal form. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U006` | Proposition 11.1.4 | Chapter 11, source L5808-L5817 | Proposition 11.1.4 (Normal forms for non-degenerate $p$ -adic lattices, $p$ odd). Let $p$ be an odd prime, $( G , b ^ { \# } )$ a non-degenerate $p$ -primary symmetric torsion form, $r$ an integer $\geq \ell ( G )$ and $t = r - \ell ( G )$ . 1. Let ?? be a non-square modulo $p$ . A non-degenerate $p$ -adic lattice $L$ of rank $r$ and with discriminant form $( G , b ^ { \# } )$ is isometric to one of the following two lattices: $$ \begin{array}{c} \begin{array} { r } { \left\{ L \simeq L _ { b ^ { \# } } \oplus \mathbb { O } ^ { t } \langle 1 \rangle \qquad i f \operatorname { d i s c } ( L ) = \operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { b ^ { \# } } ) = \| G \| \cdot \delta ( b ^ { \# } ) \qquad \right.} \\ { L \simeq L _ { b ^ { \# } } \oplus \langle \epsilon \rangle \oplus ^ { t - 1 } \langle 1 \rangle \quad i f \operatorname { d i s c } ( L ) = \epsilon \cdot \operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { b ^ { \# } } ) = \| G \| \cdot \epsilon \cdot \delta ( b ^ { \# } ) . } \end{array} \end{array} $$ 2. Let $\widetilde { L }$ be a non-degenerate integral lattice of rank $r$ . Then the discriminant form of $\widetilde { L }$ determines the localization $\widetilde { L } _ { p }$ up to isometry. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U007` | Example / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5822 | We take $p = 7$ . The squares modulo 7 are $1 , 2 , 4$ and the nonsquares are $3 , 5 , 6$ . Consider the torsion form $b ^ { \# } = \langle 3 \cdot 7 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus \langle 7 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus \langle 5 \cdot 7 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ . This is already in normal form and it is the discriminant form of $L _ { b \# } = \langle 3 \cdot 7 \rangle \textcircled { \scriptsize { \oplus } } \langle 7 \rangle \textcircled { \scriptsize { \oplus } } \langle 5 \cdot 7 ^ { 2 } \rangle$ . Its discriminant equals $7 ^ { 4 }$ (up to squares of units) and so, for example, the normal form of a 7-adic lattice $L$ of rank 8 with discriminant $5 \cdot 7 ^ { 4 }$ and discriminant symmetric form $b ^ { \# }$ is $L = \left. 3 \cdot 7 \right. \mathbb { \odot } \left. 7 \right. \mathbb { \odot } \left. 5 \cdot 7 ^ { 2 } \right. \mathbb { \odot } \left. 5 \right. \mathbb { \odot } \mathbb { \oplus } ^ { 4 } \langle 1 \rangle$ . | — |
| `FC11-C11-U008` | Example / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5824-L5826 | Let us consider the normal forms of the $p$ -adic localizations of the A-D-E root lattices, where $p$ is an odd prime. First of all we consider the unimodular root lattice $E _ { 8 }$ . Since $\mathrm { d i s c } ( E _ { 8 } ) = 1$ , all of its localizations have discriminant 1. Hence, the normal form of $E _ { 8 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ is the diagonal $p$ -adic lattice $\mathbb { O } ^ { 8 } \langle 1 \rangle$ . The normal forms of the other root lattices depend on the odd prime $p$ . To determine these, one may use Table 4.1.1. As an example, consider $A _ { 5 }$ , which has discriminant 6 and discriminant group isomorphic to $\mathbb { Z } / 6 \mathbb { Z } = \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z } \times \mathbb { Z } / 3 \mathbb { Z }$ . If $p \neq 2 , 3$ the form $A _ { 5 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ is unimodular and its discriminant is a unit in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ which may or may not be a square modulo $p$ . For instance, 6 is a non-square modulo 7 and so $A _ { 5 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { 7 } \cong \langle 6 \rangle \oplus \oplus ^ { 4 } \langle 1 \rangle$ , while $A _ { 5 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { 5 } \cong \oplus ^ { 5 } \langle 1 \rangle$ since 6 is a square modulo 5. For $p = 3$ we need the 3-adic discriminant symmetric form of $A _ { 5 }$ which is the restriction of the discriminant form of $A _ { 5 }$ to the 3-primary part by (1.20). The discriminant form of $A _ { 5 }$ is $\langle 5 / 6 \rangle$ . The 3-primary part of $\mathbb { Z } / 6 \mathbb { Z }$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb { Z } / 3 \mathbb { Z }$ and generated by the class of 2 in $\mathbb { Z } / 6 \mathbb { Z }$ , so that the 3-adic discriminant symmetric form $b ^ { \# }$ is determined by its value on the pair $( 2 , 2 )$ , which is $2 ^ { 2 } \cdot 5 / 6 \equiv 1 / 3$ modulo $\mathbb { Z }$ . So $b ^ { \# } = \langle 1 / 3 \rangle$ and $L _ { b \# } = \langle 3 \rangle$ . Hence $A _ { 5 } \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { 3 }$ has normal form $\langle 3 \rangle \oplus \langle 2 \rangle \oplus \oplus ^ { 3 } \langle 1 \rangle$ since 2 is a non-square in $\mathbb { Z } _ { 3 }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C11-U009` | Lemma 11.2.1 | Chapter 11, source L5838-L5844 | Lemma 11.2.1 (Relations between dyadic lattices and 2-primary torsion forms). Let $u , u ^ { \prime } , u ^ { \prime \prime }$ be units in $\mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ . Then $v = u u ^ { \prime } + u u ^ { \prime \prime } + u ^ { \prime } u ^ { \prime \prime }$ is $a$ 2-adic unit with $v \equiv 3 { \mathrm { ~ m o d ~ } } 8$ or $v \equiv - 1$ mod 8 and the following relations hold between symmetric 2-adic lattices: $$ \begin{array} { r } { U _ { k } \oplus U _ { k } \simeq V _ { k } \oplus V _ { k } , \quad k \geq 0 , \qquad } \\ { ( \langle u \rangle \oplus \langle u ^ { \prime } \rangle \oplus \langle u ^ { \prime \prime } \rangle ) ( 2 ^ { k } ) \simeq \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { V _ { k } \oplus U _ { k } \oplus \langle ( u + u ^ { \prime } + u ^ { \prime \prime } ) \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle } \\ { \quad i n \ c a s e \ u u ^ { \prime } + u u ^ { \prime \prime } + u ^ { \prime } u ^ { \prime \prime } \equiv 3 \bmod 8 } \\ { U _ { k } \oplus \langle ( u + u ^ { \prime } + u ^ { \prime \prime } ) \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle } \\ { \quad i n \ c a s e \ u u ^ { \prime } + u u ^ { \prime \prime } + u ^ { \prime } u ^ { \prime \prime } \equiv - 1 \bmod 8 } \\ { \quad ( \langle u \rangle \oplus \langle u ^ { \prime } \rangle ) ( 2 ^ { k } ) \simeq ( \langle - 3 u \rangle \oplus \langle - 3 u ^ { \prime } \rangle ) ( 2 ^ { k } ) , \quad k \geq 0 } \end{array} \right. \quad k \geq 0 } \end{array} $$ There are similar relations (I) – (III) between the corresponding discriminant forms. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U010` | Definition 11.2.2 | Chapter 11, source L5848-L5858 | Definition 11.2.2. • A homogeneous symmetric dyadic lattice of rank $r$ and exponent $k$ is in normal form if it is of the form $L ( 2 ^ { k } )$ with $$ L = \mathbb { O } _ { i \leq a } \langle u ^ { ( i ) } \rangle \oplus \mathbb { O } ^ { b } V \mathbb { O } ^ { c } U , \quad a \leq 2 , b \leq 1 , c = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( r - a ) - b \geq 0 , u ^ { ( i ) } \in \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } ^ { \times } . $$ For $k = 0$ and $L$ an even dyadic lattice, the cyclic forms are not present, that is, $a = 0$ in this case. • A symmetric dyadic lattice is in normal form if it is the orthogonal direct sum of homogeneous normal forms. • A 2-primary symmetric (quadratic) torsion form is in normal form if it is the discriminant form of a symmetric (quadratic) dyadic lattice in normal form. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U011` | Proposition 11.2.3 | Chapter 11, source L5862-L5864 | Proposition 11.2.3. • Let $( L , b )$ be a non-degenerate symmetric (quadratic) dyadic lattice. Then $L$ is isometric to a normal form and so is its symmetric (quadratic) discriminant form. • Every 2-primary symmetric (quadratic) torsion form is isometric to a normal form. Moreover, there is a one-to-one correspondence between normal forms for symmetric and quadratic torsion forms on the same torsion group (with the same notation, as illustrated in the table below). | — |
| `FC11-C11-U012` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5866-L5873 | For dyadic discriminant forms, the symmetric and quadratic classifications do not coincide: distinct quadratic torsion forms can have the same polar form, and a fixed symmetric discriminant form of length at most $2$ can come from several non-isometric rank-minimal dyadic lattices. In particular $U_1$ and $V_1$ have the same symmetric but different quadratic discriminant forms; for exponent $k\ge2$ this discrepancy disappears. | — |
| `FC11-C11-U013` | Proposition 11.2.4 | Chapter 11, source L5882-L5895 | Proposition 11.2.4. Let $( G , q ^ { \# } )$ be a quadratic 2-primary torsion group of length $r$ decomposed into homogeneous normal form. Then the following two types of forms occur, each with different behaviour with respect to normal forms: Type 1. No cyclic orthogonal summands of order 2 split off from $q ^ { \# }$ . Then there is a unique isometry class of a dyadic lattice $L$ of rank $r$ with discriminant form $q ^ { \# }$ represented by the normal form $L _ { q ^ { \# } }$ with discriminant $\delta ( q ^ { \# } ) \cdot \| G \|$ . Type 2. Otherwise there are precisely two isometry classes ?? (??) , $i = 1 , 2$ , of rank ?? lattices with discriminant form $q ^ { \# }$ in normal form characterized by their discriminants, $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L _ { q ^ { \# } } ^ { ( 1 ) } ) = \delta ( q ^ { \# } ) \cdot \| G \|$ , respectively $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L _ { q ^ { \# } } ^ { ( 2 ) } ) = - 3 \cdot \delta ( q ^ { \# } ) \cdot \| G \|$ . Moreover the discriminant form map $$ \left\{ \begin{array} { c c } { { i s o m . \ c l a s s e s \ o f \ d y a d i c } } \\ { { q u a d r a t i c \ l a t t i c e s \ o f \ r a n k \ r } } \end{array} \right\} \longrightarrow \left\{ \begin{array} { c c } { { i s o m . \ c l a s s e s \ o f \ 2 - p r i m a r y \ t o r s i o n } } \\ { { q u a d r a t i c \ f o r m s \ o f \ l e n g t h \ r } } \end{array} \right\} $$ is surjective, and injective on the preimage of type 1 forms, and two-to-one onto on the preimage of type 2 forms. | — |

| `FC11-C11-U015` | Example / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5904-L5911 | On $\mathbb { Q } ^ { 6 } \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ we have the following possibilities for non-degenerate quadratic torsion forms: • $\mathbb { O } ^ { 3 } u _ { 1 }$ , the discriminant form of $\mathbb { O } ^ { 3 } U _ { 1 }$ ; • $\mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } u _ { 1 } \oplus v _ { 1 }$ , the discriminant form of $\mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } U _ { 1 } \oplus V _ { 1 }$ ; • $\left. u \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \right. \mathbb { O } \left. u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \right. \mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } u _ { 1 }$ , the discriminant form of $\left. u \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } \left. u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } ^ { 2 } U _ { 1 }$ or of $\left. - 3 u \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } \left. u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } ^ { 2 } U _ { 1 }$ ; • $\langle u \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \mathbb { O } u _ { 1 } \mathbb { O } v _ { 1 }$ , the discriminant form of $\left. u \cdot 2 \right. \mathbb { \oplus } \left. u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 \right. \mathbb { \oplus } U _ { 1 } \mathbb { \oplus } V _ { 1 }$ or of $\left. - 3 u \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } \left. u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 \right. \textcircled { 1 } U _ { 1 } \textcircled { 1 } V _ { 1 }$ . | `FC11-C11-U018` | Proposition 11.2.6 | Chapter 11, source L5919-L5937 | Proposition 11.2.6 (Normal forms for quadratic dyadic lattices). Let $( G , q ^ { \# } )$ . be a 2-primary quadratic torsion group, ?? an integer $\geq \ell ( G )$ and $t = r - \ell ( G )$ . 1. A non-degenerate even 2-adic lattice $L$ of rank ?? and with discriminant quadratic form $( G , q ^ { \# } )$ is isometric to one of the following two types of lattices: (a) If $q ^ { \# }$ has no cyclic orthogonal summands of order 2 in its normal form decomposition, then $$ L \simeq \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { { \oplus ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t } U \oplus L _ { q \# } } } & { { \mathrm { ~ } i f \mathrm { ~ d i s c } ( L ) = \mathrm { d i s c } ( L _ { q \# } ) \cdot ( - 1 ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t } } } \\ { { \oplus ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t - 1 } U \oplus V \oplus L _ { q \# } } } & { { \mathrm { ~ } i f \mathrm { ~ d i s c } ( L ) = \mathrm { d i s c } ( L _ { q \# } ) \cdot 3 \cdot ( - 1 ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t - 1 } . } } \end{array} \right. $$ (b) Otherwise, that is, if $q ^ { \# } \simeq \langle u \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus q ^ { \prime }$ for some unit $u \in \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ , then $$ { \cal L } \simeq \oplus ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t } U \oplus { \cal L } _ { q ^ { \# } } ^ { ( i ) } , \quad i = 1 , 2 . $$ Which of the two cases occurs can be determined as follows: If $\delta ( q ^ { \# } ) \cdot \| G \| \equiv$ $( - 1 ) ^ { \frac { 1 } { 2 } t } \operatorname { d i s c } ( L )$ mod 4, then $i = 1$ and otherwise $i = 2$ . 2. | `FC11-C11-U021` | Definition | Chapter 11, source L5961 | Definition. The genus invariant of a non-degenerate integral quadratic lattice $( L , q )$ is the triple $\mathsf { g } ( L ) : = ( r _ { + } , r _ { - } , [ \boldsymbol { q } _ { L } ^ { \# } ] )$ , where $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ is the signature of $L$ and $[ q _ { L } ^ { \# } ]$ is the isometry class of the discriminant quadratic form $q _ { L } ^ { \# }$ of $L$ . | — |
| `FC11-C11-U022` | Theorem 11.3.1 | Chapter 11, source L5963-L5964 | Theorem 11.3.1 (Nikulin [171, Cor. 1.9.4]). The genus of a non-degenerate quadratic lattice $( L , q )$ is completely determined by its genus invariant $\xi ( L )$ . | `FC11-C11-U021` |
| `FC11-C11-U023` | Example / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5967 | The lattices $\Gamma _ { 1 6 }$ and $\Gamma _ { 8 } \oplus \Gamma _ { 8 } ( \simeq E _ { 8 } \oplus E _ { 8 } )$ . We calculate the Hasse invariants using Lemma 10.4.2. Being unimodular, both have $\varepsilon _ { p } = 1$ for $p$ odd. Since $\mathrm { d i s c ( { \Gamma _ { 8 } } ) = \mathrm { d i s c } ( { \Gamma _ { 1 6 } } ) = 1 }$ we find $\mu ( \Gamma _ { 8 } \oplus \Gamma _ { 8 } ) = \mu ( \Gamma _ { 1 6 } ) = - 1 6$ and so $\varepsilon _ { 2 } ( f ) = 1$ in both cases. It follows that both lattices have the same Hasse invariants. This shows that there is an isometry between the localized lattices, but the isometry is a priori only over $\mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ . The case $\mathbb { Q } _ { \infty }$ is evident. The next example shows that they are isometric over $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ , i.e. they belong to the same genus. | `FC11-C10-U021` |
| `FC11-C11-U024` | Example / comparison | Chapter 11, source L5969 | Unimodular quadratic lattices. These have 0 discriminant form and hence the genus is completely determined by the signature. In particular all positive definite even unimodular lattices of the same rank are in one genus. | — |

| `FC11-C12-U004` | Definition 12.2.1 | Chapter 12, source L6017 | Definition 12.2.1. Two non-degenerate symmetric lattices $L _ { 1 }$ and $L _ { \mathrm { 2 } }$ are stably equivalent if there are unimodular lattices $U _ { 1 }$ and $U _ { 2 }$ such that $L _ { 1 } \odot U _ { 1 }$ and $L _ { 2 } \odot U _ { 2 }$ are isometric. If $L _ { 1 }$ and $L _ { \mathrm { 2 } }$ are even and $U _ { 1 }$ and $U _ { 2 }$ can be taken even as well, then we say that $L _ { 1 }$ and $L _ { \mathrm { 2 } }$ are evenly stably equivalent. | — |
| `FC11-C12-U005` | Theorem 12.2.2 | Chapter 12, source L6021-L6022 | Theorem 12.2.2. Two non-degenerate symmetric integral lattices with isometric discriminant symmetric forms are stably equivalent. | `FC11-C12-U006` | Definition 12.2.3 | Chapter 12, source L6140 | Definition 12.2.3. The index modulo 8 of a non-degenerate quadratic torsion group $( G , q )$ , denoted $\tau _ { 8 } ( q )$ , is the modulo 8 integer $\tau ( L )$ mod 8 of any nondegenerate quadratic lattice $L$ whose discriminant form is $q$ . | — |
| `FC11-C12-U007` | Corollary 12.2.4 | Chapter 12, source L6144-L6152 | Corollary 12.2.4. Let $( G , q )$ be a non-degenerate quadratic torsion group and $L$ a non-degenerate quadratic lattice with $q _ { L } ^ { \# } = q$ . Then $$ \tau _ { 8 } ( q ) \equiv \tau ( L ) \bmod 8 $$ is independent of the choice of $L$ . The index modulo 8 is additive in $\mathbb { Z } / 8 \mathbb { Z }$ , that is $\tau _ { 8 } ( q _ { 1 } \oplus q _ { 2 } ) = \tau _ { 8 } ( q _ { 1 } ) + \tau _ { 8 } ( q _ { 2 } )$ | `FC11-C12-U006` |
