| `FC11-C12-U018` | Theorem ([204, IV.3.3]) | Chapter 12, source L6287-L6289 | Theorem (existence over $\mathbf Q$). Given $d\in D(\mathbf Q)$, non-negative $r_+,r_-$ with $r_++r_-\ge3$, and $\varepsilon_v\in\{\pm1\}$ for every place $v$, there is a rational quadratic form with discriminant $d$, signature $(r_+,r_-)$ and Hasse invariants $\varepsilon_v$ iff almost all $\varepsilon_v=1$, $\prod_v\varepsilon_v=1$, $d_\infty=(-1)^{r_-}$, and $\varepsilon_\infty=(-1)^s$ with $s=\frac12r_-(r_--1)$. | — |
| `FC11-C12-U019` | Theorem 12.4.1 | Chapter 12, source L6295-L6301 | Theorem 12.4.1. Let $L$ be a $\mathbb { Q }$ -vector space of dimension $\geq 2$ and suppose that for every $v \in \mathcal { P }$ a non-degenerate quadratic form $q _ { v }$ on $L _ { v }$ is given. Let ?? be a non-zero rational number. Suppose that 1. $\mathrm { d i s c } ( q _ { v } ) = d$ for all $v \in { \mathcal { P } }$ (where we consider $d \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { v } )$ under the natural homomorphism $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } ) \to \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { v } )$ induced by $\mathbb { Q } \hookrightarrow \mathbb { Q } _ { v }$ ); 2. $\varepsilon _ { v } ( q _ { v } ) = 1$ for almost all $v \in { \mathcal { P } }$ and the product formula $\begin{array} { r } { \prod _ { v \in \mathcal { P } } \varepsilon _ { v } ( q _ { v } ) = 1 } \end{array}$ holds. Then there exists a $\mathbb { Q }$ -valued (non-degenerate) quadratic form $q$ on $L$ such that $\operatorname { d i s c } ( q ) = d$ (up to squares) and the localization of $q$ at $v$ is isometric to $q _ { v }$ for all $v \in \mathcal { S } ^ { \mathsf { \Delta } }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C12-U020` | Theorem 12.4.2 | Chapter 12, source L6305 | Theorem 12.4.2 (Hasse principle for lattices). Let $n \geq 1$ , $d \ne 0$ be integers. Suppose that for every prime $p$ a non-degenerate $p$ -adic integral quadratic form $q _ { p }$ of rank ?? and discriminant $d$ is given, as well as a real valued form $q _ { \infty }$ of rank $n$ and discriminant $d$ . If there exists a rational quadratic form $g$ such that $g _ { v } \simeq q _ { v } \otimes \mathbb { U } _ { v }$ for all $v \in \mathcal { P }$ , then there exists an integral quadratic form $q$ whose localization at $v$ is equivalent (over $\mathbb { Z } _ { v }$ ) to $q _ { v }$ for all places $v \in \mathcal { P }$ (with the convention that $\mathbb { Z } _ { \infty } = \mathbb { R }$ ). | — |
| `FC11-C12-U021` | Corollary 12.4.3 | Chapter 12, source L6309-L6318 | Corollary 12.4.3. Let $L$ be a free $\mathbb { Z }$ -module of finite rank $r \geq 2$ . Suppose that a pair $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ of non-negative integers is given with $r = r _ { + } + r _ { - }$ , and for every prime $p$ a non-degenerate $p$ -adic integral quadratic form $q _ { p }$ on $L _ { p }$ . There exists a non-degenerate integral quadratic form $q$ on $L$ such that $q \otimes \mathbb { Z } _ { p } \simeq$ $q _ { p }$ for all primes $p$ and with signature $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ (corresponding to a real form $q _ { \infty }$ with this signature) if and only if the following conditions hold: 1. $L _ { p }$ is unimodular for almost all primes $p$ ; 2. Setting2 $d = ( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } } \prod _ { p }$ prime $\| \mathrm { d g } _ { L _ { p } }$ \|, then up to a $p$ -adic unit $\mathrm { d i s c } ( q _ { p } ) = d$ for all primes $p$ ; 3. The product formula $\begin{array} { r } { \prod _ { v \in \mathcal { P } } \varepsilon _ { v } ( q _ { v } ) = 1 } \end{array}$ holds. | — |
| `FC11-C12-U022` | Theorem 12.4.4 | Chapter 12, source L6333-L6344 | Theorem 12.4.4 (Existence of even lattices, [171, Thm. 1.10.1]). Let $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ be $a$ pair of non-negative integers and let $( G , q ^ { \# } )$ be a non-degenerate quadratic torsion form. There exists a non-degenerate quadratic lattice $L$ with $\mathsf { g } ( L ) = ( r _ { + } , r _ { - } , [ q ^ { \# } ] )$ (and hence of rank $r = r _ { + } + r .$ −) if and only if all of the following conditions are fulfilled. 1. $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \equiv \tau _ { 8 } ( q ^ { \# } )$ mod 8; 2. $r \geq \ell ( G )$ ; 3. For all primes $p \neq 2$ for which $G _ { p }$ has length $r$ and for $p = 2$ in case $G _ { 2 }$ has length $r$ but does not split off an order two cyclic summand, one has3 $$ \operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { q _ { p } ^ { \# } } ) = ( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } } \| G \| \ i n \ \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ) . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C12-U023` | Remark 12.4.5 | Chapter 12, source L6413-L6429 | Remark 12.4.5. 1. Existence of a genus does not imply uniqueness of the lattice itself. We have seen that for instance non-isometric definite lattices exist in the same genus (see e.g. Table 1.12.1). . Let us illustrate what limitations condition 3 puts on a genus. • First, assume $p$ is an odd prime. For example, assume that $p \equiv - 1$ mod 8. Then $^ { - 1 }$ is a non-square in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ (see e.g. [204, Ch. I, Theorem 5]). Suppose that $G$ is $p$ -primary of length $r$ and homogeneous of odd degree $k$ carrying the torsion form $q ^ { \# }$ of the lattice $L _ { q \# } = L ( p ^ { k } )$ , $L = \langle - 1 \rangle \oplus \oplus ^ { r - 1 } \langle 1 \rangle$ . From the table of Proposition 12.3.2, we see that $\tau _ { 8 } = - 2 + 2 ( r - 1 ) = 2 r - 4$ mod 8. But $2 r _ { - } ( L ) = r - \tau ( L ) \equiv - r + 4$ mod 8 and so $r$ has to be even and the parity of $r _ { - } ( L )$ is the parity of ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } r$ . So, if ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } r$ is even, $( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } ( L ) } = 1$ . This shows that condition 3 does not hold unless ${ \frac { 1 } { 2 } } r$ is odd. So one cannot weaken this condition to $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { q \# } ) = \pm \| G \|$ . • For the prime 2 this is different. It turns out that if $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { q ^ { \# } } ) = \pm \| G \|$ and $q ^ { \# }$ does not have cyclic order 2 factors, the sign is automatically equal to $( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } }$ . For this reason V. Nikulin in [171, Thm. 1.10.1] stated that in this situation the condition $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { q ^ { \# } } ) = \pm \| G \|$ is necessary and sufficient. To indicate why this is the case, it is helpful to distinguish two types of normal forms: Let us say that we have type $I$ if the discriminant equals $\pm \| G \|$ and type $I I$ in case the discriminant is $\pm 3 \| G \|$ . For type $I$ one calculates as before that $\begin{array} { r } { r _ { - } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( r - \tau ) } \end{array}$ mod 4, from which the parity of $r _ { - }$ follows. Comparing with the sign of $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L _ { q ^ { \# } } )$ then shows that this sign equals $( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } }$ . For type $I I$ one gets the opposite sign while $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L _ { q \# } ) = \pm 3 \cdot \| G \|$ . Since the latter lattice cannot be isometric to the 2-adic localization of an integral lattice of the same rank, this shows that in the homogeneous situation the signs match. It also shows that in general an even number of type $I I$ homogeneous summands must be present. Since the parities of $r _ { - }$ for the various homogeneous summands add, while the discriminants multiply (an even number giving $\pm 1$ ), one easily checks that the signs match in general. For example, consider first a homogeneous lattice in normal form, $L _ { q ^ { \# } } =$ $L ( 2 ^ { k } )$ , $k$ odd, with $L = \left. 3 \right. \mathbb { \oplus \oplus ^ { c - 1 } } U \oplus V$ , a lattice of rank $2 c + 1$ . From the table of Proposition 12.3.3 we see that $\tau _ { 8 } ( q ^ { \# } ) = 3$ . Since $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L ) = \pm 1$ we have a type $I$ lattice and so $r _ { - } \equiv c + 1$ mod 2. In fact, $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L ) = ( - 1 ) ^ { c + 1 }$ , and so the signs match. To give an example of an inhomogeneous normal form, consider $M _ { k } \oplus M _ { k + 2 }$ with $k$ odd and $M = \left. 1 \right. \mathbb { \oplus } \mathbb { \oplus } ^ { c - 1 } U \oplus V$ so that $M _ { k }$ and $M _ { k + 2 }$ are both of type $I I$ . One has $r ( M ) = 2 c + 1$ , and from the table of Proposition 12.3.3, one sees that $\tau _ { 8 } ( q ^ { \# } ( M _ { k } ) ) = \tau _ { 8 } ( q ^ { \# } ( M _ { k + 2 } ) ) = - 3$ . One calculates $r _ { - } ( M _ { k } ) = r _ { - } ( M _ { k + 1 } ) \equiv$ $c - 1$ mod 2. Then $\operatorname { d i s c } ( L ) = 3 \cdot 3 \| G \| = \| G \|$ since $\mathrm { d i s c } ( M ) = 3 ( - 1 ) ^ { c }$ , while $r _ { - } \equiv 2 ( c - 1 ) \equiv 0$ mod 2. So, again, the signs match. 3. The theorem implies that unimodular even lattices of index $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ exist if and only if $r _ { + } - r _ { - }$ is divisible by 8. We saw this already in Chapters 1 and 2: That even unimodular lattices have index divisible by 8 is part of the statement of Theorem 2.4.2, existence in the indefinite case is Corollary 2.4.3, while the definite case follows from the existence of $E _ { 8 }$ . 4. Observe that the existence proof is not constructive at all. Consider for instance the genus given by signature $( 8 , 0 )$ and zero torsion form. The proof starts out with $L ^ { \prime } = 0$ and enlarges it to $L ^ { \prime } = \mathbb { \Phi } ^ { \mathrm { s } } U$ . Then $L _ { p } ^ { \prime }$ is replaced by a trivial rank 8 lattice for all odd $p$ as well as for the place $\infty$ , while $L _ { 2 } ^ { \prime }$ is replaced by $\mathbb { O } ^ { 4 } U$ . Then existence is deduced from the modified Hasse principle. Of course we know the required lattice exists: $L = E _ { 8 }$ , but it never comes up during the proof. | `FC11-C02-U015`, `FC11-C02-U016`, `FC11-C12-U015`, `FC11-C12-U016` |
| `FC11-C12-U024` | Corollary 12.4.6 | Chapter 12, source L6433-L6436 | Corollary 12.4.6. Let $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ be a pair of non-negative integers and let $( G , q )$ be a non-degenerate quadratic torsion group. There exists a non-degenerate even lattice $L$ of rank $r = r _ { + } + r _ { - }$ , signature $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ and with discriminant form $( G , q )$ if the following two conditions are fulfilled. 1. $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \equiv \tau _ { 8 } ( q )$ mod 8, 2. $r > \ell ( G )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C12-U025` | Lemma 12.5.1 | Chapter 12, source L6442-L6450 | Lemma 12.5.1. The following pairs of 2-primary quadratic forms have isometric polar forms: 1. $\langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ and $\langle 3 \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ have polar form $\langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ , 2. $\langle 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ and $\langle - 3 \cdot 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ have polar form $\langle 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ , $\langle 3 \cdot 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ and $\left. - 1 \cdot 2 ^ { - 2 } \right.$ ⟩ have polar form $\langle - 1 \cdot 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle$ , 3. $u _ { 1 }$ and $v _ { 1 }$ have polar form $\left. u _ { 1 } \right.$ . If the normal form of a 2-primary quadratic torsion form does not contain either one of the above forms, then it is the polar form of a unique quadratic torsion form. | — |
| `FC11-C12-U026` | Proposition 12.5.2 | Chapter 12, source L6454 | Proposition 12.5.2. Two non-degenerate 2-primary quadratic torsion forms with isometric polar forms are themselves isometric if and only if they have the same index modulo 8. | `FC11-C12-U006` |
| `FC11-C12-U027` | Definition 12.5.3 | Chapter 12, source L6458-L6460 | Definition 12.5.3. The index set of a non-degenerate 2-primary symmetric torsion form $^ { b }$ is defined as the following subset of $\mathbb { Z } / 8 \mathbb { Z }$ $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( b ) = \{ \tau _ { 8 } ( q ) \in \mathbb { Z } / 8 \mathbb { Z } \mid q$ a non-degenerate quadratic torsion form with $b _ { q } \simeq b \}$ . | — |
| `FC11-C12-U028` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 12, source L6462 | If $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( b ) \subset \{ 0 , \pm 2 , 4 \}$ , respectively $\mathcal T _ { 8 } ( b ) \subset \{ \pm 1 , \pm 3 \}$ , one calls $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( b )$ an even-index set, respectively an odd–index set. If equality holds, we say that $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( b )$ is a complete even-index set, respectively a complete odd-index set. | `FC11-C12-U027` |
| `FC11-C12-U029` | Definition / construction | Chapter 12, source L6462 | An index set $\mathcal T_8(b)$ is called even-index if it is contained in $\{0,\pm2,4\}$ and odd-index if it is contained in $\{\pm1,\pm3\}$; it is complete when the corresponding containment is equality. | `FC11-C12-U027` |
| `FC11-C12-U030` | Example / comparison | Chapter 12, source L6464 | Example 12.5.4. The polar forms of Lemma 12.5.1 have the following index sets $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( \langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle ) ~ = ~ \{ \bar { 1 } , - \bar { 1 } \}$ , $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( \langle 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle ) = \{ \bar { 1 } , - \bar { 3 } \}$ , $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( \langle - 2 ^ { - 2 } \rangle ) = \{ - \bar { 1 } , \bar { 3 } \}$ , $\mathcal { T } _ { 8 } ( u _ { 1 } ) ~ = ~ \{ 0 , 4 \}$ . | `FC11-C12-U031` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 12, source L6466-L6472 | Index sets of non-degenerate $2$-primary symmetric torsion forms add by Minkowski sum in $\mathbf Z/8\mathbf Z$, and $\mathcal T_8(b)+\mathcal T_8(b\prime)\subseteq\mathcal T_8(b\oplus b\prime)$. | `FC11-C12-U033` | Lemma 12.5.6 | Chapter 12, source L6483-L6512 | Lemma 12.5.6. Let $u , u ^ { \prime } , u ^ { \prime \prime }$ be units in $\mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ . Then the following relations hold between non-homogeneous dyadic lattices. $$ \langle u \cdot 2 ^ { k - 1 } \rangle \oplus U _ { k } \simeq \langle - 3 u \cdot 2 ^ { k - 1 } \rangle \oplus V _ { k } ~ f o r ~ k \geq 1 $$ $$ U _ { k - 1 } \odot \langle u \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \simeq \langle - 3 u \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \oplus V _ { k - 1 } ~ f o r ~ k \geq 1 $$ $$ \langle u \cdot 2 ^ { k - 1 } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \simeq \langle ( u + 2 u ^ { \prime } ) \cdot 2 ^ { k - 1 } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle ( u ^ { \prime } + 2 u ) \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle f o r k \geq 1 $$ $$ ( \langle u \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle u ^ { \prime } \rangle ) ( 2 ^ { k - 1 } ) \mathbb { O } \langle 2 ^ { k } \rangle \simeq ( \langle u + 2 \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle u ^ { \prime } - 2 \rangle ) ( 2 ^ { k - 1 } ) \mathbb { O } \langle - 3 \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle $$ $$ u \equiv u ^ { \prime } { \bmod { 4 } } \quad k \geq 1 $$ $$ \langle u \cdot 2 ^ { k - 2 } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \simeq \langle - 3 u \cdot 2 ^ { k - 2 } \rangle \mathbb { O } \langle - 3 u ^ { \prime } \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle , f o r k \geq 3 . $$ Similar relations hold for their quadratic torsion forms, that is, for the 2- primary quadratic torsion groups where the exponents $2 ^ { j }$ are replaced by $2 ^ { - j }$ and for $u _ { k }$ and $v _ { k }$ instead of $U _ { k }$ and $V _ { k }$ . Let $\pmb { b } _ { 2 }$ be a non-degenerate torsion symmetric form on a 2-primary group $\mathbf { G } _ { 2 }$ such that $\pmb { b } _ { 2 } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ splits off $\langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ . 1. If $b _ { 2 } ^ { \geq 2 } = b _ { 2 } ( 2 ^ { - 1 } )$ for some 2-primary torsion symmetric form $b _ { 2 }$ which does not split off $\langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle$ , then $t _ { 8 } ( b _ { 2 } ) = \tau _ { 8 } ( \pmb { q } _ { 2 } ^ { \geq 2 } )$ , where $\pmb q _ { 2 } ^ { \geq 2 }$ is a quadratic form with polar form ??≥2. | — |
| `FC11-C12-U042` | Theorem 12.5.13 | Chapter 12, source L6630-L6636 | Theorem 12.5.13 (Existence for odd lattices [171, Thm. 1.16.5]). Let there be given two non-negative integers $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ and a non-degenerate symmetric torsion group $( G , b ^ { \# } )$ . There exists a non-degenerate odd lattice $( L , b )$ of rank $r = r _ { + } + r _ { - }$ , signature $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ and with discriminant bilinear form $( G , b ^ { \# } )$ if and only if all of the following conditions are fulfilled. a) $r \geq \ell ( G _ { p } )$ for odd primes $p$ , $r \geq \ell ( G _ { 2 } ) + 1$ (recall that $G _ { p }$ is the $p$ -primary part of $G$ ). b) For every odd prime $p$ with $r ~ = ~ \ell ( G _ { p } )$ we have $( - 1 ) ^ { r _ { - } } \| G _ { p } \| ~ = ~ \operatorname { d i s c } ( b _ { p } ^ { \# } )$ · $( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ in $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } )$ . c) Suppose that $r ~ = ~ \ell ( G _ { 2 } ) + 1$ and no non-degenerate cyclic rank 2 torsion quadratic form splits off from $b _ { 2 } ^ { \# }$ . Then $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \equiv t _ { 8 } ( b ^ { \# } ) + \delta$ mod 8, $\delta \in$ $\{ 1 , - 1 \}$ . d) Suppose that $r ~ = ~ \ell ( G _ { 2 } ) + 2$ and no non-degenerate cyclic rank 2 torsion quadratic form splits off from $b _ { 2 } ^ { \# }$ . Then $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \equiv t _ { 8 } ( b ^ { \# } ) + \delta$ mod 8, $\delta \in$ $\{ 0 , 2 , - 2 \}$ . | — |

There exists a non-degenerate odd lattice $L$ of rank $r$ , signature $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ and with discriminant form $( G , b )$ if $r \_ { \geq }$ $\ell ( G ) + 3$ . | — |
| `FC11-C13-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 13, source L6707 | The spin group of a quadratic vector space $( V , q )$ over a field $k$ is defined by means of its Clifford algebra ${ \mathsf { C } } ( q )$ . As a vector space this is just the exterior algebra $\Lambda ( V )$ and it contains $V$ . The algebra structure comes from $q$ . For us its importance lies in the action of the algebra conjugation. Firstly, conjugation with a non-isotropic vector $u \in V$ preserves $V$ and, secondly, in $V$ this gives the hyperplane reflection $\sigma _ { u }$ up to a sign. This property for $u$ can be extended to a ”large” subgroup of ${ \mathsf { C } } ( q )$ . To do this properly, one makes use of a canonical splitting of ${ \mathsf { C } } ( q )$ into even and odd elements. The even elements form a subalgebra, the even Clifford algebra. The conjugation with $u$ receives a sign according to whether $u$ is odd or even. The Clifford group consists of the invertible elements $u$ of the Clifford algebra which have the property that twisted conjugation with $u$ preserves $V$ . | — |
| `FC11-C13-U002` | Definition 13.1.1 | Chapter 13, source L6739 | Definition 13.1.1. The Clifford algebra ${ \mathsf { C } } ( q )$ is defined as the quotient of the tensor algebra $T ( V )$ on $V$ by the two-sided ideal $I ( q )$ generated by $x \otimes x - q ( x ) \cdot { \bf 1 }$ , $x \in V$ , and with multiplication induced by the multiplication in $T ( V )$ . We denote the multiplication in the Clifford algebra by a dot. | — |
| `FC11-C13-U003` | Definition / construction | Chapter 13, source L6741-L6747 | The quotient construction of the Clifford algebra carries a canonical linear map $\iota:V\to\mathsf C(q)$ with $\iota(x)^2=q(x)1$; this map is injective, and $V$ is hence identified with its image in $\mathsf C(q)$. | `FC11-C13-U002` |
| `FC11-C13-U004` | Definition / construction | Chapter 13, source L6749 | Equivalently, the Clifford algebra is characterized by the universal property that every linear map $u:V\to A$ into a unital algebra with $u(x)^2=q(x)1$ extends uniquely to an algebra homomorphism $\mathsf C(q)\to A$. | `FC11-C13-U002` |
| `FC11-C13-U005` | Definition / construction | Chapter 13, source L6751-L6763 | The Clifford algebra has a canonical $\mathbf Z/2\mathbf Z$-grading $\mathsf C(q)=\mathsf C^0(q)\oplus\mathsf C^1(q)$. The grading involution $\alpha$ is $+1$ on the even Clifford algebra and $-1$ on the odd part. | `FC11-C13-U002` |
| `FC11-C13-U006` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6765 | The tensor-degree filtration descends to $\mathsf C(q)$ and its associated graded algebra is canonically isomorphic to the exterior algebra $\Lambda V$. | — |
| `FC11-C13-U007` | Lemma 13.1.2 | Chapter 13, source L6769 | Lemma 13.1.2. There is a canonical vector space isomorphism $\Lambda ( V ) { \stackrel { \cong } { \to } } \mathsf C ( q )$ . In particular, dim?? C(??) = 2dim ??. | — |
| `FC11-C13-U008` | Example / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6771-L6773 | Hamiltonian quaternions revisited. Consider the standard diagonal form $x _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 3 } ^ { 2 }$ on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ . Its Clifford algebra has as a vector space basis $\{ 1 , e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 } , i = e _ { 2 } e _ { 3 } , j = e _ { 3 } e _ { 1 } , k = e _ { 2 } e _ { 1 } , e _ { 1 } e _ { 2 } e _ { 3 } \}$ , where $\{ e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 } \}$ is the standard basis of $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ . Since for $i , j = 1 , 2 , 3$ we have $e _ { i } e _ { j } = - e _ { j } e _ { i }$ for $i \neq j$ , and $e _ { i } ^ { 2 } = 1$ , it follows that $\pmb { i } \cdot \pmb { j } = e _ { 2 } e _ { 3 } e _ { 3 } e _ { 1 } = e _ { 2 } e _ { 1 } = \pmb { k }$ and similarly for the cyclic permutations of $i , j , k$ . Moreover, $\pmb { i } ^ { 2 } = \pmb { j } ^ { 2 } = \pmb { k } ^ { 2 } = - 1$ . So the vector space with basis $\{ 1 , \pmb { i } , \pmb { j } , \pmb { k } \}$ generates the algebra of the Hamilton quaternions $\mathbb { H }$ which we already encountered in $\ S$ 5.4.A. This is the even Clifford algebra for the dot product form on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ . As a special case of the second example below we shall see that $\mathbb { H }$ also figures as the full Clifford algebra of the form $- x _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } - x _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$ . Recall also that we explained in Section 5.4 how to associate a ternary quadratic form to any quaternion algebra $\begin{array} { r } { D = \left( \frac { a , b } { k } \right) } \end{array}$ , namely the norm form restricted to the subspace ${ \cal D } ^ { ( 0 ) }$ of the quaternions of trace $0$ . For the Hamilton quaternions this is the dot product form on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ where $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ is identified with the real space with basis $\{ \dot { \pmb { i } } , \dot { \pmb { j } } , \pmb { k } \}$ , the so-called pure quaternions. In general the even Clifford algebra of the norm form on ${ \cal D } ^ { ( 0 ) }$ is precisely $D$ . | `FC11-C13-U002` |
| `FC11-C13-U009` | Example / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6775-L6815 | Real Clifford algebras (cf. [68, 179]) By Sylvester’s law 8.1.3 nondegenerate real forms are classified by their signature $( r , s )$ with representing diagonal quadratic forms $\textstyle \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { r } x _ { i } ^ { 2 } - \sum _ { i = r + 1 } ^ { n } x _ { i } ^ { 2 }$ , $n = r + s$ . The corresponding Clifford algebra ${ \mathsf C } _ { r , s }$ is then generated by the standard basis $\{ \pmb { e } _ { 1 } , \ldots , \pmb { e } _ { r + s } \}$ subject to the relations $$ \begin{array} { r } { e _ { i } ^ { 2 } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } i \leq r } \\ { - 1 } & { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } i > r } \end{array} \right. \quad \mathrm { a n d } \quad e _ { i } \cdot e _ { j } + e _ { i } \cdot e _ { j } = 0 \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } i \neq j . } \end{array} $$ For example, ${ \mathrm { C } } _ { 0 , 0 } = \mathbb { R }$ , $\mathrm { C } _ { 1 , 0 } = \mathbb { R } \oplus \mathbb { R }$ , while ${ \mathsf C } _ { 0 , 1 } = \mathbb C$ . We shall now show that the isomorphism class of the Clifford algebra of a real quadratic form does not determine the signature. To do so we make basic use of quadratic forms over $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ as suggested by the signs that appear in the above relations. We start by considering the group ring $\mathbb { R } [ G ]$ , $G = \Pi ^ { n } C _ { 2 }$ . where $C _ { 2 }$ is the cyclic group of order 2. Viewing $C _ { 2 }$ as the field $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ and switching to additive notation and identifying groupelements of $G$ with vectors $\pmb { x } \in \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n }$ , this group ring becomes the real vector space $V : = \oplus _ { x \in G } \mathbb { R } \cdot e _ { x }$ with algebra structure induced by the additive group structure, i.e., the product $^ *$ is given by $e _ { x } * e _ { y } = e _ { x + y }$ . As vector spaces $V$ and ${ \mathsf C } _ { r , s }$ are isomorphic: identify $e _ { x } \in \ V$ with the element $\pmb { e } _ { i _ { 1 } } \cdot \cdot \cdot \pmb { e } _ { i _ { k } } \in \mathbb { C } _ { r , s }$ where $_ x$ has $i$ -th coordinate 1 precisely if $i \in \{ i _ { 1 } , \ldots , i _ { k } \}$ . In particular, if $\{ e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { n } \}$ is the standard basis of $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ , $$ e _ { e _ { k } } { \mathrm { ~ c o r r e s p o n d s ~ t o ~ } } e _ { k } . $$ The possibly non-commutative Clifford algebra ${ \mathsf C } _ { r , s }$ comes from the twisted group action $$ e _ { x } \cdot e _ { y } = ( - 1 ) ^ { \beta ^ { r , s } ( x , y ) } e _ { x } * e _ { y } , $$ where $\beta ^ { r , s }$ is the $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ -valued (in general non-symmetric) bilinear form on $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n }$ , which in coordinates with respect to the standard basis $\{ e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { n } \}$ is given by $x A ^ { r , s } { \bf y } ^ { \scriptscriptstyle 1 }$ , where $A$ is the upper-triangular matrix $$ A ^ { r , s } = \left( \begin{array} { l l l l } { 0 } & { 1 } & { \cdots } & { 1 } \\ { \vdots } & { 0 } & { \ddots } & { \vdots } \\ { \vdots } & { \ldots } & { \ddots } & { 1 } \\ { 0 } & { \cdots } & { \ldots } & { 0 } \end{array} \right) + \left( \begin{array} { l } { 0 _ { r } } \\ { \phantom { - } } \\ { 0 _ { s \times r } } \end{array} \right\| \left( \begin{array} { l } { 0 _ { r \times s } } \\ { \phantom { - } } \\ { \phantom { - } } \\ { 0 _ { s \times r } } \end{array} \right) $$ By the correspondence (13.4) the relations $( - 1 ) ^ { A _ { i j } ^ { r s } } = - 1$ for $j > i$ and $( - 1 ) ^ { A _ { i j } ^ { r s } } = 1$ for $j < i$ imply that the desired anti-commutation relations hold, while $( - 1 ) ^ { A _ { i i } ^ { r s } } = \mathbf { 1 }$ for $i \leq r$ and $( - 1 ) ^ { A _ { i i } ^ { r s } } = - { \bf 1 }$ for $i > r$ , proving the remaining two relations. Observe that as in equation (1.1) in Chapter 1, a bilinear form defines a unique quadratic form on $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n }$ . Conversely, given a basis for a $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ -vector space, a quadratic form $q$ determines an upper-triangular bilinear form $\beta$ which can be used to define a twisted product (13.5). The resulting associative algebra is denoted $C _ { \mathbb { R } } [ \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n } , q ]$ . This algebra has a special structure: either the generators $\mathbf { \boldsymbol { e } } _ { i }$ and $e _ { j }$ for $i \neq j$ anticommute (namely if $\beta _ { i j } = 1$ for $i < j$ since then $\pmb { e } _ { i } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { j } = - \pmb { e } _ { i } * \pmb { e } _ { j }$ and $\pmb { e } _ { j } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { i } = \pmb { e } _ { i } * \pmb { e } _ { j }$ ), or they commute (if $\beta _ { i j } = 0$ for $i < j$ , since then $\pmb { e } _ { i } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { j } = \pmb { e } _ { j } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { i } = \pmb { e } _ { i } * \pmb { e } _ { j }$ ). It follows that isometric forms $q$ give isomorphic algebras. So the classification of the real Clifford algebras follows from the classification of the quadratic forms on the vector spaces $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n }$ as given in Example 8.2.7. The nondegenerate forms live on even-dimensional spaces and come in two types depending on the Arf invariant. The ones with zero Arf invariant are the form $q _ { 0 } = \mathbb { Q } ^ { m } U$ , $n = 2 m$ , the ones with Arf invariant 1 are of the form $q _ { 1 } = \Phi ^ { m - 1 } U \odot \Phi ^ { 2 } \langle 1 \rangle$ , $n = 2 m$ . On odd dimensional spaces, say of dimension $n = 2 m + 1$ , one has three types with $\mathrm { d i m } \operatorname { r a d } ( q ) \leq 1$ , namely $q _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } = \Phi ^ { m } U \circledast 0$ , $q _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } = \oplus ^ { m - 1 } U \oplus \oplus ^ { 2 } \langle 1 \rangle \oplus 0$ and $q _ { 2 } = \Phi ^ { m } U \odot$ [1]. We shall show that these types indeed give all the real Clifford algebras ${ \mathsf C } _ { r , s }$ . Note however, to identify $\mathsf { C } _ { \mathbb { R } } [ \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { n } , q ]$ with some ${ \mathsf C } _ { r , s }$ , one has to transform each of the 5 types of quadratic forms $q ( { \pmb x } )$ into the desired shape $\pmb { x } A ^ { r , s } \pmb { x } ^ { \scriptscriptstyle 1 }$ , where $A ^ { r , s }$ is as in formula (13.6). This poses no problem for $\mathsf { C } _ { \mathbb { R } } \left[ \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } , q _ { i } \right]$ , $i \in \{ 0 , 1 \}$ : these are the 4-dimensional Clifford algebras $\mathrm { C _ { 2 , 0 } , C _ { 0 , 2 } }$ with generators $\mathbf { \boldsymbol { e } } _ { 1 } , \mathbf { \boldsymbol { e } } _ { 2 }$ subject to $$ \begin{array} { r l } { \pmb { e } _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } = \pmb { e } _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } = 1 , \quad } & { \pmb { e } _ { 1 } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { 2 } = - \pmb { e } _ { 2 } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { 1 } \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ } q _ { 0 } } \\ { \pmb { e } _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } = \pmb { e } _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } = - 1 , \quad } & { \pmb { e } _ { 1 } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { 2 } = - \pmb { e } _ { 2 } \cdot \pmb { e } _ { 1 } \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ } q _ { 1 } . } \end{array} $$ Next, since $x _ { 1 } x _ { 2 } + x _ { 2 } ^ { 2 }$ has Arf invariant $0$ it is isometric to $x _ { 1 } x _ { 2 }$ (in fact $( x _ { 1 } , x _ { 2 } ) \mapsto$ $\left( x _ { 1 } + x _ { 2 } , x _ { 2 } \right)$ is an isometry). The first corresponds to $\mathrm { C _ { 1 , 1 } }$ and the second to $\mathrm { C _ { 2 , 0 } }$ which indeed shows that the Clifford algebra does not determine the signature. To continue, one checks that $\mathsf C _ { 2 m , 0 } = \mathsf C _ { \mathbb R } [ \mathbb F _ { 2 } ^ { 2 m } , q _ { 0 } ]$ since $A ^ { 2 m , 0 }$ corresponds to $q _ { 0 }$ . Likewise ${ \mathsf C } _ { 0 , 2 m } = { \mathsf C } _ { \mathbb R } [ \oplus ^ { 2 m } \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } , q _ { 1 } ]$ . The forms $q _ { 0 } ^ { \prime } , q _ { 1 } ^ { \prime }$ correspond to $\mathrm { C } _ { 2 m + 1 , 0 }$ , respectively $\mathsf { C } _ { 0 , 2 m + 1 }$ and $q _ { 2 }$ corresponds to $\mathsf { C } _ { m , m + 1 }$ . The resulting 2 types of $2 ^ { 2 m }$ - dimensional real Clifford algebras and 3 types of $2 ^ { 2 m + 1 }$ -dimensional real Clifford algebras fit in a remarkable periodic table of period 8 displayed as [137, Table II in §I.4]. This table makes use of an identification of these Clifford algebras with familiar matrix algebras as follows. The relations (13.7) show that the assignments $\pmb { e } _ { 1 } \mapsto \left( \begin{array} { c c } { { 1 } } & { { 0 } } \\ { { 0 } } & { { - 1 } } \end{array} \right)$ −1 , ??2 ↦→ $\pmb { e } _ { 2 } \mapsto \left( \begin{array} { c c } { { 0 } } & { { 1 } } \\ { { 1 } } & { { 0 } } \end{array} \right)$ give an algebra-isomorphism of $C _ { 2 , 0 }$ with $\mathsf { M a t } ( 2 , \mathbb { R } )$ . Similarly, the map induced by $\pmb { e } _ { 1 } \mapsto \pmb { i }$ , $\pmb { e } _ { 2 } \mapsto \pmb { j }$ (13.8) identifies the Clifford algebra $\mathsf { C } _ { 0 , 2 }$ with the Hamiltonian quaternions. It follows that $\mathsf C _ { 2 m , 0 } = \mathsf C _ { \mathbb R } [ \mathbb F _ { 2 } ^ { 2 m } , q _ { 0 } ]$ leads to $\otimes ^ { m } \mathsf { M a t } ( 2 , \mathbb { R } ) = \mathsf { M a t } ( 2 ^ { m } , \mathbb { R } )$ and $\mathsf { C } _ { 0 , 2 m } = \mathsf { C } _ { \mathbb { R } } \left[ \oplus ^ { 2 m } \mathbb { F } _ { 2 } , q _ { 1 } \right]$ to $\mathsf { M a t } ( 2 , \mathbb { R } ) \otimes \mathbb { H } \simeq$ ${ \mathsf { M a t } } ( 2 ^ { m - 1 } , \mathbb { H } )$ . Finally, the Clifford algebras $\mathsf { C } _ { 2 m + 1 , 0 }$ , $\mathrm { C } _ { 0 , 2 m + 1 }$ and $\mathsf { C } _ { m , m + 1 }$ are isomorphic to $\mathsf { M a t } ( 2 ^ { m } , \mathbb { R } ) + \mathsf { M a t } ( 2 ^ { m } , \mathbb { R } )$ , $\mathsf { M a t } ( 2 ^ { m - 1 } , \mathbb { H } ) + \mathsf { M a t } ( 2 ^ { m - 1 } , \mathbb { H } )$ , respectively ${ \mathsf { M a t } } ( 2 ^ { m } , \mathbb { C } )$ . | `FC11-C08-U014`, `FC11-C13-U002` |
| `FC11-C13-U010` | Proposition 13.1.5 | Chapter 13, source L6833-L6836 | Proposition 13.1.5. Suppose that $\operatorname { c h a r } ( k ) = 2$ . Let $q$ be a non-degenerate quadratic form, and let $\{ e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { 2 n } \}$ be a symplectic basis such that $\begin{array} { r } { q = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { n } x _ { i } x _ { n + i } { + } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { 2 n } a _ { j } x _ { j } ^ { 2 } } \end{array}$ . Setting $\pmb { z } = e _ { 1 } e _ { n + 1 } + \cdot \cdot \cdot + e _ { n } e _ { 2 n } \in \mathsf { C } ^ { 0 } ( q )$ , the center of ${ \mathsf { C } } ( q )$ is $k$ and the center of ${ \mathsf { C } } ^ { 0 } ( q )$ equals $k + k z$ . Moreover, $z$ satisfies the relation ${ \pmb z } ^ { 2 } + { \pmb z } \equiv { \pmb a } ( q )$ mod $\wp ( k )$ , where we recall that ${ \mathfrak { a } } ( q )$ is the Arf invariant of $q$ . | — |
| `FC11-C13-U011` | Definition / construction | Chapter 13, source L6855 | The Clifford group consists of the invertible Clifford-algebra elements whose twisted adjoint action preserves $V$. | — |
| `FC11-C13-U012` | Example / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6865-L6871 | 3.2). The involution $\mathbf { \Delta } \mathbf { x } \mapsto \mathbf { \Delta } \mathbf { x } ^ { * }$ sending $\pmb { x } = \pmb { x } _ { 0 } +$ $x _ { 1 } \pmb { i } \pmb { x } _ { 2 } \pmb { j } \pmb { + } x _ { 3 } \pmb { k }$ to $\pmb { x } ^ { * } = x _ { 0 } - x _ { 1 } \pmb { i } - x _ { 2 } \pmb { j } - x _ { 3 } \pmb { k }$ (the usual extension of complex conjugation to quaternions) can be used to define the norm $\mathsf { N m } ( \pmb { x } ) = \pmb { x } \pmb { x } ^ { * } = \pmb { x } _ { 0 } ^ { 2 } + \pmb { x } _ { 1 } ^ { 1 } + \pmb { x } _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } + \pmb { x } _ { 3 } ^ { 2 } \in \mathbb { R }$ of a quaternion $_ x$ . A non-zero element $\pmb { x } \in \mathbb { H }$ has $\mathsf { N m } ( \pmb { x } ) ^ { - 1 } \pmb { x }$ as its inverse. It is easy to verify that conjugating by $_ x$ preserves the span of $e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 }$ . So $\mathbb { H } - \{ 0 \}$ is the special Clifford group. Now identify the pure quaternions, i.e. those with $x _ { 0 } = 0$ , with (another copy of) $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ . These are preserved under conjugation with a non-zero quaternion, as one easily verifies. This gives a representation of the special Clifford group on $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ by means of orthogonal transformations. In fact, one can show that it gives a surjection onto the rotation group SO(3) with kernel $\mathbb { R } ^ { \times }$ , whence a finite surjective homomorphism $$ \mathrm { S p i n ( 3 ) } = \{ { \pmb x } \in \mathbb { H } \mid \mathsf { N m } ( { \pmb x } ) = 1 \} \mathrm { S O ( 3 ) } . $$ The left-hand side is the classical spin group. The map turns out to have degree 2 and gives the universal cover of SO(3). See also Remark 13.3.6.3. | `FC11-C13-U011` |
| `FC11-C13-U013` | Example / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6873-L6875 | Let us consider the Lorentzian type inner product space $\{ e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 } \}$ and quadratic form given by $q ( x _ { 1 } , x _ { 2 } , x _ { 3 } ) = x _ { 1 } ^ { 2 } + x _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } - x _ { 3 } ^ { 2 }$ $\mathbb { R } ^ { 3 }$ with standard basis . T ${ \mathsf { C } } ^ { 0 } ( q )$ is spanned by $\boldsymbol { j } = e _ { 1 } e _ { 3 }$ $\pmb { k } = e _ { 2 } e _ { 1 }$ , but now $\pmb { i } ^ { 2 } = \pmb { j } ^ { 2 } = 1$ $\pmb { k } ^ { 2 } = - 1$ , ${ \pmb i } { \pmb j } = - { \pmb j } { \pmb i } = - { \pmb k }$ and similarly for cyclic permutations of $i , j , k$ . This algebra has a representation as a matrix algebra, where $x + i y + j z + k w$ corresponds to the matrix $\begin{array} { r l } { \left( x + y \right. } & { { } z - u } \\ { \left. z + u \right. } & { { } x - y } \end{array}$ The twisted adjoint is not a faithful representation. Its kernel is going to play a central role in Section 13.3. | — |

| `FC11-C13-U015` | Lemma 13.2.3 | Chapter 13, source L6901-L6902 | Lemma 13.2.3. The two involutions $\alpha$ and $^ *$ preserve the Clifford group $\operatorname { C l i f } ( q )$ | `FC11-C13-U011` |
| `FC11-C13-U016` | Example / comparison | Chapter 13, source L6911 | Example 13.2.4. For Examples 13.2.1 we find that $1 ^ { * } = 1$ and $\mathbf { i } ^ { * } = - \mathbf { i }$ , $\pmb { j } ^ { * } = - \pmb { j }$ and $k ^ { * } = - k$ . This shows that the notation is consistent with the notation for the classical Hamiltonians. For the latter we have seen that ${ \pmb x } \cdot { \pmb x } ^ { * } \in \mathbb { R }$ . This is also true for the second example: we find that $\pmb { x } \cdot \pmb { x } ^ { * } = x ^ { 2 } - y ^ { 2 } - z ^ { 2 } + w ^ { 2 }$ . However, for mixed degree elements in a Clifford algebra this need not be the case as is shown by the example ${ \pmb x } = e _ { 1 } + e _ { 1 } e _ { 2 }$ , where ${ \pmb x } \cdot { \pmb x } ^ { * } = ( e _ { 1 } + e _ { 1 } e _ { 2 } ) ( e _ { 1 } + e _ { 2 } e _ { 1 } ) = 2 - 2 e _ { 2 }$ . | `FC11-C13-U002`, `FC11-C13-U012` |

| `FC11-C13-U019` | Corollary 13.3.3 | Chapter 13, source L6947-L6958 | Corollary 13.3.3. 1. For $u \in \operatorname { C l i f } ( q )$ the induced map $\widetilde { \mathrm { A d } _ { u } }$ on $V$ is an orthogonal transformation, and if $u \in \mathrm { C l i f } ^ { 0 } ( q )$ , it is a rotation. 2. $u \in \operatorname { C l i f } ( q )$ can be written as a product $u = v _ { 1 } \cdot \cdot \cdot v _ { r }$ with non-isotropic $v _ { j } \in V$ , $j = 1 , \dotsc , r$ . Consequently $$ \mathsf { N m } _ { \mathrm { s p i n } } ( u ) = u \cdot \alpha ( u ^ { * } ) = ( - 1 ) ^ { r } q ( v _ { 1 } ) \cdot \cdot \cdot q ( v _ { r } ) \in k ^ { \times } . $$ If $u \in \mathrm { C l i f } ^ { 0 } ( q )$ , then $r$ can be taken even. 3. | `FC11-C13-U024` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 13, source L7013-L7017 | The image of the spinor norm on the rotation group is a subgroup of $\mathsf { D } ( k )$ which might be a proper subgroup as shown in Remark 13.3.6.1 below. | `FC11-C13-U025` | Remark 13.3.6 | Chapter 13, source L7019-L7021 | Remark 13.3.6. 1. For $k = \mathbb { R }$ the group $\mathsf { D } ( k )$ is the cyclic group $C _ { 2 }$ represented by the real numbers $\{ 1 , - 1 \}$ . Often we use these representatives instead of their classes. Note that if $q$ is positive definite, the spinor norm is always 1 and so $\mathsf { N m } _ { \mathrm { s p i n } }$ is not surjective in this case. In particular, ${ \mathsf { S O } } ^ { + } ( q ) = { \mathsf { S O } } ( q )$ and $0 ^ { + } ( q ) = 0 ( q )$ . 2. Suppose $k$ is a topological field. Then the orthogonal groups ${ \mathrm { O } } ( q )$ and SO(??) are in a natural way topological groups. The preceding constructions give the Clifford group $\operatorname { C l i f } ( q )$ and the spin group $\mathrm { S p i n } ( q )$ the structure of a topological group. The adjoint representation as well as the spinor norm are continuous. So all the maps in the preceding diagram are continuous. This holds for instance for the fields $\mathbb { R , C }$ with the usual topology, or for $\mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ endowed with the $p$ -adic topology. | `FC11-C13-U011`, `FC11-C13-U020`, `FC11-C13-U021` |

The groups $\mathsf { S O } ( r , s )$ have two connected components in case $r , s ~ \geq ~ 1$ . The component of the identity is ${ \mathsf { S O } } ^ { + } ( r , s )$ . • ${ \mathsf { S O } } ( 1 , s )$ , $s ~ \geq ~ 1$ has fundamental group $\mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ and the universal cover is $\mathsf { S p i n } ( r , s )$ ; • $\mathsf { S O } ( r , s )$ , $r , s \ge 2$ has fundamental group $\mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z } \times \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ and the universal cover is an unramified double cover of $\mathsf { S p i n } ( r , s )$ . | — |

| `FC11-C13-U033` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 13, source L7080-L7084 | To extend the definition of the $\mathrm { ( s ) p i n }$ group itself to the setting of lattices, we define $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \mathrm { \ } \mathsf { C } ( L ) : = \mathrm { s u b r i n g ~ o f ~ } \mathsf { C } ( q ) \mathrm { ~ g e n e r a t e d ~ b y ~ } 1 \mathrm { ~ a n d ~ t h e ~ e l e m e n t s ~ } x \in L , } \\ & { \mathrm { \ } \mathsf { C } ^ { 0 } ( L ) = \mathsf { C } ( L ) \cap \mathsf { C } ^ { 0 } ( q ) , } \\ & { \mathrm { \ } \mathsf { P i n } ( L ) = \mathsf { C } ( L ) \cap \mathsf { P i n } ( q ) , } \\ & { \mathrm { \ } \mathsf { S p i n } ( L ) = \mathsf { C } ( L ) \cap \mathsf { S p i n } ( q ) . } \end{array} $$ | — |

| `FC11-C14-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 14, source L7094 | In this chapter $L$ , $L ^ { \prime }$ , $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ , $M$ are free $\mathbb { Z }$ -modules of full rank within a quadratic innerproduct space $( V , q )$ over $\nsubseteq$ . In particular, $V = L _ { \mathbb { Q } } = L _ { \mathbb { Q } } ^ { \prime } = L _ { \mathbb { Q } } ^ { \prime \prime } = M _ { \mathbb { Q } }$ . Moreover the forms $b , b ^ { \prime } , \ldots$ , induced on $L , L ^ { \prime } , \ldots$ are assumed to be integer-valued. | `FC11-C14-U002` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 14, source L7100 | As explained in Section 14.1, the appropriate way to compare integral lattices in a given genus is to consider these as a full rank lattice of a fixed (quadratic) inner product space over the rational numbers, say $V$ . Observe that this fixes the signature so that the place at infinity does not play a role. Isometric lattices $L , L ^ { \prime }$ have isometric localizations $L _ { v } \simeq L _ { v } ^ { \prime }$ and so by definition belong to the same genus and belong to the localization $V _ { v }$ of $V$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U003` | Proposition 14.1.1 | Chapter 14, source L7112-L7119 | Proposition 14.1.1. Let $L , M \subset V$ be free ℤ-submodules of $V$ of maximal rank. Then 1. $M = \gamma ( L )$ for some vector space isomorphism $\gamma : V \to V$ . 2. $L _ { p } = M _ { p }$ for almost all primes $p$ . 3. $L _ { p } = M _ { p }$ for all primes $p$ if and only if $L = M$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U004` | Theorem 14.1.2 | Chapter 14, source L7132-L7138 | Theorem 14.1.2 (Glueing local lattices, [36, Ch.11, Thm. 1.1]). Let $V$ be a finite dimensional vector space over $\mathbb { Q }$ and let $L$ be $a$ free $\mathbb { Z }$ -submodule of $V$ of maximal rank. For every prime $p$ , let $M ^ { ( p ) }$ be a $p$ -adic free $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ -module of maximal rank in the localization $V _ { p }$ of $V$ . Then there exists a free $\mathbb { Z }$ -submodule $M$ of $V$ of maximal rank whose localizations are $M ^ { ( p ) }$ if and only if $$ M ^ { ( p ) } = L _ { p } \quad f o r \ a l m o s t \ a l l \ p r i m e s \ p . $$ If $M$ exists, it is uniquely determined by the condition $M ^ { ( p ) } = M _ { p }$ for all primes $p$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U005` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 14, source L7140 | When lattices in one genus are identified inside a fixed rational quadratic space $V$, two sublattices are called equivalent if a rational isometry of $V$ sends one to the other; they are genus-equivalent when this holds locally at every prime. | — |
| `FC11-C14-U006` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 14, source L7142 | If we follow the preceding convention, first of all, two sublattices $L ^ { \prime } , L ^ { \prime \prime }$ of $V$ are isometric precisely if there is a vector space isometry sending $L ^ { \prime }$ to $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ in which case we call $L ^ { \prime }$ and $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ equivalent lattices. Secondly, $L ^ { \prime }$ and $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ belong to the same genus precisely if for all primes $p$ local isometries $g _ { p }$ of $V _ { p }$ exist with $g _ { p } L _ { p } ^ { \prime } = L _ { p } ^ { \prime \prime }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U007` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 14, source L7144 | It is natural to consider a more restricted form of equivalence between sublattices $L ^ { \prime } , L ^ { \prime \prime }$ of $V$ , namely equivalence under the rotation group ${ \mathsf { S O } } ( V , q )$ in which case we call the sublattices properly equivalent. The isometry group of the vector space $V$ always contains isometries other than rotations, e.g., if $\{ e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { n } \}$ is an orthogonal basis, the linear map $\tau$ sending $e _ { 1 }$ to $- e _ { 1 }$ and fixing all other basis elements is such an isometry. Then, by definition, the lattices $L$ and $L ^ { \prime } = \tau ( L )$ are equivalent, but they need not be properly equivalent as shown in Example 3 below. | — |

It is, however, not properly equivalent to $q$ . This last example leads to the following observation. | `FC11-C06-U062` |
| `FC11-C14-U011` | Lemma 14.1.4 | Chapter 14, source L7154-L7156 | Lemma 14.1.4. Let $L$ be a sublattice of $V$ of maximal rank. If every automorphism of $L$ is a rotation, the equivalence class of $L$ splits into two proper equivalence classes. | `FC11-C14-U013` | Corollary 14.1.5 | Chapter 14, source L7166-L7176 | Corollary 14.1.5. Let $L ^ { \prime }$ be a lattice in $V$ of maximal rank. 1. If $g _ { p } ^ { \prime } \in \mathsf { S O } ( L _ { p } ^ { \prime } )$ for almost all $p$ , then there is a unique lattice $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ in $V$ for which $$ L _ { p } ^ { \prime \prime } = g _ { p } ^ { \prime } L _ { p } ^ { \prime } \quad f o r \ a l l \ p . $$ In particular $L ^ { \prime }$ and $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ belong to the same genus. 2. | `FC11-C14-U019` | Example / comparison | Chapter 14, source L7239-L7243 | Consider the integral lattice $L = A _ { 2 }$ with quadratic form $x ^ { 2 } + x y + y ^ { 2 }$ . This lattice is positive definite and has discriminant 3. For primes $p \neq 3$ the lattice $L _ { p }$ is unimodular and then, by the argument of example 1, $\mathsf { S } _ { L _ { p } } = \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ . For the prime 3 the situation is different since the lattice $L _ { 3 }$ is not unimodular. The basis $e _ { 1 } = ( 1 , 0 )$ , $e _ { 2 } = ( - 1 , 2 )$ exhibits $L _ { 3 } \simeq [ 1 ] \odot$ [3] and the corresponding reflections $\sigma _ { e _ { 1 } }$ and $\sigma _ { e _ { 2 } }$ have spinor norm 1, respectively 3. Since $q ( x e _ { 1 } + y e _ { 2 } ) =$ $x ^ { 2 } + 3 y ^ { 2 }$ cannot be of the form $- z ^ { 2 }$ in $\mathbb { Q } _ { 3 }$ (a square is 1 modulo 3), the group $\mathsf { S } _ { L _ { 3 } }$ is the cyclic group of order 2 generated by the class of ${ 1 \cdot 3 }$ and so again is strictly smaller than $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { 3 } )$ . The next result demonstrates how to use information about the images of the local spinor norms to deduce that the genus of $L$ contains only one spinor genus: | `FC11-C14-U014` |
| `FC11-C14-U020` | Theorem 14.2.5 | Chapter 14, source L7245-L7271 | Theorem 14.2.5 (Spinor equivalence $=$ genus equivalence). Let $( V , q )$ be a quadratic inner product space of dimension $\geq 3$ over $\mathbb { Q }$ and let $L \subset V$ be a maximal rank sublattice with $b _ { q } ( L , L ) \subset \mathbb { Z }$ . If for all primes $p$ the coset in $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } )$ of every $p$ -adic unit is the spinor norm of some rotation of $L _ { p }$ , all lattices in the genus of $L$ are spinor equivalent. In other words, every lattice in the same genus as $L$ is spinor equivalent to it if $$ \mathsf { S } _ { L _ { p } } = \mathsf { N m } _ { \mathrm { s p i n } } ( \mathsf { S O } ( L _ { p } ) ) \supset \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 } \ w i t h i n \ \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ) \ ( f o r \ a l l \ p r i m e s \ p ) . $$ For a given prime $p$ the inclusion (14.4) holds if a collection of $p$ -adic units $u _ { j }$ , and vectors $x _ { j } \in L _ { p }$ , $j \in J$ , exists such that (a) $q ( x _ { j } ) = u _ { j } p ^ { k }$ for all $j \in J$ , and some fixed $k$ , and all reflections $\sigma _ { x _ { j } }$ preserve the lattice $L _ { p }$ ; (b) the set of pairwise products $u _ { j _ { 1 } } u _ { j _ { 2 } }$ generate $\mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ . This occurs for instance in the following cases: 1. In case $p \neq 2$ : at least one homogeneous summand of exponent $k$ and rank $\geq 2$ splits off from $L _ { p }$ . This holds if for example $\ell ( { \mathrm { d g } _ { L _ { p } } } ) \le \mathrm { r a n k } ( L ) - 2$ . 2. In case $p = 2$ : (a) If $L$ (and hence $L _ { 2 }$ ) is an even lattice, at least one homogeneous summand of exponent $k$ and rank $\geq 2$ splits off from $L _ { 2 }$ , for example in case $\ell ( \mathrm { d g } _ { L _ { 2 } } ) \leq$ $\mathrm { r a n k } ( L ) - 2$ . (b) If ?? (and hence $L _ { 2 }$ ) is an odd lattice and a summand of one of the following three types splits off from $L _ { 2 }$ : • either one of the rank two lattices $U _ { k }$ , $V _ { k }$ ; • any homogeneous summand of exponent $k$ and rank $\geq 3$ ; • a rank 3 lattice of the form ${ \cal M } = \langle u ^ { ( 1 ) } \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \oplus \langle u ^ { ( 2 ) } \cdot 2 ^ { k } \rangle \oplus \langle u ^ { ( 3 ) } \cdot 2 ^ { k + 1 } \rangle$ for some dyadic units $\boldsymbol { u } ^ { ( i ) }$ , $i = { 1 , 2 , 3 }$ . This is the case if for example $\ell ( \mathrm { d g } _ { L _ { 2 } } ) \le \mathrm { r a n k } ( L ) - 3$ or if $u _ { k }$ or $v _ { k }$ , $k \geq 1$ , splits off from $q _ { L } ^ { \# }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U021` | Corollary 14.2.6 | Chapter 14, source L7302-L7305 | Corollary 14.2.6. Let $( W , q )$ be a quadratic inner product space over $\mathbb { Q }$ of dimension $n \geq 3$ and let $L , L ^ { \prime }$ be maximal rank quadratic sublattices of $W$ with the same discriminant $d$ . Suppose that the factorization of $d$ contains the prime 2 to the power $< \lfloor \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } ( n ^ { 2 } + 1 ) \rfloor$ and any odd prime to the power $\begin{array} { r } { < \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( n ( n - 1 ) ) } \end{array}$ . Then $L , L ^ { \prime }$ are spinor equivalent if and only if they are in the same genus. In the case of odd symmetric lattices, the estimate for the power of 2 dividing ?? can be replaced $b y < \lfloor \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( ( n - 1 ) ^ { 2 } + 1 ) \rfloor$ . | — |
| `FC11-C14-U022` | Remark 14.2.7 | Chapter 14, source L7318-L7326 | Remark 14.2.7. The theory of binary forms (lattices of rank 2) cannot be treated with the above approach. See [36, Chapter 14 and Section 13.3] for a method to classify the latter. J. Cassels gives in [36, Section 11.3] a constructive way to calculate the number of spinor genera in a given genus as the order of a certain computable finite group $G ( L )$ of exponent 2. The latter is defined as follows. Fix a finite set $P$ of prime numbers such that $2 \in P$ and $\mathsf { N m } _ { \mathrm { s p i n } } ( \mathsf { S O } ( L _ { p } ) ) = \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times } \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ for all $p \notin P$ . Such sets exist: Take for $P$ the set of primes dividing $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L )$ and add 2 if $\mathrm { d i s c } ( L )$ happens to be odd. This follows from Example 14.2.4.1 since $L _ { p }$ is unimodular for all $p \in P$ and $p \neq 2$ . Making use of $P$ , introduce the groups $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { S = \displaystyle \prod _ { p \in P } \mathsf { S } _ { L _ { p } } , \quad \mathsf { S } _ { L _ { p } } = \mathsf { N m } _ { \mathrm { s p i n } } ( \mathsf { S O } ( L _ { p } ) ) , \qquad R = \displaystyle \prod _ { p \in P } \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ) } \\ & { T = \{ ( t , \dots , t ) \in R \mid t \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } ) \mathrm { ~ s u c h ~ t h a t ~ } \left\{ t \in \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ) \mathrm { ~ s i m u l t a n e o u s l y ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ } p \notin P \right\} \quad } . \end{array} $$ Here we make use of the map $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } ) \to \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } )$ induced by the embedding $\mathbb { Q } \hookrightarrow \mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ (which preserves units and squares). Finally set $G ( L ) = R / S T$ . The result alluded to is as follows: | `FC11-C14-U018` |
| `FC11-C14-U023` | Theorem 14.2.8 | Chapter 14, source L7328 | Theorem 14.2.8 (Cassels, [36, §11.3]). The (finite commutative) group $G ( L )$ does not depend on the choice of $P$ . It has exponent 2. The set of spinor genera in the genus of $( L , b )$ forms a principal space under $G ( L )$ and so its number is equal to the order of the group $G ( L )$ and hence a power of 2. | — |
| `FC11-C14-U024` | Example / comparison | Chapter 14, source L7334-L7342 | Example 14.2.9. We enlarge the lattice of Example 14.2.4.2 to a rank 3 even lattice as follows. The Gram matrix $$ \left( { \begin{array} { c c c } { 2 } & { 1 } & { 0 } \\ { 1 } & { 2 } & { 0 } \\ { 0 } & { 0 } & { 1 8 } \end{array} } \right) $$ defines a quadratic lattice isometric to $M = L \textcircled { \ ! } \langle 2 { \cdot } 3 ^ { 2 } \rangle$ , $L \simeq A _ { 2 }$ , (with quadratic form $q ( x , y , z ) = x ^ { 2 } + x y + y ^ { 2 } + 3 ^ { 2 } z ^ { 2 } )$ . It has discriminant $2 { \cdot } 3 ^ { 3 }$ . We can take $P = \{ 2 , 3 \}$ . The local lattice $M _ { 3 }$ is isometric to $\left. 2 \right. \mathbb { O } \left. 2 \cdot 3 \right. \mathbb { O } \left. 2 \cdot 3 ^ { 2 } \right.$ . The corresponding reflections in $e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 }$ have spinor norms $1 , 3 , 1$ . Note that $q ( x , y , z ) = x ^ { 2 } + 3 y ^ { 2 } + 3 ^ { 2 } z ^ { 2 }$ does not represent $- 1 \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ since squares modulo 3 are 1. So $\mathsf { S } _ { M _ { 3 } }$ is the group generated by the class $3 \cdot ( \mathbb { Q } _ { 3 } ^ { \times } ) ^ { 2 }$ . The local lattice $M _ { 2 }$ is isometric to $L _ { 2 } \textsuperscript { \textregistered } \left. 2 \right.$ and so, by Theorem 14.2.5, $\mathsf { S } _ { M _ { 2 } }$ contains all units. In fact, it is not larger: any reflection has to preserve the indecomposable summands and we have seen in Example 14.2.4.2 that reflections from $L _ { \mathrm { 2 } }$ have spinor norm a unit, while the reflection $e _ { 3 } \ \to \ - e _ { 3 }$ has spinor norm 1. There is a subtle interaction between $S$ and $T$ in this example. Here $R = \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { 2 } ) \times \mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { 3 } )$ contains 32 elements and the group $S T$ contains 16 elements: the 8 elements $( \pm 1 , 1 ) , ( \pm 3 , 1 ) , ( \pm 1 , 3 ) , ( \pm 3 , 3 )$ and (via the diagonal action of $\{ ( 2 , 2 ) , ( 3 , 3 ) \} ~ \subset ~ T$ by coordinate-wise multiplication) we obtain the supplementary set $\{ ( \pm 2 , - 1 ) , ( \pm 6 , - 1 ) , ( \pm 2 , - 3 ) , ( \pm 6 , - 3 ) \}$ also consisting of 8 elements. Hence $G ( M ) = R / S T$ is cyclic of order two and so there are two spinor genera. A representing quadratic form for the second spinor genus is given by $q ^ { \prime } ( x , y , z ) = z ^ { 2 } + 3 ( x ^ { 2 } + x y + y ^ { 2 } )$ . To show this, observe first of all that $q ^ { \prime }$ and $q$ are in the same genus (they are positive definite and have the same localizations at 2 and 3). On the other hand $q$ and $q ^ { \prime }$ are not isometric. This can be seen using the unique splitting of positive definite forms into indecomposable forms (Eichler’s theorem 1.12.3). Indeed, the form $x ^ { 2 } + x y + y ^ { 2 }$ is indecomposable, since this is the case for its localization $L _ { \mathrm { 2 } }$ (see Section 10.1) and so is $3 ( x ^ { 2 } + x y + y ^ { 2 } )$ . So the forms $q$ and $q ^ { \prime }$ are already given as sums of indecomposable summands, and these are different. | `FC11-C01-U148`, `FC11-C14-U014`, `FC11-C14-U019`, `FC11-C14-U020` |

| `FC11-C15-U002` | Example / comparison | Chapter 15, source L7611 | Example 15.1.2. An example illustrating Proposition 15.1.1 is the following. Let $L = U \mathbb { \ Q } U$ be the sum of two copies of the hyperbolic plane $U$ , the first one with the usual basis $e , f$ (so $b ( e , e ) = b ( f , f ) = b ( e , f ) - 1 = 0 ,$ ) and the second one with similar basis $e ^ { \prime } , f ^ { \prime }$ . Take $S$ to be the span of $e + f , e ^ { \prime } + f ^ { \prime }$ in $L$ , and let $T$ be the span of $e - f , e ^ { \prime } - f ^ { \prime }$ in $L$ . Then $L$ is an even unimodular overlattice of $S \textsuperscript { \textregistered T }$ . The discriminant groups of $S$ and $T$ are $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { d } \mathrm { g } _ { S } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } S / S = \langle u = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( e + f ) + S , u ^ { \prime } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( e ^ { \prime } + f ^ { \prime } ) + S \rangle } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { \mathrm { d } \mathrm { g } _ { T } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } T / T = \langle v = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( e - f ) + T , v ^ { \prime } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } ( e ^ { \prime } - f ^ { \prime } ) + T \rangle } \end{array}$ . The image of $L / ( S \mathbb { Q } T )$ in $\mathsf { d g } _ { S } \oplus \mathsf { d g } _ { T }$ equals $\left\{ 0 , ( u , v ) , ( u ^ { \prime } , v ^ { \prime } ) , ( u + u ^ { \prime } , v + v ^ { \prime } ) \right\}$ , which is the graph of the isomorphism $\psi _ { S , T } : \mathrm { d g } _ { S } \mathrm { d g } _ { T }$ determined by $\psi _ { S , T } ( u ) = v$ and $\psi _ { S , T } ( u ^ { \prime } ) = v ^ { \prime }$ . Note that $\begin{array} { r } { b _ { S } ^ { \# } ( u , u ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } + \mathbb { Z } = - b _ { T } ^ { \# } ( v , v ) } \end{array}$ , etc. Also, for the associated quadratic form $\begin{array} { r } { q ( x ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } b ( x , x ) } \end{array}$ we have $\begin{array} { r } { q _ { S } ^ { \# } ( u ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \cdot \frac { 1 } { 2 } + \mathbb { Z } = \frac { 1 } { 4 } + \mathbb { Z } } \end{array}$ and $\begin{array} { r } { q _ { T } ^ { \# } ( v ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } \cdot - \frac { 1 } { 2 } + \mathbb { Z } = - \frac { 1 } { 4 } + \mathbb { Z } } \end{array}$ , etc. | `FC11-C15-U001` |
| `FC11-C15-U003` | Proposition 15.1.3 | Chapter 15, source L7615-L7622 | Proposition 15.1.3. Let $S$ and $T$ be non-degenerate symmetric (respectively quadratic) lattices, $\mathsf { H } _ { S } \subset \mathsf { d g } _ { S }$ a subgroup, and $\psi _ { S , T } : { \mathsf { H } } _ { S } \hookrightarrow { \mathsf { d g } } _ { T }$ an injective homomorphism. Suppose that the glueing criterion of Proposition (15.1.1) holds. Then, setting $N : = S \textcircled { \perp } T$ , we have 1. The lattice $L = \{ y \in N ^ { * } \mid y$ mod $N \in \mathsf { H }$ }, where $\mathsf { H }$ is the graph of $\psi _ { S , T }$ , is $a$ symmetric (respectively quadratic) overlattice of $N$ satisfying $[ L : N ] = \| \mathsf { H } _ { S } \|$ ; 2. ?? and $T$ embed primitively in $L$ and $T = S ^ { \perp }$ , $S = T ^ { \perp }$ ; 3. If $\mathsf { H } _ { S } = \mathsf { d } \mathrm { g } _ { S }$ and $\psi _ { S , T }$ is an isomorphism, then $L$ is unimodular. | — |
| `FC11-C15-U004` | Corollary 15.1.4 | Chapter 15, source L7629-L7630 | Corollary 15.1.4. Let $L$ be a non-degenerate even lattice with one isometry class in its genus and suppose that the non-degenerate lattice $S$ embeds primitively in $L$ . Then any lattice $S ^ { \prime }$ in the same genus as $S$ embeds primitively in $L$ . | — |
| `FC11-C15-U005` | Example / comparison | Chapter 15, source L7633 | Example 15.1.5. Here is an example of two non-isometric lattices in the same genus that embed in the same even unimodular lattice. The lattices $E _ { 8 } \ @ E _ { 8 }$ and $\Gamma _ { 1 6 }$ are in the same genus, but are not isometric (see Examples 1.5.1 and 11.3.2). The indefinite unimodular lattices $\{ \Phi ^ { k } U \ @ E _ { 8 } \ @ E _ { 8 }$ and $\mathbb { \oplus } ^ { k } U \mathbb { \oplus } \Gamma _ { 1 6 }$ , $k = 1 , 2 , \ldots$ , however, are both even and have the same signature, and so are isometric by Theorem 2.4.1. Note that Corollary 15.1.4 applies in this case, but is not needed. | `FC11-C01-U052`, `FC11-C02-U014`, `FC11-C15-U004` |
| `FC11-C15-U006` | Proposition 15.1.6 | Chapter 15, source L7637-L7649 | Proposition 15.1.6 (Extending isometries, II). Let $S , S ^ { \prime } , T , T ^ { \prime }$ be non-degenerate quadratic lattices related by isometries $\sigma : S \stackrel { \sim } { \to } S ^ { \prime }$ and $\tau : T \stackrel { \sim } { } T ^ { \prime }$ . Suppose $L$ , $L ^ { \prime }$ are quadratic overlattices of $S \oplus T$ , $S ^ { \prime } \mathcal { \text O T } ^ { \prime }$ , respectively. Assume that $S$ and $T$ are primitively embedded in $L$ , and similarly for $S ^ { \prime }$ and $T ^ { \prime }$ . Then the isometry $\lambda = \sigma \oplus \tau$ extends to an isometry $L \stackrel { \sim } { } L ^ { \prime }$ if and only if (the map induced by) $\lambda$ sends the subgroup $L / ( S \mathbb { Q } T )$ of $\mathsf { d g } _ { S } \mathbb { O } \mathsf { d g } _ { T }$ to the subgroup $L ^ { \prime } / ( S ^ { \prime } \mathbb { \oplus } T ^ { \prime } )$ of $\mathsf { d g } _ { S ^ { \prime } } \mathbb { O } \mathsf { d g } _ { T ^ { \prime } }$ . In other words, using the notation of diagram (15.1), $\lambda$ extends if and only if there is a commutative diagram $$ \begin{array} { r l } { \mathrm { d } \mathrm { g } _ { S } \longleftrightarrow } & { { } \longleftrightarrow \mathsf { H } _ { S } \longleftrightarrow \quad \quad \psi _ { S , T } \quad \quad \mathrm { ~ } \mathsf { \Gamma } \lambda } \\ { r _ { S , S ^ { \prime } } ( \sigma ) \underset { \mathrm { d } \mathrm { g } _ { S ^ { \prime } } \longleftrightarrow } { \downarrow } } & { { } \underset { H _ { S ^ { \prime } } \longleftrightarrow } { \downarrow } \underbrace { \vphantom { \sqrt { \Gamma _ { T , T ^ { \prime } } ( \tau ) } } \mathrm { d } r _ { T , T ^ { \prime } } ( \tau ) } } & { { } \mathrm { ~ } \forall r _ { T , T ^ { \prime } } ( \tau ) } \end{array} $$ where $r _ { S , S ^ { \prime } } ( \sigma )$ and $r _ { T , T ^ { \prime } } ( \tau )$ are induced by $\sigma$ and $\tau$ , respectively. In particular, if $L$ (and hence $L ^ { \prime }$ ) is unimodular, using Propositions 15.1.1, 15.1.3, this reduces to the glueing criterion exhibited by the commutative diagram $$ \begin{array} { r l } { \mathrm { d g } _ { S } \cfrac { \simeq } { \psi _ { S , T } } } & { \to \mathrm { d g } _ { T } } \\ { r _ { S , S ^ { \prime } } ( \sigma ) \sqrt { \qquad } } & { \sqrt { r _ { T , T ^ { \prime } } ( \tau ) } } \\ { \mathrm { d g } _ { S ^ { \prime } } \cfrac { \simeq } { \psi _ { S ^ { \prime } , T ^ { \prime } } } } & { \to \mathrm { d g } _ { T ^ { \prime } } } \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC11-C15-U007` | Theorem 15.1.7 | Chapter 15, source L7653-L7656 | Theorem 15.1.7 (Witt’s extension theorem for lattices). 1. Let $L$ be a nondegenerate even lattice and let $i , i ^ { \prime } : S \hookrightarrow L$ be two primitive embeddings. Let $( r _ { + } , r _ { - } )$ be a pair of non-negative integers for which $r _ { + } { - } r _ { - } \equiv 0$ mod 8 and let $( s _ { + } , s _ { - } )$ be a pair of non-negative integers with $s _ { + } \leq r _ { + }$ and $s _ { - } \leq r _ { - }$ . | `FC11-C15-U018` | Proposition 15.2.9 | Chapter 15, source L7769-L7784 | Proposition 15.2.9. Let $L$ be a quadratic unimodular lattice, $S \subset L$ a primitive non-degenerate sublattice, $T = S ^ { \perp }$ and $\sigma \in \operatorname { O } ^ { \# } ( S )$ . | `FC11-C15-U020` | Proposition 15.3.1 | Chapter 15, source L7809-L7820 | Proposition 15.3.1. Let $S$ and $M$ be non-degenerate quadratic lattices with genus invariants $\mathsf { g } ( S ) = ( s _ { + } , s _ { - } , [ q _ { S } ^ { \# } ] )$ , $\mathsf { g } ( M ) = ( m _ { + } , m _ { - } , [ q _ { M } ^ { \# } ] )$ , and satisfying $s _ { \pm } ~ \leq ~ m _ { \pm }$ . Primitive embeddings $S \hookrightarrow M$ are determined by quadruples $( H _ { S } , \psi , K , \gamma _ { K } ^ { \psi } )$ , where • $H _ { S } \subset \mathrm { d g } _ { S }$ is a subgroup, $\psi : H _ { S } \hookrightarrow \mathrm { d g } _ { M }$ an embedding of quadratic torsion groups, that is, $\psi ( H _ { S } )$ is a subgroup of ${ \mathsf { d g } } _ { M }$ and $q _ { S } ^ { \# } ( x ) = q _ { M } ^ { \# } ( \psi ( x ) )$ for all $x \in H _ { S }$ ; • $K$ is a quadratic lattice with genus-invariant $( m _ { + } - s _ { + } , m _ { - } - s _ { - } , [ - \kappa ^ { \psi } ] ) ,$ $\kappa ^ { \psi } : =$ $\Gamma _ { \psi } { ^ { \perp } } / \Gamma _ { \psi }$ , where $\Gamma _ { \psi } \subset \mathsf { d g } _ { S } \oplus \mathsf { d g } _ { M }$ is the graph of $\psi$ and $\Gamma _ { \psi } ^ { \bot }$ its orthogonal complement with respect to the polar form of $q _ { S } ^ { \# } \oplus q _ { M } ^ { \# }$ ; • $\gamma _ { K } ^ { \psi } : q _ { K } ^ { \# } \{ \tilde { \to } - \kappa ^ { \psi }$ is an isometry. The orthogonal complement of $S$ in $M$ in this embedding is isometric to $K$ . Two embeddings $i : S \hookrightarrow M$ and $i ^ { \prime } : S \hookrightarrow M$ given by $( H _ { S } , \psi , K , \gamma )$ and $( H _ { S } ^ { \prime } , \psi ^ { \prime } , K ^ { \prime } , \gamma ^ { \prime } )$ are equivalent if and only if the following two conditions hold simultaneously: • $H _ { S } = H _ { S } ^ { \prime }$ and there exists an isometry $\lambda$ of $\mathsf { d g } _ { M }$ such that $\lambda \circ \psi = \psi ^ { \prime }$ ; • there is an isometry $\varphi : K { \stackrel { \sim } { \to } } K ^ { \prime }$ such that $\mathrm { d g } _ { \varphi } \circ \gamma = \gamma ^ { \prime } \circ \lambda ^ { \prime }$ , where the isometry $\lambda ^ { \prime } : - \kappa ^ { \psi } \stackrel { \sim } { } - \kappa ^ { \psi ^ { \prime } }$ is induced by $\lambda$ . | — |
| `FC11-C15-U021` | Remark 15.3.2 | Chapter 15, source L7853-L7855 | Remark 15.3.2. 1. The case $H _ { S } = 0$ is allowed and serves for instance to cover unimodular lattices $M$ . | `FC11-C15-U025` | Example / comparison | Chapter 15, source L7873-L7885 | Let $M = L \oplus L ^ { \prime } ( - 2 )$ with $L$ and $L ^ { \prime }$ indefinite unimodular quadratic lattices. Consider the $\mathrm { O } ( M )$ -orbits of vectors $x \in M$ with $q ( x ) = - 2$ . Then $S = \langle - 4 \rangle$ and hence $\mathrm { d g } _ { S } = \frac { 1 } { 4 } \mathbb { Z } / \mathbb { Z } \simeq \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z }$ . So $H _ { S } = 0$ or $H _ { S } = \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ . By the classification of even indefinite unimodular forms as given in Section 2.4, the lattice $M$ splits off $U \oplus U ( - 2 )$ , and so the lattice $K = S ^ { \perp }$ is indefinite of rank $\geq 3$ . As follows from Example 1.6.8.2 and 11.2.5.3, the discriminant quadratic form of $L ^ { \prime } ( 2 )$ is isometric to a number of copies of $u _ { 1 }$ $\mathbb { Q } ^ { m } u _ { 1 }$ $2 m = \mathrm { r a n k } ( L ^ { \prime } )$ If ???? = 0, ??#?? $q _ { K } ^ { \# } = q _ { L ^ { \prime } ( 2 ) } ^ { \# }$ hich off a co $u _ { 1 }$ ence Theorem 14.5.5 shows that conditions 2 and 3 are satisfied. Condition 1 being trivially satisfied, the preceding theorem shows that there is a unique orbit of $x$ of this type. Since $L$ contains a copy of $U$ , the orbit contains $e \mathrm { - 2 } f$ , where $\{ e , f \}$ is the standard basis of $U$ . This deals with the case $H _ { S } = 0$ . Assume now that $H _ { S } = \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ . We can view ${ \mathsf { d g } } _ { M }$ as a symplectic $\mathbb { F } _ { 2 }$ -space and so by an argument as in the proof of Proposition A.5.2 we may assume that $H _ { S }$ embeds in the first copy of $u _ { 1 }$ as $\langle { \bar { e } } + { \bar { f } } \rangle$ , where $\{ \bar { e } , f \}$ is the standard basis of $u _ { 1 }$ . Then $q _ { K } ^ { \# } \simeq \langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus ^ { m - 1 } u _ { 1 }$ . In this case $$ \ell ( \mathrm { d } \mathrm { g } _ { K } ) = \mathrm { r a n k } ( L ^ { \prime } ) - 1 = \mathrm { r a n k } ( M ) - \mathrm { r a n k } ( L ) - 1 \leq \mathrm { r a n k } ( M ) - 3 , $$ and hence conditions 2 and 3 are satisfied. Note that the two types of orbits of $x$ can be characterized as follows: for $H _ { S } = 0$ one must have $x \cdot M = \mathbb { Z }$ (since $x$ can be assumed to be in the unimodular lattice $L$ ). For $H _ { S } = \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ for similar reasons one must have $x \cdot M = 2 \mathbb { Z }$ . | `FC11-C01-U088`, `FC11-C14-U042` |

Either $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \not \equiv 0$ mod 8, or else, ${ \mathsf { O } } ( q ^ { \# } )$ is properly included in ${ \mathsf { O } } ( b ^ { \# } )$ and a (non-degenerate) even lattice $T$ of signature $( r _ { + } - s _ { + } , r _ { - } - s _ { - } )$ with discriminant form $- b ^ { \# }$ exists; 3. Either $r _ { + } - r _ { - } \not \equiv 0$ mod 8, or else, ${ \mathsf { O } } ( q ^ { \# } )$ is properly included in ${ \mathsf { O } } ( b ^ { \# } )$ and a (non-degenerate) even lattice $T ^ { \prime }$ of signature $( r _ { - } - s _ { - } , r _ { + } - s _ { + } )$ with discriminant form $b ^ { \# }$ exists. If, moreover, both $r _ { + } > 0$ and $r _ { - } ~ > ~ 0$ , the unimodular lattice $L$ is unique up to isometry. Then $S$ cannot be embedded in $L$ with even orthogonal complement, but if $a \mathrm { ~ + ~ } c \mathrm { ~ \geq ~ 3 ~ }$ the lattice $S$ can be embedded in $L$ with $S ^ { \perp } = \oplus ^ { b } \langle 1 \rangle \oplus \oplus ^ { b } \langle - 1 \rangle$ . | `FC11-C15-U030` | Theorem 15.4.4 | Chapter 15, source L7923 | Theorem 15.4.4. The classical theta functions give an abundance of such functions. | `FC11-C16-U036` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 16, source L8291-L8295 | 16.4.C Weil pairing on torsion points of $J ( C )$ . Then $k D _ { j } = \operatorname { d i v } ( f _ { j } )$ for some non-zero rational functions $f _ { 1 } , f _ { 2 }$ . If $D _ { 1 }$ and $D _ { 2 }$ have disjoint supports, then so have $f _ { 1 }$ and $D _ { 2 }$ as well as $f _ { 2 }$ and $D _ { 1 }$ . Hence we can define $$ \begin{array} { r } { e _ { k } ( D _ { 1 } , D _ { 2 } ) : = f _ { 1 } ( D _ { 2 } ) / f _ { 2 } ( D _ { 1 } ) , \quad \mathrm { d i v } ( f _ { j } ) = k D _ { j } . } \end{array} $$ | — |

| `FC11-C16-U039` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 16, source L8349 | On the other hand, a sum $q + q ^ { \prime }$ of two quadratic forms in $Q ( V )$ is a linear form, and hence has the shape $x \mapsto J ( x , v )$ for a vector $v = v ( q , q ^ { \prime } )$ uniquely determined by $q$ and $q ^ { \prime }$ . So adding two quadratic forms $q$ and $q ^ { \prime }$ defines a unique vector in $V$ . The set $Q ( V )$ is not a vector space, since it has no zero, but the disjoint union of $V$ and $Q ( V )$ is a vector space of dimension $2 n + 1$ if we take the original vector space structure on $V$ , we use the $t$ -action on $Q ( V )$ for addition of vectors $v \in V$ and $q \in Q ( V )$ , and, finally, we define $\boldsymbol { q } + \boldsymbol { q } ^ { \prime } = \boldsymbol { v } ( \boldsymbol { q } , \boldsymbol { q } ^ { \prime } )$ whenever $q , q ^ { \prime } \in Q ( V )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C16-U040` | Proposition 16.4.3 | Chapter 16, source L8353-L8354 | Proposition 16.4.3. There are $2 ^ { g - 1 } ( 2 ^ { g } + 1 )$ forms with Arf invariant 0 and $2 ^ { g - 1 } ( 2 ^ { g } - 1 )$ with Arf invariant 1. | — |
| `FC11-C16-U041` | Theorem 16.4.4 | Chapter 16, source L8359 | Theorem 16.4.4. Let $c$ be a smooth complex algebraic curve of genus $g ~ \geq ~ 1$ . Then $c$ has $2 ^ { g - 1 } ( 2 ^ { g } + 1 )$ even and $2 ^ { g - 1 } ( 2 ^ { g } - 1 )$ odd theta characteristics. The even theta characteristics correspond to quadratic forms with Arf invariant 0 and the odd ones to quadratic forms with Arf invariant 1. | `FC11-C16-U031` |

| `FC11-C17-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 17, source L8391 | We also saw that we may assume that $x$ is primitive since the above condition is insensitive under scaling of $x$ . The resulting vector is unique up to sign and so $k : = 2 q ( x ) \in \mathbb { Z }$ is an invariant of the reflection, and $x$ is called the $k$ -root associated to the reflection and the reflection is called a reflection in a $k$ - root. Note that $k$ is even for quadratic lattices but for odd lattices it might be odd. Since $x$ is primitive, the function on $L$ given by $y \mapsto 2 b ( x , y ) / k$ is integral valued and defines an element in the discriminant group ${ \mathsf { d g } } _ { L }$ . It is torsion of order ${ \scriptstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } \| k \|$ or $\| k \|$ according to whether $k$ is even or odd respectively. In particular, for a unimodular integral lattice one can only have $k = \pm 1$ or $k = \pm 2$ . The sublattice of $L$ generated by all $k$ -roots for varying $k$ is called its root sublattice, and the group generated by reflections in the roots, the reflection group of $L$ . If $L$ is spanned by its roots, it is called a root lattice. Since roots play such a central role, it is crucial to know for which lattices there are many roots, a special case of Corollary A.3.7 from the Appendix gives an instance where this is the case: | — |
| `FC11-C17-U002` | Proposition 17.1.1 | Chapter 17, source L8393 | Proposition 17.1.1. Suppose $L$ is an indefinite lattice of rank $\geq 4$ . If $L$ contains $a$ $k$ -root, it contains infinitely many $k$ -roots. | — |

| `FC11-C17-U019` | Proposition 17.2.5 | Chapter 17, source L8504-L8512 | Proposition 17.2.5. Then $\{ x \in ( \mathsf C _ { L _ { \mathbb R } } \mid b ( x , r ) > 0$ for all $r \in \Delta \}$ is $a$ Weyl chamber. 3. W (??) acts simply transitively on root bases and on Weyl chambers. 4. The roots of given length form one orbit under ${ \mathsf { W } } ( L )$ . | `FC11-C17-U018` |
| `FC11-C17-U020` | Observation | Chapter 17, source L8516-L8518 | Observation. Reflections in $k$ -roots with $k < 0$ preserve the components of the light cone. By (16.3) the subgroup of isometries of $L$ preserving the two components of the light cone is $\mathrm { O } ^ { - } ( L )$ . In particular it contains the reflections in $k$ -roots, $k < 0$ , since these have signed spinor norm 1. The corresponding Weyl group ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( L )$ is a normal subgroup of $\mathrm { O } ^ { - } ( L )$ . | — |

| `FC11-C17-U022` | Proposition 17.2.6 | Chapter 17, source L8522-L8526 | Proposition 17.2.6. Let $\Delta ( L )$ be the set of roots $r \in L$ with $b ( r , r ) ~ < ~ 0$ . Let $\mathcal { C }$ be a Weyl chamber. The set of roots $\Delta _ { \mathcal { C } } \ = \ \{ r \ \in \ \Delta ( L ) \ \| \ b ( x , r ) \ >$ 0 for all $x \in \mathcal { C }$ } has the property that $\Delta ( L ) = \Delta _ { \mathcal { C } } \cup - \Delta _ { \mathcal { C } }$ . 2. Conversely, let $\Delta ( L ) = \Delta \cup - \Delta$ be a partition of the set $\Delta ( L )$ such that $\Delta$ is closed under taking sums, i.e., any non-negative integral linear combination of roots in $\Delta$ belongs to $\Delta$ . Then $\{ x \in \mathsf C _ { V _ { \mathbb R } } \mid b ( x , r ) > 0$ for all $r \in \Delta$ } is $a$ Weyl chamber. | `FC11-C17-U021` |
| `FC11-C17-U023` | Corollary 17.2.7 | Chapter 17, source L8528 | Corollary 17.2.7. The group $0 ^ { - } ( L )$ of isometries preserving the light cone is the semi-direct product of ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( L )$ and the stabilizer in $0 ^ { - } ( L )$ of $a$ Weyl chamber. Consequently, the latter group is isomorphic to $0 ^ { - } ( L ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L )$ . | `FC11-C17-U021` |
| `FC11-C17-U024` | Remark 17.2.8 | Chapter 17, source L8530 | Remark 17.2.8. | `FC11-C17-U025` | Theorem 17.2.9 | Chapter 17, source L8534 | Theorem 17.2.9 ([239, 240, 172, 72]). A root lattice of signature $( 1 , n )$ is reflective if and only if $n \in \{ 1 , . . . , 1 9 , 2 1 \}$ . If $n = 2 1$ such a lattice is even: it is the unique reflective Lorentzian lattice of rank 22 (see Example 17.2.10.5 below). In particular there are no odd reflective Lorentzian lattices of rank 22. | `FC11-C17-U014` |
| `FC11-C17-U026` | Example / comparison | Chapter 17, source L8538-L8550 | The Lorentz lattice $\mathbb { Z } ^ { 1 , n }$ , $n \geq 2$ , is a root lattice. Indeed, using, as before, the standard basis $\{ e _ { 0 } , \ldots , e _ { n } \}$ for $\mathbb { R } ^ { n + 1 }$ it has a new basis $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \alpha _ { 0 } = ( 1 , - 1 , - 1 , - 1 , 0 , \ldots , 0 ) , \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ } n \geq 3 , \quad = ( 1 , - 1 , - 1 ) \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ } n = 2 , } \\ & { \alpha _ { j } = e _ { j } - e _ { j + 1 } , \quad j = 1 , \ldots , n - 1 , } \\ & { \alpha _ { n } = e _ { n } . } \end{array} $$ The vectors $\alpha _ { j }$ , $j = 1 , \dotsc , n - 1$ , are $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots, $\alpha _ { n }$ is a $( - 1 )$ -root and $\alpha _ { 0 }$ is a $\left( - 2 \right)$ -root for $n \geq 3$ , but a $( - 1 )$ -root for $n = 2$ . Although the Lorentz lattice has a basis of roots, the corresponding reflections might not generate the full reflection group. E. Vinberg developed an algorithm which terminates if a finite set of roots exists such that the corresponding reflection group has finite index in ${ \boldsymbol { \mathrm { O } } } ( \mathbb { Z } ^ { 1 , n } )$ .1 Termination occurs if and only if $n \leq 1 9$ and so precisely then $\mathbb { Z } ^ { 1 , n }$ is reflective. For a proof which is more in the spirit of lattice theory see [24, §6, especially Cor. 6.3 and Lemma 6.5]. These results show in particular that for $2 \leq n \leq 9$ the above root basis suffices to generate the full reflection group. The corresponding graphs resemble the graphs $E _ { n + 1 } ( - 1 )$ (cf. Eqn. (4.4)): ![](images/72cceafc8337fff99b5ce7659457a77fd26007b70fd1cd72b0500fdb6ac6978e.jpg) Here we denote the self-intersections of the roots below the vertices: among the depicted roots the black ones are $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots and the white ones are $( - 1 )$ -roots. As mentioned before, for $2 \leq n \leq 1 9$ the lattices $\mathbb { Z } ^ { 1 , n }$ are reflective, but they might not be $\left( - 2 \right)$ -reflective because of the presence of $( - 1 )$ -roots in the basis. For $2 \leq n \leq 9$ we shall show below in Example 17.3.8 that $\mathbb { Z } ^ { 1 , n }$ is not $\left( - 2 \right)$ -reflective. | `FC11-C17-U014`, `FC11-C17-U018` |

| `FC11-C17-U028` | Example / comparison | Chapter 17, source L8554-L8560 | The hyperbolic $T$ -shaped lattices from Section 4.1. Recall that the lattices $T _ { p , q , r }$ are even root lattices which for $p ^ { - 1 } + q ^ { - 1 } + r ^ { - 1 } < 1$ are all Lorentzian. Apart from the lattices with $( p , q , r ) = ( 2 , 3 , r )$ , $r = 7 , 8 , 9$ , from Example 2, only the lattices with $( p , q , r ) = ( 2 , 4 , r )$ with $r = 5 , 6 , 7$ and $( p , q , r ) = ( 3 , 3 , r )$ with $r = 4 , 5 , 6$ are reflective. The roots corresponding to the vertices give a root basis only for $( p , q , r ) = ( 2 , 3 , 7 ) , ( 3 , 3 , 4 ) , ( 2 , 4 , 5 )$ . To obtain a generating set of roots for the other root lattices, some roots have to be added to a basis. See [172] for details.
