In our case this is equivalent to $b ( \alpha _ { 1 1 } , \alpha _ { 1 2 } ) = - 2$ . 4. Reflection groups related to the Leech lattice $\Gamma _ { 2 4 }$ . Recall (Section 5.1) that the Leech lattice is a positive definite rank 24 unimodular lattice, the unique Niemeier lattice without roots. The even unimodular Lorentzian lattice $$ I I _ { 1 , 2 5 } : = U \oplus \Gamma _ { 2 4 } ( - 1 ) $$ is by classification (cf. Corollary 2.4.3) isometric to $U \ @ E _ { 8 } ^ { \oplus 3 } ( - 1 )$ . Since $U \mathbb { \oplus } E _ { 8 } ( - 1 )$ and $E _ { 8 } ( - 1 )$ are spanned by their roots (see $\ S$ 4.1.C) so is $I I _ { 1 , 2 5 }$ . The group generated by the reflections in these roots has a fundamental domain $P$ described in [42] whose bounding hyperplanes are orthogonal to roots of the form $e - ( 1 + q ( v ) ) f + v$ where $v \in \Gamma _ { 2 4 } ( - 1 )$ and $\{ e , f \}$ is the standard basis of $U$ . Those roots are called the Leech roots. The lattice $I I _ { 1 , 2 5 }$ is not reflective: the symmetry group of $P$ turns out to be isomorphic to $\Gamma _ { 2 4 } \triangleleft ( \Gamma _ { 2 4 } )$ , an infinite group isomorphic to $0 ^ { - } ( L ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L )$ . | `FC11-C02-U016`, `FC11-C17-U014` |
| `FC11-C17-U030` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 17, source L8568 | is by classification (cf. Corollary 2.4.3) isometric to $U \ @ E _ { 8 } ^ { \oplus 3 } ( - 1 )$ . Since $U \mathbb { \oplus } E _ { 8 } ( - 1 )$ and $E _ { 8 } ( - 1 )$ are spanned by their roots (see $\ S$ 4.1.C) so is $I I _ { 1 , 2 5 }$ . The group generated by the reflections in these roots has a fundamental domain $P$ described in [42] whose bounding hyperplanes are orthogonal to roots of the form $e - ( 1 + q ( v ) ) f + v$ where $v \in \Gamma _ { 2 4 } ( - 1 )$ and $\{ e , f \}$ is the standard basis of $U$ . Those roots are called the Leech roots. The lattice $I I _ { 1 , 2 5 }$ is not reflective: the symmetry group of $P$ turns out to be isomorphic to $\Gamma _ { 2 4 } \triangleleft ( \Gamma _ { 2 4 } )$ , an infinite group isomorphic to $0 ^ { - } ( L ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L )$ . | `FC11-C02-U016`, `FC11-C17-U014` |
| `FC11-C17-U031` | Example / comparison | Chapter 17, source L8570 | The Borcherds lattice [24]. The sublattice $L _ { 1 , 2 1 } \subset E _ { 2 1 } ( - 1 )$ consisting of vectors $\sum x _ { i } e _ { i }$ with $\textstyle \sum x _ { i }$ even defines an even lattice. It is isometric to $U \mathbb { \oplus } D _ { 2 0 } ( - 1 )$ and turns out to be a reflective root lattice having a generating set of 210 roots. Moreover, the quotient $0 ^ { - } ( L _ { 1 , 2 1 } ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L _ { 1 , 2 1 } )$ is isomorphic to a finite group of order $2 ^ { 8 } \cdot 3 ^ { 3 } \cdot 5 \cdot 7$ . It is the reflective Lorentzian lattice of maximal possible rank. We shall encounter this lattice again as an example of a supersingular K3 lattice (see Remark 19.5.3). | `FC11-C17-U014` |

| `FC11-C17-U038` | Lemma 17.3.2 | Chapter 17, source L8655-L8662 | Lemma 17.3.2. Assume that $q ( y ) \neq 0$ . | `FC11-C17-U039` | Lemma 17.3.3 | Chapter 17, source L8673-L8688 | Lemma 17.3.3. Suppose $f \in L$ is a primitive isotropic vector and suppose $y \in L$ satisfies $b ( y , y )$ even and $b ( f , y ) = 0$ . Then $\psi _ { f , y }$ induces a lattice isometry of $L$ with the following properties. 1. $\psi _ { f , y } \in { 0 } ^ { \in } ( L )$ for $\epsilon = -$ as well as for $\epsilon = +$ . 2. $\psi _ { f , y }$ induces the identity on the discriminant group. 3. One has $\psi _ { f , y } = \psi _ { f , y + f }$ and the map $$ \begin{array} { r } { L _ { f } = f ^ { \perp } / \mathbb { Z } f \stackrel { \psi _ { f } } { \longrightarrow } 0 ( L ) } \\ { y + \mathbb { Z } f \longmapsto \psi _ { f , y } } \end{array} $$ is a well-defined injective group homomorphism. In particular $\psi _ { f } ( L _ { f } )$ is an abelian subgroup of $\mathrm { O } ( L )$ whose rank equals $\operatorname { r a n k } ( L _ { f } )$ . 4. $\psi _ { f } ( L _ { f } )$ is a normal subgroup of ${ \sf O } _ { f } ( L )$ , the stabilizer of $f$ . | — |

The image of the $\left( - 2 \right)$ -root lattice of $L _ { f }$ under $\psi _ { f } : L _ { f } \to { \mathsf { O } } ( L )$ is contained in the Weyl group $W ^ { - } ( f ^ { \bot } )$ considered as the subgroup of ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( L )$ generated by reflections in $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots of $f ^ { \bot }$ (and hence preserving $f$ ). 3. | `FC11-C17-U043` | Lemma | Chapter 17, source L8747-L8754 | Lemma. Suppose that a Lorentzian lattice $L$ contains a (primitive) isotropic vector $f$ . | `FC11-C18-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 18, source L8839 | In practice $L$ is often the lattice associated to a generalized Dynkin diagram $\Gamma$ . However, in general the roots corresponding to the vertices of $\Gamma$ do not define a vanishing lattice. | `FC11-C18-U002` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 18, source L8845 | In this section $( L , q )$ is a quadratic integral lattice with polarization $^ { b }$ . By ”root” we shall mean ” $\left( - 2 \right)$ -root” and accordingly we set $\epsilon = -$ . With appropriate changes all results are valid for 2-roots and $\epsilon = +$ . The set of roots in $L$ is denoted $\Delta ( L )$ , the lattice generated by these $L _ { \mathrm { r o o t } }$ , and the Weyl group ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( L )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C18-U003` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 18, source L8847 | The lattices we consider in this chapter are spanned by roots which form the nodes of a graph. If $r , r ^ { \prime }$ are such roots and $b ( \boldsymbol { r } , \boldsymbol { r } ^ { \prime } ) = 1$ , as usual, we connect the corresponding nodes by an unlabelled edge. In the lattices also pairs $\{ r , r ^ { \prime } \}$ occur for which $b ( r , r ^ { \prime } ) = - 2$ . We connect the corresponding nodes by a doubly dashed edge. The resulting graphs $\Gamma$ are called (generalized) Dynkin diagrams and the corresponding even integral lattice $L _ { \Gamma }$ they give rise to the associated rootlattice. Identifying the vertices of $\Gamma$ with roots in $L _ { \Gamma }$ , the Weyl group ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( { \Gamma } )$ of $\Gamma$ is the group generated by the reflections determined by $\Gamma$ . | — |
| `FC11-C18-U004` | Definition / construction | Chapter 18, source L8849 | The distinguished root set is $1$-connected when any two relevant roots can be joined through roots with successive pairing $1$. | — |

| `FC11-C18-U007` | Definition 18.1.2 | Chapter 18, source L8863-L8865 | Definition 18.1.2. 1. A vanishing lattice consists of a pair $( L , \Delta )$ of a lattice $L$ and a set of roots $\Delta$ spanning $L$ and forming a single orbit under $W ^ { - } ( \Delta ) =$ $\langle \sigma _ { r } \rangle _ { r \in \Delta }$ , that is $\Delta = \Delta$ . 2. A vanishing lattice $( L , \Delta )$ contains the vanishing lattice $( L ^ { \prime } , \Delta ^ { \prime } )$ if $L ^ { \prime }$ is a primitive sublattice of $L$ and $\Delta ^ { \prime } \subset \Delta$ . | — |
| `FC11-C18-U008` | Remark 18.1.3 | Chapter 18, source L8867 | Remark 18.1.3. The vertices of a generalized Dynkin diagram $\Gamma$ form a finite set of roots, say $\Delta$ , spanning the lattice $L _ { \Gamma } = \mathbb { Z } \cdot \Delta$ . If $\Delta$ is 1-connected, by Lemma 18.1.1 all roots in $\Delta$ are in the same orbit under the reflection group ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( \Delta )$ , but $\Delta$ need not be an entire orbit. Taking $\Delta = \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \Delta )$ instead of $\Delta$ then yields a vanishing lattice. In general, if the subset $\Delta$ of roots of $L$ belongs to a single ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( \Delta )$ -orbit, this procedure yields a vanishing lattice. Clearly, this also shows that such a vanishing lattice is determined by the lattice itself. | `FC11-C18-U006`, `FC11-C18-U007` |

The intersection behaviour of roots in the socle and with other roots $r \in \Lambda$ is as in the following graph: ![](images/8efac71491350b32349c96ce8640d61d191c7346b7bb81725fec158edf8c8997.jpg) In other words, $b ( r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } ) = 1$ , $b ( r _ { 2 } , r _ { 3 } ) = - 2$ , $b ( r _ { 1 } , r ) = 0$ for $r \neq r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 }$ and, finally, $b ( r , r _ { 2 } ) = b ( r , r _ { 3 } )$ for $r \neq r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } , r _ { 3 }$ . | `FC11-C18-U017` | Theorem 18.1.7 | Chapter 18, source L8906-L8915 | Theorem 18.1.7 ([60, Thm 3]). Let $( L , q )$ be a quadratic lattice of rank at least 4 containing a special subset Λ of roots which span $L$ . Then $( L , \pmb { \Lambda } )$ is a vanishing lattice. If, moreover, $L$ splits off an extra hyperbolic plane besides the socle plane, then $\mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L ) = \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \Lambda )$ . More precisely, we have: 1. • Let $U$ be the socle plane and $L = U \mathbb { \textcircled { 1 } L ^ { \prime } }$ the corresponding socle decomposition. Then $L ^ { \prime }$ is spanned by $\Lambda ^ { \prime } = \Lambda - \{ r _ { 1 } , r _ { 2 } \}$ . • Conversely, let $L$ be a quadratic lattice of rank at least 4 with a decomposition $L = U \oplus L ^ { \prime }$ , where $L ^ { \prime }$ is spanned by a 1-connected set of roots $\Lambda ^ { \prime }$ . Let $\{ e , f \}$ be the standard basis of $U$ and $r _ { 3 } \in \Lambda ^ { \prime }$ . Then the set of roots $r _ { 1 } = e - f , r _ { 2 } = - e + r _ { 3 } , r _ { 3 } , r \in \Lambda ^ { \prime }$ forms a special subset of roots. 2. With $\Lambda ^ { \prime }$ as in $\mathit { 1 }$ , the roots $r ^ { \prime } \in \Lambda ^ { \prime }$ belong to the same $\mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( L ^ { \prime } )$ -orbit. 3. $\psi _ { U } L ^ { \prime } \subset \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \Lambda )$ 4. If, moreover, $L ^ { \prime } = U ^ { \prime } \oplus L ^ { \prime \prime }$ for some hyperbolic plane $U ^ { \prime }$ , then $W ^ { - } ( \Lambda ) \ =$ ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( L )$ . In other words, the Weyl group of $L$ is generated by the reflections $\sigma _ { r }$ , where the roots $r$ belong to $\Lambda$ . | `FC11-C18-U007`, `FC11-C18-U014` |

In dimension 2 these singularities are given by: $$ f = 0 , \quad f = x ^ { p } + y ^ { q } + z ^ { r } + a x y z , \quad a \in \mathbb { C } ^ { \times } , \quad { \frac { 1 } { p } } + { \frac { 1 } { q } } + { \frac { 1 } { r } } < 1 . $$ With $J _ { f }$ the jacobian ideal, we see that the vector space $\mathbb { C } [ \left\lfloor x , y , z \right\rfloor ] / ( J _ { f } , f )$ is generated by 1 together with the monomials $x ^ { k }$ , $k = 1 , \ldots , p - 1$ , $y ^ { \ell }$ , $\ell = 1 , \ldots , q - 1$ , $z ^ { m }$ , $m = 1 , \ldots , r - 1$ , and so $\tau ( f ) = p + q + r - 2$ . Because of the relation $$ p ^ { - 1 } x f _ { x } + q ^ { - 1 } y f _ { y } + r ^ { - 1 } z f _ { z } - f = ( p ^ { - 1 } + q ^ { - 1 } + r ^ { - 1 } - 1 ) a x y z , $$ we find $\mu = p + q + r - 2$ . For all non-zero $a$ the singularities have the same Milnor number. It turns out (see [5, Ch. I.2.3]) that the isomorphism class of the singularity varies with $a$ , and so it is a unimodal singularity. | — |

| `FC11-C18-U043` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 18, source L9167 | parametrizes the smooth hypersurfaces. A generic line $\ell \subset \mathbb { P } _ { n , d }$ meets $\mathsf { D } _ { d }$ transversally in points $P$ corresponding to hypersurfaces with exactly one ordinary double point. Every such point $P$ gives rise to a vanishing cycle $\delta _ { P }$ in a nearby smooth fiber. This situation resembles the local situation we just treated. The present situation is the one Lefschetz had in mind in the classical monograph [138] and this is why the restriction $F _ { n , d }$ to the line $\ell$ is called a Lefschetz pencil. The global theory mirrors the local theory exactly. This leads to an associated vanishing lattice, but this time in cohomology, as we shall now explain. | `FC11-C18-U007` |

All vanishing cocycles are in the same orbit under the monodromy group, and $( H _ { \mathrm { p r i m } } ^ { n } ( X _ { * } ) , \Delta _ { d , n } )$ is a vanishing lattice. | `FC11-C18-U007` |

| `FC11-C19-U004` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9275-L9279 | For brevity, the classes of effective, respectively ample divisors in the N´eron– Severi lattice $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ are called effective, respectively ample classes. Likewise, the class of a nodal curve is called a nodal class. Observe that nodal classes are roots in $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ , but not conversely. In fact, $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots in the N´eron–Severi lattice are classes of divisors $D$ with $D \cdot D = - 2$ which are not necessarily effective or irreducible. Reflections in $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots in $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ generate its Weyl group. Since the corresponding hyperplane reflections extend to $H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } )$ , this group can be considered as a subgroup of $\mathsf { O } ( H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } ) )$ . We call it the Weyl group of $X$ : $$ \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( X ) : = \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \mathsf { N S } ( X ) ) \subset \mathsf { O } ( H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } ) ) . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C19-U005` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 19, source L9281 | The Weyl group of a K3 surface is a normal subgroup of the orthogonal group of its integral second cohomology. | — |

| `FC11-C19-U007` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9289 | The Hodge index theorem B.2.3 states that the intersection product on $H _ { \mathbb { R } } ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X ) =$ $H ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X ) \cap H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { R } )$ has signature $( 1 , h ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X ) - 1 )$ , and so it gives $H _ { \mathbb { R } } ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X )$ the structure of a hyperbolic vector space containing $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ . As explained in Appendix B.2, there is a preferred component $\complement _ { X }$ of the “light cone” $\{ x \in H _ { \mathbb { R } } ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X ) \mid x \cdot x > 0 \}$ , namely the one that contains the K¨ahler classes and which is called the positive cone. The group $\mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( X )$ acts on $H _ { \mathbb { R } } ^ { 1 , 1 } ( X )$ and by (16.3) it preserves the positive cone. | — |

| `FC11-C19-U028` | Remark 19.2.12 | Chapter 19, source L9424-L9426 | Remark 19.2.12. 1. In the non-algebraic situation $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ can indeed be negative definite or have a one-dimensional null-space. The last two cases occur if and only if the function field of $X$ has transcendence degree $a ( X ) = 0$ , respectively $a ( X ) = 1$ . Indeed, if an isotropic $f \in \mathsf { N S } ( X )$ exists, then $\pm f$ is effective and one can show (cf. [124, Thm. 4.1,4.2]) that a suitable multiple defines an elliptic fibration. This implies that $a ( X ) \geq 1$ . If $a ( X ) = 2$ the surface $X$ is algebraic (cf. [15, Ch. IV. Corollary 6.5]) and its N´eron–Severi lattice is not negative semi-definite. If $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ is negative definite, $X$ cannot be elliptic or algebraic, and hence $a ( X ) = 0$ . 2. By Theorem 15.2.6 any unimodular lattice $S$ of rank $\leq 2 0$ embeds uniquely in the K3 lattice provided $T = S ^ { \perp }$ is indefinite. This ceases to be true if $T$ is definite which may only occur in the non-algebraic situation. The simplest example is presented by the sublattice $S = \mathbb { O } ^ { 3 } U$ . In the standard embedding its orthogonal complement is $\mathbb { O } ^ { 2 } E _ { 8 } ( - 1 )$ . However, as we have seen (Example 1.5.1.2), there is another even unimodular rank 16 lattice which is negative definite and indecomposable, $\Gamma _ { 1 6 } ( - 1 )$ . By Theorem 2.4.1, which gives the classification of indefinite even unimodular lattices, $\Phi ^ { 3 } U \oplus \oplus ^ { 2 } E _ { 8 } ( - 1 ) \simeq \oplus ^ { 3 } U \oplus \Gamma _ { 1 6 } ( - 1 )$ . So two possible period domains then occur for (non-algebraic) K3 surfaces having the same N´eron–Severi lattice, but with different transcendental lattices. | `FC11-C01-U053`, `FC11-C02-U014`, `FC11-C15-U014` |

The period map $\mathcal { n }$ is ´etale in the sense that every $[ u ] \in D ( \Lambda )$ has an open neighbourhood $U$ so that $\mathcal { n }$ maps every connected component of $n ^ { - 1 } U$ biholomorphically to $U$ . | — |
| `FC11-C19-U030` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9446 | The above Hodge structures for varying $[ u ] \in D ( \Lambda )$ define a variation of Hodge structure over $D ( \Lambda )$ in the technical sense of [89] which is also called the tautological variation of Hodge structure. There does not exist a family of marked K3 surfaces $( X _ { u } , \varphi _ { u } )$ , $u \in D ( \Lambda )$ , with the property that the Hodge decompositions of $H ^ { 2 } ( X _ { u } )$ under the marking $\varphi _ { u }$ give the tautological variation, but we can achieve this locally as follows. Let $X _ { 0 }$ be a K3-surface. There is a locally universal deformation $p : \mathcal { X } _ { U } \to U$ of $X _ { 0 } = p ^ { - 1 } ( 0 )$ consisting of K3 surfaces, the so-called Kuranishi family for $X _ { 0 }$ . One may take for $U$ a disc centered at 0 and then there is a constant marking $\varphi _ { s } : H ^ { 2 } ( X _ { s } , \mathbb { Z } ) \stackrel { \sim } { \to } \Lambda$ over $U$ , i.e., a trivialization of the local system $R ^ { 2 } p _ { * } \mathbb { Z }$ . There are no two isomorphic (marked) fibres in the Kuranishi family. If this would be the case, there is an isomorphism $g : ( X _ { 1 } , \varphi _ { 1 } ) ( X _ { 2 } , \varphi _ { 2 } )$ , and then $\varphi _ { 1 } g ^ { * } \varphi _ { 2 } ^ { - 1 } = \mathrm { i d } _ { \Lambda }$ since $X _ { 1 } , X _ { 2 }$ are in a single trivialized family with discrete fibres in the local system $R ^ { 2 } p _ { * } \mathbb { Z }$ . This implies $\varphi _ { 1 } g ^ { * } = \varphi _ { 2 }$ so that we would get identical period points by (a variation of) Lemma 19.2.5, which contradicts that the period map is an embedding (cf. [87, Part II, §2]). | `FC11-C19-U017` |
| `FC11-C19-U031` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9458-L9462 | In this alternative description, the fiber of $\mathcal { \boldsymbol { n } }$ over a period point $\lfloor u \rfloor$ of $( X , \varphi )$ is constructed in such a way that it indeed parametrizes the supplementary data of Weyl chambers over $[ u ]$ which together with $\varphi$ define a strongly marked $\mathbf { \Delta } \mathbf { K } \mathbf { \mathcal { 3 } }$ surface. Recall first (cf. Section B.4) that the intersection form induces a nondegenerate quadratic form of signature $( 1 , 1 9 )$ on $\Lambda _ { u , \mathbb { R } } ^ { 1 , 1 }$ , and that the light cone has two components $\pm C _ { u }$ . The cone $C _ { u }$ is the union of Weyl chambers ${ \mathsf { C } } _ { u } ^ { P }$ parametrized by partitions labeled by $P$ $$ \Delta _ { u } : = \Delta _ { u } ^ { P } \cup - \Delta _ { u } ^ { P } $$ | — |
| `FC11-C19-U032` | Definition / construction | Chapter 19, source L9464-L9472 | The strong-marking cover $\widetilde D(\Lambda)$ is obtained by adjoining to each period point $u$ a choice of signed Weyl chamber determined by a partition of the roots orthogonal to $u$, and topologizing these local sheets by the stated Weyl-chamber gluing rule; its projection to $D(\Lambda)$ is locally a homeomorphism. | — |

| `FC11-C19-U034` | Proposition 19.3.2 | Chapter 19, source L9486 | Proposition 19.3.2. There is an open dense subset $\mathring { D } \left( S ^ { \perp } \right) \subset D ( S ^ { \perp } )$ so that the roots in $\Delta _ { u }$ , $\vert u \vert \in \mathcal { M } _ { S }$ , are irreducible, i.e., for some marking all roots correspond to nodal classes. Let $P$ be a partition of the roots of $S$ . The connected components of $\mathcal { M } _ { S }$ consist of the non-Hausdorff smooth analytic spaces $\mathcal { M } _ { S } ^ { \pm , P }$ of dimension $2 0 - \operatorname { r a n k } ( S )$ . | `FC11-C19-U004` |
| `FC11-C19-U035` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9508 | Let $I$ be an isotropic subspace of $\Lambda _ { 2 } \otimes \mathbb { R }$ . It corresponds to a boundary component $F _ { I } \subset \hat { D } ( \Lambda _ { 2 } )$ of $D ( \Lambda _ { 2 } )$ whose closure is of the form $\overline { { F } } _ { I } = \mathbb { P } ( I _ { \mathbb { C } } ) \cap \overline { { D ( \Lambda _ { 2 } ) } }$ (in the case the dimension of $I$ is 1 this is just a point). For the SBB compactification in our setting, only the so-called rational boundary components play a role. These are the boundary components that correspond to isotropic subspaces defined over the rationals, so that we can restrict our attention to primitive isotropic sublattices $I$ of $\Lambda _ { 2 }$ . Since the signature of $\Lambda _ { 2 }$ is $( 2 , 1 9 )$ , such isotropic sublattices can only have rank 1 or 2. In the first case the boundary component is a single point, in the second case $F _ { I }$ is isomorphic to a half-plane in $\mathbb { P } ( I _ { \mathbb { C } } )$ . If $I \subset J$ , where $I$ is a rank 1 isotropic sublattice and $J$ a rank 2 isotropic sublattice, then $F _ { I }$ is contained in the closure of $F _ { J }$ . | — |

| `FC11-C19-U039` | Definition / construction | Chapter 19, source L9578 | A K3 surface in arbitrary characteristic is a smooth proper surface with trivial canonical bundle and $H^1(O_X)=0$. | — |
| `FC11-C19-U040` | Definition / construction | Chapter 19, source L9580 | For a surface in positive characteristic, the $\ell$-adic Betti numbers are the dimensions of its $\ell$-adic étale cohomology groups. | — |
| `FC11-C19-U041` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9584 | As demonstrated in [23], the Betti numbers of a K3 surface turn out to be the same as in the complex case: $b _ { 1 } ( X ) = 0$ and $b _ { 2 } ( X ) = 2 2$ . However, the N´eron– Severi group can have rank $\rho ( X )$ up to 22. We concentrate on the maximal value. A K3-surface with $\rho ( X ) = 2 2$ is called a supersingular K3 surface. So for those surfaces $\mathsf { N S } ( X )$ is an integral lattice of rank 22. It has signature $( 1 , 2 1 )$ but it turns out to be non-unimodular. | — |
| `FC11-C19-U042` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 19, source L9586 | 19.5.B Supersingular K3-lattices. The N´eron–Severi lattices for supersingular K3 surfaces all belong to the class of the so-called supersingular K3-lattices defined below (cf. Definition 19.5.1).7 We explain how these can be classified using the theory we have developed. We also give their construction as given in loc. cit. | `FC11-C19-U041` |
| `FC11-C19-U043` | Definition 19.5.1 | Chapter 19, source L9588-L9593 | Definition 19.5.1. Let $p$ be a prime number. A non-degenerate quadratic lattice $N$ of rank $n$ is called a supersingular $\kappa \mathrm { 3 }$ -lattice with Artin invariant $\boldsymbol { \mathsf { O } }$ if 1. $n \equiv 6$ mod 8, 2. $N$ is Lorentzian, that is, its signature is $( 1 , n - 1 )$ , 3. $N$ is $p$ -elementary, i.e., $p N ^ { * } \subset N \subset N ^ { * }$ , 4. $N ^ { * } / N$ is an $\mathbb { F } _ { p }$ -vector space of even dimension $2 \sigma$ . | — |
| `FC11-C19-U044` | Construction / comparison | Chapter 19, source L9595 | For a supersingular K3-lattice, the rank/signature conditions force index $4$ modulo $8$; at $p=2$, type I and type II are distinguished by whether the discriminant quadratic form is $\frac12\mathbf Z/\mathbf Z$-valued or assumes a quarter-integral value. | `FC11-C19-U043` |
| `FC11-C19-U045` | Proposition 19.5.2 | Chapter 19, source L9597-L9606 | Proposition 19.5.2. For any odd prime $p$ a supersingular $K \mathcal { 3 }$ -lattice $N$ is uniquely determined by $n$ and $\boldsymbol { \mathsf { O } }$ with $n , \sigma$ as in Definition 19.5.1. Such lattices only exist if $0 < \sigma < { \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } n }$ . If $p = 2$ we have $0 < \sigma \leq { \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } n$ . There are two types of discriminant forms: $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \boldsymbol { q } ^ { \# } = \mathbb { O } ^ { \sigma - 1 } \boldsymbol { u } _ { 1 } \oplus \boldsymbol { v } _ { 1 } , 1 \le \sigma < \displaystyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } \boldsymbol { n } , \quad ( T y p e ~ I ) , } \\ & { \boldsymbol { q } ^ { \# } = \langle 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus \langle 3 \cdot 2 ^ { - 1 } \rangle \oplus \mathbb { O } ^ { \sigma - 2 } \boldsymbol { u } _ { 1 } \oplus \boldsymbol { v } _ { 1 } , \sigma \ge 2 , \quad ( T y p e ~ I I ) . } \end{array} $$ If $\sigma = 1$ one has $q ^ { \# } = v _ { 1 }$ and if $\sigma \ = \ { \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } n$ , $q ^ { \# }$ is of type II. In all these cases there exists an up to isometry unique 2-elementary supersingular K3-lattice with discriminant form $q ^ { \# }$ . | `FC11-C19-U043` |

| `FC11-C19-U049` | Theorem ([199, §3,4]) | Chapter 19, source L9671 | Theorem ([199, §3,4]). Supersingular $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surfaces in characteristic 2 with Artin invariant $\sigma$ have a moduli variety $M _ { \sigma }$ of dimension $\sigma - 1$ . | `FC11-C20-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L9697 | In Section 20.1 we describe the automorphism group of a K3 surface through the action on cohomology, making use of the Torelli theorem 19.2.1. By definition, the subgroup of symplectic automorphisms is the subgroup fixing a holomorphic 2-form. For algebraic K3 surfaces this group is of finite index in the full group and the quotient group is well understood. This gives a finiteness criterion for automorphisms in terms of the N´eron–Severi lattice. | `FC11-C19-U012` |

| `FC11-C20-U006` | Criterion 20.1.3 | Chapter 20, source L9747-L9748 | Criterion 20.1.3 ([189, §7, Theorem 1]). The automorphism group of a complex projective $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface $X$ is finite if and only if $0 ^ { - } ( S ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( S )$ is finite. In other words, ${ \mathrm { A u t } } ( X )$ is finite if and only if the Weyl group of $X$ (cf. 19.1) embeds as a finite index subgroup in the isometry group of the N´eron–Severi lattice $S$ . | — |

| `FC11-C20-U009` | Example / comparison | Chapter 20, source L9755-L9763 | More generally, let $\mathcal { F } ^ { \rho }$ be the set of isometry classes of even lattices $S$ of signature $( 1 , \rho - 1 )$ for which $0 ^ { - } ( S ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( S )$ finite. The images of $G$ in $\mathrm { O } ( \Lambda )$ for the members of the universal family of $( \iota , G )$ -marked K3 surfaces over a connected component of $\mathcal { M } _ { \iota ( G ) }$ belong to the same conjucacy class in $\mathrm { O } ( \Lambda )$ . | `FC11-C20-U024` |
| `FC11-C20-U030` | Proposition 20.2.7 | Chapter 20, source L9882-L9886 | Proposition 20.2.7. Let $G$ be a finite group of automorphisms of a K3 surface $X$ , then: 1. If $G$ acts symplectically, $X / G$ has at most du Val singularities and its minimal resolution is a K3 surface; 2. If $G$ does not act symplectically, $X / G$ is either rational or birational to an Enriques surface. It is isomorphic to an Enriques surface if $G$ has order 2 and acts freely on $X$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U031` | Theorem 20.3.1 | Chapter 20, source L9897-L9898 | Theorem 20.3.1 (Mukai, [159]). A finite group $G$ arises as a subgroup of $\operatorname { A u t } _ { s } ( X )$ for some projective $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface $X$ if and only if $G$ is isomorphic to a subgroup of the Mathieu group $M _ { 2 3 }$ for which the induced action on $\Omega$ has at least 5 orbits. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U032` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L9941 | 20.4.A An inventory. Suppose $G$ acts symplectically on a K3 surface $X$ . By Proposition 20.2.7, $Y = X / G$ has at most du Val singularities and its minimal resolution $\widetilde { Y }$ is a K3 surface. The classes of the components in the minimal resolution $\ddot { Y }$ generate a sublattice of $H ^ { 2 } ( { \widetilde { Y } } , \mathbb { Z } )$ which is denoted $M _ { Y } ^ { \prime }$ . Its primitive closure, $M _ { Y }$ , is called the resolution lattice of the quotient K3 surface. | `FC11-C20-U030` |

| `FC11-C20-U035` | Lemma 20.4.3 | Chapter 20, source L9987-L9992 | Lemma 20.4.3. Let $X$ be a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface and $G$ a finite abelian group acting symplectically on $X$ with quotient $Y = X / G$ . Let $E _ { Y }$ be the collection of classes of the exceptional curves of the minimal resolution $\widetilde { Y }$ of $Y$ , $M _ { Y } ^ { \prime }$ the lattice they span and $M _ { Y }$ its resolution lattice, i.e., the primitive closure of $M _ { Y } ^ { \prime }$ in $H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } )$ . 1. There exists an isometry $\varphi : M _ { Y } \ { \overset { \sim } { \to } } \ M _ { G }$ such that $\varphi ( E _ { Y } ) = E _ { G }$ ; hence $\varphi$ sends $M _ { Y } ^ { \prime }$ to $M _ { G } ^ { \prime }$ inducing a group isomorphism $\bar { \varphi } : M _ { Y } / M _ { Y } ^ { \prime } \stackrel { \sim } { } M _ { G } / M _ { G } ^ { \prime }$ . 2. The embedding $M _ { Y } { \overset { \varphi } { \to } } M _ { G } \hookrightarrow \Lambda$ extends to an $M _ { G }$ -marking $H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) \stackrel { \sim } { \to } \Lambda$ . Any two embeddings of $M _ { Y } \hookrightarrow \Lambda$ are conjugate under ${ \sf O } ( \Lambda )$ . | — |

| `FC11-C20-U038` | Proposition 20.4.5 | Chapter 20, source L10016-L10027 | Proposition 20.4.5. 1) Let $\widetilde { Y }$ be a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface admitting an $( M _ { G } , E _ { G } )$ -marking. • The $( M _ { G } , E _ { G } )$ -marking gives a canonical identification $$ \iota ^ { * } : \mathrm { E x t } ^ { 1 } ( G ) \stackrel { \sim } { \to } M _ { Y } / M _ { Y } ^ { \prime } . $$ • There exists a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ -surface $X$ and an embedding $\iota : G \hookrightarrow \operatorname { A u t } _ { s } ( X )$ such that the minimal resolution of singularities of $X / \iota ( G )$ is isomorphic to $\widetilde { Y }$ . Moreover, $\widetilde { Y }$ has an $( M _ { G } , E _ { G } )$ -marking inducing $\iota ^ { * }$ . 2) Conversely, every $K \mathcal { 3 }$ -surface $X$ with symplectic $G$ -action comes in this way from some $( M _ { G } , E _ { G } )$ -marked $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface. Two such marked $K \mathcal { 3 }$ -surfaces come from (possibly different) embeddings $\iota , \iota ^ { \prime } : G \hookrightarrow \operatorname { A u t } _ { s } ( X )$ if and only if $\iota ^ { \prime } = \iota \circ \alpha$ where $\alpha$ is an automorphism of the group $G$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U039` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10038 | A marked K3 surface with symplectic $G$-action and induced lattice embedding $\iota:G\hookrightarrow O(\Lambda)$ is called $(\iota,G)$-marked. | `FC11-C20-U024` |
| `FC11-C20-U040` | Theorem 20.4.6 | Chapter 20, source L10042-L10047 | Theorem 20.4.6 ( [169, Thm. 4.7]). Let $G$ be a finite abelian group acting symplectically (and effectively) on some $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface $X$ . Then the $G$ -action on the $K \mathcal { 3 }$ lattice induced by some marking of $X$ is up to conjugacy uniquely determined by $G$ . To achieve our goal, first recall that by Corollary 20.2.6 over connected components of the moduli space $\mathcal { M } _ { \iota ( G ) }$ incorporating the action of the group $G$ , we stay within the same conjugacy class. So there are at most two classes. To compare K3 surfaces whose moduli points are in different connected components we will use the moduli space $\mathcal { M } _ { M _ { G } }$ related to the quotients $Y = X / G$ . This comparison makes essential use of the fact that $\mathcal { M } _ { M _ { G } }$ does not depend on the embedding of $G$ in the orthogonal group of the K3 lattice. We divide the proof accordingly into several steps. | `FC11-C20-U029` |

| `FC11-C20-U049` | Lemma 20.5.2 | Chapter 20, source L10179-L10182 | Lemma 20.5.2. Suppose $X$ is a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface with an involution $g : X \to X$ . If $g$ is symplectic, it has 8 isolated fixed points and the quotient is a surface with 8 ordinary double points with a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface as its minimal resolution. In that case, $\mathrm { T r } ( g ^ { * } \| _ { H ^ { 2 } ( X ) } ) = 6$ and the invariant lattice has rank 14, i.e., $a + c = 1 4$ , where $( a , b , c )$ is the type of $g ^ { * }$ . If $g$ is not symplectic, there are at most disjoint fixed point curves and $Y = X / G$ is smooth. If $\mathrm { T r } ( g ^ { * } \| _ { H ^ { 2 } ( X ) } ) = - 2$ , then $a + c = 1 0$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U050` | Remark 20.5.3 | Chapter 20, source L10193 | Remark 20.5.3. If the second case of the above lemma occurs and no fixed curves are present, $Y$ is an Enriques surface. We give two examples where $Y$ is a rational surface. First, consider an elliptic pencil $\lambda C + \mu C ^ { \prime } = 0$ , $( \lambda : \mu ) \in \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ , on $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ where $C , C ^ { \prime }$ are smooth elliptic curves intersecting transversally (in 9 points), and let $Y$ be the blow-up of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ in the nine base points of the pencil. Let $X$ be the double cover of $Y$ branched in the strict transform of $C + C ^ { \prime }$ . Note that $e ( Y ) = 3 + 9 = 1 2$ and $e ( X ) = 2 4 $ as it should. Classical algebraic geometry teaches us that coordinates can be fixed so that a pencil of cubics passes through 4 fixed points in general position and the pencil is determined by the choice of 4 further points, giving 8 moduli. The resulting surface $X$ is a K3 surface admitting an involution which produces $Y$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U051` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10195 | The second example starts from a pencil of elliptic curves on $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ defined by a pair of smooth curves of bidegree $( 2 , 2 )$ meeting transversally (in 8 points). So here we have to blow up the 8 intersection points which gives a smooth rational surface with $e = 4 + 8 = 1 2$ as well. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U052` | Proposition 20.5.4 | Chapter 20, source L10197-L10202 | Proposition 20.5.4. Let $X$ be a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface with an involution $g$ inducing $g ^ { * }$ on $H ^ { 2 } ( X )$ and set $G = \langle g ^ { * } \rangle$ . If $g$ is an Enriques involution, i.e., a fixed point free involution, then $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G } \simeq$ $U ( 2 ) \mathbb { \oplus } E _ { 8 } ( - 2 )$ of signature $( 1 , 9 )$ . The invariants $( a ( g ^ { * } ) , b ( g ^ { * } ) , c ( g ^ { * } ) )$ are $( 0 , 2 , 1 0 )$ . If $g$ is a Nikulin involution (i.e., a symplectic involution), the discriminant group of $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G }$ is isomorphic to $\oplus ^ { 8 } \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ . The invariants of $g ^ { * }$ are $( 6 , 0 , 8 )$ and the signature of $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G }$ equals $( 3 , 1 1 )$ . | — |

| `FC11-C20-U057` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10263 | 20.6.B Characterizing Kummer surfaces. If $X$ is a complex two-torus, we denote by $Y = \mathsf { K m } ( X )$ the associated (smooth) Kummer surface and by $\pi ^ { \prime } : X \xrightarrow { } Y$ the canonical rational 2-to-1 map. We need an auxiliary Hodge-theoretic result. | `FC11-C20-U002` |

| `FC11-C20-U060` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10329-L10333 | The lattice Nikulin involution is the involution used to model the cohomological action of a symplectic involution on a marked K3 surface. | `FC11-C20-U024` |
| `FC11-C20-U061` | Proposition 20.7.1 | Chapter 20, source L10339-L10348 | Proposition 20.7.1. Let $X$ be a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface admitting a Nikulin involution ?? and let $G$ be the cyclic group generated by this involution. Then there is a commutative diagram $$ \begin{array} { r l r } { H ^ { 2 } ( X ) \xrightarrow [ \lambda ] { \sim } } & { \ N } & { } \\ { \iota \bigg \downarrow } & { } & { \iota _ { \mathrm { N i k } } \bigg \downarrow } \\ { H ^ { 2 } ( X ) \xrightarrow [ \lambda ] { \sim } } & { \ N . } \end{array} $$ In other words, a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface with a Nikulin involution admits a marking which turns this involution into a lattice Nikulin involution. Its period point belongs to $D ( \Lambda ^ { G } )$ , and7 $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G } \simeq \Lambda ^ { G } = \mathbb { O } ^ { 3 } U \mathbb { O } E _ { 8 } ( - 2 )$ . A K3 surface $X$ with period point in $D ( \Lambda ^ { G } )$ admits a Nikulin involution which is, up to conjugation by an element of $\mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( X )$ , induced by the lattice Nikulin involution ??Nik as in (20.16). If the period point is general, ${ \mathsf { N S } } ( X ) \simeq \Lambda _ { G } \simeq E _ { 8 } ( - 2 )$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U062` | Proposition 20.7.2 | Chapter 20, source L10361-L10362 | Proposition 20.7.2 ([83, §2]). | `FC11-C20-U063` | Proposition 20.7.3 | Chapter 20, source L10373-L10376 | Proposition 20.7.3. | `FC11-C20-U068` | Corollary 20.8.3 | Chapter 20, source L10483 | Corollary 20.8.3. A $K \mathcal { 3 }$ -surface $X$ whose transcendental lattice $T$ has rank 2 is obtained from a product of two elliptic curves by means of a Shioda–Inose structure. Such surfaces have no moduli and correspond one-to-one to points of the set of isometry classes of positive definite even lattices of rank 2. | `FC11-C20-U064` |
| `FC11-C20-U069` | Example / comparison | Chapter 20, source L10485-L10491 | Example 20.8.4. There are non-algebraic K3 surfaces with transcendental lattice $U^{\oplus3}$ whose Nikulin quotient is not Kummer, and also non-algebraic surfaces admitting Shioda--Inose structures. For the non-standard embedding $U^{\oplus3}\subset\Lambda_{K3}$ with orthogonal complement $\Gamma_{16}(-1)$, the domain $D(U^{\oplus3})$ is the period domain of K3 surfaces admitting a Shioda--Inose structure; its general point is non-algebraic. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U070` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10495 | In this section $X$ is any compact (smooth) complex surface with a faithful action of a finite group $G$ , and $\widetilde { Y }$ denotes the minimal resolution of $Y = X / G$ . We write $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) , H ^ { 2 } ( Y ) , \ldots$ to mean integral cohomology while $H _ { 1 } ( X ) , H _ { 2 } ( Y ) , . . .$ . stands for integral homology. We relate the intersection lattices of $X$ and $\widetilde { Y }$ . The main result, Theorem 20.9.6, is quite technical, but it is of crucial importance for applications to K3 surfaces in Section 20.2. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U071` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 20, source L10497 | 20.9.A The set-up. By [35], the action of $g \in G$ on the tangent space at a fixed point can be linearized which implies that the fixed locus of $g$ in $X$ is either empty or a disjoint union of smooth curves and points. The image in $Y$ of an isolated fixed point of $g$ acting on $X$ by definition is a quotient singularity, hence by Proposition 4.5.2, a du Val singularity. We assume that there are only (finitely many) isolated fixed points and we employ the following notation: | `FC11-C04-U058` |
| `FC11-C20-U072` | Lemma 20.9.1 | Chapter 20, source L10522-L10529 | Lemma 20.9.1. Let $\pi : X ^ { \prime } Y ^ { \prime } = X ^ { \prime } / G$ be as above. Then 1. $\mathfrak { t } ^ { * } \pi ^ { * } : H ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } ) \to H ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } )$ is multiplication by $n = \| G \|$ . $\pi ^ { * } \mathfrak { t } ^ { * } : H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) \to H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } )$ is induced by the action of $\textstyle \sum _ { g \in G } g \in \mathbb { Z } [ G ]$ 2. $\pi ^ { * } : H ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } ) \to H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } )$ has its image in $H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) ^ { G }$ . The kernel of $\pi ^ { * }$ as well as the kernel of $\mathfrak { t } ^ { * } : H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) ^ { G } H ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } )$ is $n$ -torsion. 3. If $H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } )$ is $a$ free $\mathbb { Z }$ -module, then $H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) ^ { G }$ is the primitive closure of $\operatorname { I m } ( \pi ^ { * } )$ | — |
| `FC11-C20-U073` | Remark 20.9.2 | Chapter 20, source L10536-L10540 | Remark 20.9.2. If $X ^ { \prime }$ (and hence $Y ^ { \prime }$ ) are oriented compact connected manifolds, the transfer homomorphisms are the same as the Gysin homomorphisms. If $X ^ { \prime }$ and $Y ^ { \prime }$ are oriented but not necessarily compact, the Gysin maps $\pi _ { ! } : H _ { c } ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) \to H _ { c } ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } )$ extend the transfer maps, i.e., there is a commutative diagram $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { H ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) \xrightarrow { \quad \overbrace { \mathrm { { ~ \delta ~ } } \mathbf { t } ^ { * } \quad } ^ { \pi ^ { * } } } H ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } ) } \\ & { \quad \quad \bigg \| ^ { k } } \\ & { H _ { c } ^ { k } ( X ^ { \prime } ) \xrightarrow { \pi _ { ! } } H _ { c } ^ { k } ( Y ^ { \prime } ) . } \end{array} $$ | `FC11-C20-U053` |
| `FC11-C20-U074` | Lemma 20.9.3 | Chapter 20, source L10544-L10545 | Lemma 20.9.3. 1. One has $\begin{array} { r } { \widetilde { \pi } ^ { * } \widetilde { \pi } _ { ! } ( u ) = \sum _ { g \in G } g ^ { * } ( u ) } \end{array}$ , $u \in H ^ { 2 } ( X )$ . 2. The Gysin morphism $\tilde { \pi } _ { ! } : H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { X } ) \to H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } )$ restricted to $H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G }$ is injective and multiplies the intersection form by $\| G \|$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U075` | Lemma 20.9.4 | Chapter 20, source L10574-L10575 | Lemma 20.9.4. With $X , G , \Sigma , Y , { \widetilde { Y } }$ as in $\boldsymbol { \xi }$ 20.9.A (with $G$ abelian), let $G _ { p } , p \in$ $\Sigma$ , be the stabilizer of $p$ in $G$ . Suppose that $X$ is simply connected and that the homomorphism $\oplus _ { p \in \Sigma } G _ { p } \to G$ induced by inclusion is surjective. Then $H _ { 1 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) = 0$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U076` | Example / comparison | Chapter 20, source L10590 | Example 20.9.5. If $G$ is a finite abelian group acting symplectically on a K3 surface $X$ , Tables 20.4.1 and 20.4.2 show that the assumption on the stabilizers is satisfied in this case. However, the conclusion that $H _ { 1 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) = 0$ in this case follows also directly since $\widetilde { Y }$ is a K3 surface. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U077` | Theorem 20.9.6 | Chapter 20, source L10606-L10625 | Theorem 20.9.6. Let $X$ be a compact complex surface admitting an action by a finite abelian group $G$ of order $n$ , let $\pi : X \to Y = X / G$ be the quotient map, $\widetilde { Y }$ the minimal resolution of singularities of $Y$ , $X ^ { \prime }$ the complement of the fixed points of $G$ , $Y ^ { \prime } = X ^ { \prime } / G$ , and $j ^ { \prime } : X ^ { \prime } \hookrightarrow X$ , ${ \widetilde { j } } : Y ^ { \prime } \hookrightarrow { \widetilde { Y } }$ the inclusions. Assume that $Y : = X / G$ has at most cyclic quotient singularities, that $X$ is simply connected and that $H _ { 1 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) = 0$ . Then the following assertions hold: 1. Setting $\theta : = ( j ^ { \prime } { } ^ { * } ) ^ { - 1 } \circ \pi ^ { * } \circ { \widetilde { j } } ^ { * } : H ^ { 2 } ( { \widetilde { Y } } ) H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G }$ , the image of $\theta$ belongs to H (cf. (20.23)) and there is an exact sequence $$ 0 \to M _ { Y } / M _ { Y } ^ { \prime } \to H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) / M _ { Y } ^ { \prime } \overset { \theta } { \to } \mathsf { H } \to 0 . $$ The factor group ${ M _ { Y } } / { M _ { Y } ^ { \prime } }$ is canonically isomorphic to $\operatorname { E x t } ^ { 1 } ( G )$ . 2. $\theta$ induces an isomorphism $H ^ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) / M _ { Y } \stackrel { \simeq } { } \mathsf { H }$ and an injection $M _ { Y } ^ { \bot } \hookrightarrow \mathsf { H }$ . This is an injection of free $\mathbb { Z }$ -modules of the same rank, but $\theta \| _ { M _ { Y } ^ { \perp } }$ multiplies the intersection form with $\| G \|$ . In case $G$ is cyclic, ${ \mathsf { H } } = H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G }$ . 3. The elementary divisors $e _ { 1 } \| e _ { 2 } \| \cdots \| e _ { r }$ of the discriminant group of $M _ { Y }$ all divide $n = \| G \|$ . 4. The length of the discriminant group of the sublattice $\mathsf { H } \subset H ^ { 2 } ( X )$ is at least equal to $\mathrm { r a n k } ( H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G } ) - r$ . Its elementary divisors are obtained by omitting the integers equal to 1 from the list $( n / e _ { 1 } , \ldots , n / e _ { r } , n , \ldots , n )$ (the size of this list equals $\operatorname { r a n k } ( \mathsf { H } ) = \operatorname { r a n k } ( H ^ { 2 } ( X ) ^ { G } ) = b _ { 2 } ( { \bar { Y } } ) - \operatorname { r a n k } ( M _ { Y } ) )$ . The discriminant bilinear form of $\mathsf { H }$ assumes values in $n ^ { - 1 } { \mathbb { Z } } / { \mathbb { Z } }$ . If the intersection form on $X$ is even, the discriminant quadratic form takes values in $( 2 n ) ^ { - 1 } \mathbb { Z } / \mathbb { Z }$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U078` | Example / comparison | Chapter 20, source L10645 | Example 20.9.7. For an involution one has $n = 2$ , and all elementary divisors of the discriminant group of $M _ { Y }$ are equal to 2. Hence, in this case the discriminant group of the invariant lattice is isomorphic to $\oplus ^ { \ell } \mathbb { Z } / 2 \mathbb { Z }$ , $\ell = b _ { 2 } ( \widetilde { Y } ) - \ell ( M _ { Y } )$ , and if the intersection form on $X$ is even, then the discriminant quadratic form can be of type I, or of type II (cf. Definition 1.7.2). | `FC11-C01-U097` |
| `FC11-C20-U079` | Remark 20.9.8 | Chapter 20, source L10647 | Remark 20.9.8. Contrary to the claim in [169, §8.5], the homomorphism $\pi ^ { \prime } { ^ { * } }$ : $H ^ { 2 } ( Y ^ { \prime } ) H ^ { 2 } ( X ^ { \prime } ) ^ { G }$ is not in general surjective, for instance if $b _ { 1 } ( X ^ { \prime } ) \neq 0$ . | — |
| `FC11-C20-U080` | Example / comparison | Chapter 20, source L10649-L10656 | Table 20.9.1 records the invariant lattices $\Lambda^G$ for symplectic finite-group actions on K3 surfaces, using the corrected cohomological calculation in which the relevant pullback need only be surjective up to a finite subgroup of $H^3(G)$. | — |
| `FC11-C20-U081` | Definition / construction | Chapter 20, source L10664 | A generalized Kummer surface is the minimal resolution associated to the indicated finite quotient of a complex torus. | `FC11-C20-U002` |
| `FC11-C20-U082` | Definition / construction | Chapter 20, source L10666 | A generalized Shioda--Inose structure is a triple $(X,A,G)$ in which $X$ is a K3 surface, $A$ an abelian surface, $G$ acts symplectically on $X$ and finitely on $A$, and the quotients $X/G$ and $A/G$ give the same generalized Kummer surface. | `FC11-C20-U002`, `FC11-C20-U081` |
| `FC11-C21-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 21, source L10690-L10694 | 21.1.A Moduli for Enriques surfaces. The fixed point free involution $j$ on the K3 surface $X$ induces an involution $j ^ { * }$ on $H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } )$ which can be described abstractly, using a properly chosen isometry $H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } ) \ \simeq \Lambda$ . To explain this, we rewrite the K3 lattice as $\Lambda = U \oplus W \oplus W ^ { \prime }$ , where $W , W ^ { \prime }$ are copies of the Enriques lattice. This last decomposition can be used to define the lattice Enriques involution $$ \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( u , v , v ^ { \prime } ) = ( - u , v ^ { \prime } , v ) , \quad ( u , v , v ^ { \prime } ) \in U \oplus W \oplus W ^ { \prime } . $$ | — |
| `FC11-C21-U002` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 21, source L10702-L10706 | Such a marking $\varphi$ is called a marking for $( X , j )$ . Since $Y = X / G = X / \langle j \rangle$ has no non-zero holomorphic 2-forms, the involution $j ^ { * }$ acts as −id on $H ^ { 2 , 0 } ( X )$ and hence also on the corresponding period point $[ \omega ] = \varphi [ H ^ { 2 , 0 } ( X ) ]$ . So $[ \omega ]$ belongs to $D ( \Lambda ) \cap \mathbb { P } ( \Lambda _ { G } ) = D ( \Lambda _ { G } )$ where $\Lambda _ { G }$ is the anti-invariant lattice. For later reference, we observe $$ \Lambda _ { { \cal G } } = \{ ( u , w , - w ) \in { \cal U } \oplus { \cal W } \oplus { \cal W } ^ { \prime } \} \simeq { \cal U } \oplus \Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( 2 ) . $$ | `FC11-C21-U001` |
| `FC11-C21-U003` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 21, source L10716-L10720 | and that, conversely, such points correspond to period points for Enriques surfaces coming from marked pairs $( X , j )$ of a K3 surface equipped with an Enriques involution. Such period points will be called period points of Enriques surfaces. The group $$ \Gamma _ { \mathsf { E n r } } : = \{ \gamma \in \mathsf { O } ( \Lambda ) \mid \gamma \circ l _ { \mathsf { E n r } } = \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } ^ { \circ } } \gamma \} $$ | — |
| `FC11-C21-U004` | Definition 21.1.1 | Chapter 21, source L10728-L10736 | Definition 21.1.1. For any subset $R$ of a lattice, we let $\langle R \rangle$ be the smallest primitive sublattice containing $R$ . 1. An ordered pair of $\left( - 2 \right)$ -roots $\{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \}$ , $r \in \Lambda$ , which satisfy $r \cdot \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) = 0$ is called an $\iota _ { \mathsf { E } \mathsf { n r } }$ -adapted root pair . The collection of all $\iota _ { \mathsf { E } \mathsf { n r } }$ -adapted root pairs of $\Lambda$ is denoted by $\Delta ( \Lambda , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } )$ . If $\{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( \Lambda , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } )$ , we set $r ^ { \pm } : = r \pm \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r )$ . The corresponding root $\pmb { r } \in \Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } }$ in the Enriques lattice is given by $\pi ^ { * } r = r ^ { + }$ . In particular $\Delta ^ { \pm } ( X ) ) \subset { \mathsf { N S } } ( X )$ . 2. Two sets $R , R ^ { \prime }$ of roots in $\Lambda$ are said to be equivalent if the sublattices $\langle R \rangle$ and $\langle R ^ { \prime } \rangle$ of $\Lambda$ are isometric under ${ \mathrm { O } } ( \Lambda )$ . 3. Geometry enters by passing to roots that are nodal classes, that is, classes of smooth $\left( - 2 \right)$ -curves: $$ \begin{array} { r c l } { { \Delta ( X ) } } & { { : = } } & { { \{ \{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( \Lambda , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ) \mid r \mathrm { ~ i s ~ a ~ n o d a l ~ c l a s s } \} , } } \\ { { \Delta ^ { \pm } ( X ) } } & { { : = } } & { { \{ r ^ { \pm } \mid \{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( X ) \} , } } \\ { { \Delta ( Y ) } } & { { : = } } & { { \{ r \mid \{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( X ) \} \subset \Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } } . } } \end{array} $$ | — |

If $\{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \}$ and $\{ s , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( s ) \}$ are two $\iota _ { \mathsf { E } \mathsf { n } \mathsf { r } }$ -adapted root pairs, there exists $\gamma \in \Gamma _ { \mathsf { E n r } }$ for which $\gamma ( r ^ { - } ) = s ^ { - }$ . | — |

| `FC11-C21-U009` | Proposition 21.1.3 | Chapter 21, source L10752-L10763 | Proposition 21.1.3. Let $( X , j )$ be a pair of a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface equipped with an Enriques involution and let $Y = X / G$ be the quotient Enriques surface. Use a marking for $( X , j )$ to identify $H ^ { 2 } ( X )$ with $\Lambda$ and $H ^ { 2 } ( Y ) /$ Tors with $\Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } } = \Lambda ^ { G } \big ( \frac { 1 } { 2 } \big )$ . Let $\Delta ( X )$ be the nodal type of $X$ and set $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { \mathsf { C } _ { X , j } ^ { \mathrm { a m p } } : = \{ x \in \Lambda ^ { G } \otimes \mathbb { R } \mid x \in \mathsf { C } _ { X } , x \cdot r ^ { + } > 0 \ f o r \ a l l \ \{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( X ) \} } \\ & { \quad \quad \quad = \{ y \in \Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } } \otimes \mathbb { R } \mid y \in \mathsf { C } _ { Y } , y \cdot r > 0 \ f o r \ a l l \ r \in \Delta ( Y ) \} . } \end{array} $$ Then $\mathsf { C } _ { X , j } ^ { \mathrm { a m p } } = \mathsf { C } _ { X } ^ { \mathrm { a m p } } \cap \Lambda ^ { G } \otimes \mathbb { R }$ corresponds to the ample cone of $Y$ . Its closure is a fundamental domain for the action of $\mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \Delta ^ { + } ( X ) ) : = s u b g r o u p$ of ${ \mathsf { O } } ( \Lambda ^ { G } )$ generated by $\sigma _ { r ^ { \circ } } \sigma _ { \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } } ( r ) = \sigma _ { r ^ { + } }$ , $\{ r , \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( r ) \} \in \Delta ( X )$ on the positive cone of $\Lambda ^ { G } \otimes \mathbb { R }$ . The latter Weyl group depends only on the set of nodal classes on $Y$ , more precisely, on $\Delta ( Y )$ . | `FC11-C21-U006` |

| `FC11-C21-U011` | Lemma 21.1.5 | Chapter 21, source L10782 | Lemma 21.1.5. Let ?? be the period point of a marked pair $( X , j )$ , $X$ a $K \mathcal { 3 }$ surface $X$ equipped with an Enriques involution $j$ , and let the nodal set $\Delta _ { \omega }$ be given by equation (21.6). Then any partition $\Delta _ { \omega } = \Delta _ { \omega } ^ { + } \cup - \Delta _ { \omega } ^ { + }$ closed under taking sums determines $\Delta ( X )$ up to equivalence. In other words, the nodal type of $X$ is completely determined by $\Delta _ { \omega }$ . | `FC11-C21-U006` |
| `FC11-C21-U012` | Remark 21.1.6 | Chapter 21, source L10784 | Remark 21.1.6. Let $\omega$ be the period point of a $j$ -marked K3 surface $( X , \varphi )$ and let $\gamma \in \Gamma _ { \mathsf { E n r } }$ . Then $\omega ^ { \prime } = \gamma ( \omega )$ is the period point of an isomorphic $j ^ { \prime }$ -marked K3- surface $( X ^ { \prime } , \gamma \circ \varphi )$ . Under ?? the sets $\Delta _ { \omega }$ and $\Delta _ { \omega ^ { \prime } }$ correspond and the same holds for their Weyl chambers. If $r \in \Delta ( X )$ is indecomposable, also $\gamma ( r )$ is indecomposable. Hence isomorphic Enriques surfaces have the same nodal type. | `FC11-C21-U006` |

| `FC11-C21-U019` | Definition / construction / notation | Chapter 21, source L10820-L10824 | Observe now that for any $x \in \Lambda$ the linear forms $b ( x , - )$ and $b ( \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( x ) , - )$ evaluated on $\Lambda _ { G }$ only differ by a sign and so the hyperplanes $H _ { x } = \{ [ \omega ] \in D _ { \Lambda _ { G } } \|$ $\boldsymbol { \omega } \cdot \boldsymbol { x } = 0 \}$ and $H _ { \iota _ { \mathsf { E n r } } ( x ) }$ are the same. | `FC11-C21-U026` | Theorem 21.2.3 | Chapter 21, source L10876-L10883 | Theorem 21.2.3 ([16, 172]). Let $Y$ be an Enriques surface without nodal curves. | `FC11-C21-U032` | Remark 21.2.6 | Chapter 21, source L10952 | Remark 21.2.6. The group $\mathsf { O } ^ { - } ( \langle \Delta ^ { - } \rangle )$ contains ${ \mathsf { W } } ^ { - } ( \Delta ^ { - } )$ . By Lemma 21.1.8 $\Delta ^ { - } ( - \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } )$ is a root lattice and so is an orthogonal sum of (classical) irreducible root lattices of type $A$ -??- $E$ . This decomposition is unique up to permuting isometric irreducible sublattices (by the unicity of the decomposition of a positive definite lattice stated by Theorem 1.12.3) and so by Corollary 17.2.3, $0 ^ { - , \# } ( \langle \Delta ^ { - } \rangle ) / \mathsf { W } ^ { - } ( \Delta ^ { - } )$ is a product of permutation groups coming from isometric factors of the same type provided $\Delta ^ { - } ( - \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } )$ does not contain irreducible sublattices of type $D _ { 4 }$ . In that case the occurrence of such a factor contributes an extra factor, namely the cyclic group $C _ { 3 }$ . | `FC11-C01-U148`, `FC11-C17-U016`, `FC11-C21-U016` |

| `FC11-C21-U038` | Remark 21.2.12 | Chapter 21, source L10996-L11002 | Remark 21.2.12. Recall (cf. Lemma 4.1.5) that $\widetilde { T } _ { 2 , 3 , 6 }$ is an isometric copy of the Enriques lattice. Let $r$ be the root $\alpha _ { 9 }$ in the diagram for Lemma 4.1.5. Then $\mathbb { Z } r \oplus r ^ { \perp } \subset \Lambda _ { \mathsf { E n r } }$ is isometric to $\widetilde { T } _ { 2 , 4 , 6 } \subset \widetilde { T } _ { 2 , 3 , 6 }$ , the so-called Reye lattice. Using this, D. Allcock [1] and F. Cossec–I. Dolgachev [45] gave an alternative proof of Corollaries 21.2.9 and 21.2.10. | `FC11-C04-U009` |

| `FC11-A-U003` | Lemma A.1.1 | Appendix A, source L11022 | Lemma A.1.1. Let $R$ be a principal ideal domain and let $M$ be a free finitely generated $R$ -module and $M ^ { \prime } \subset M$ a submodule of the same rank. Then $M / M ^ { \prime }$ is an $R$ -torsion module and every torsion module is of this form. More precisely, $M$ admits a basis $\{ e _ { 1 } , \ldots , e _ { r + s } \}$ such that $M ^ { \prime } = \oplus _ { k = 1 } ^ { r } R e _ { k } \oplus \oplus _ { k = 1 } ^ { s } R \cdot ( d _ { k } e _ { r + k } )$ with $d _ { j }$ non-units such that $d _ { 1 } \| d _ { 2 } \| \cdots \| d _ { s }$ , and hence $M / M ^ { \prime } \simeq R / d _ { 1 } R \oplus \cdot \cdot \cdot \oplus R / d _ { s } R$ . | — |
| `FC11-A-U004` | Theorem A.1.2 | Appendix A, source L11026 | Theorem A.1.2 (Elementary divisor theorem). Let $R$ be a principal ideal domain and let $T$ be a finitely generated torsion $R$ -module. There is an isomorphism $T \simeq$ $R / d _ { 1 } R \oplus \cdot \cdot \cdot \oplus R / d _ { s } R$ , with $d _ { j }$ non-units such that $d _ { 1 } \| d _ { 2 } \dotsm \| d _ { s }$ . The ideals generated by $d _ { j }$ , the elementary divisors, are uniquely determined by $T$ and the resulting decomposition is the invariant factor decomposition of $T$ . | — |
| `FC11-A-U005` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix A, source L11028-L11032 | There is another canonical decomposition for $T$ . First some terminology. For $p \in R$ irreducible, one says that a finitely generated torsion module is $p$ -primary if every element is annihilated by some power of $p$ . Setting $$ T _ { p } = \{ x \in T \mid p ^ { n } \cdot x = 0 \quad { \mathrm { f o r ~ s o m e ~ p o w e r ~ } } p ^ { n } { \mathrm { ~ o f ~ } } p \} , $$ | — |
| `FC11-A-U006` | Definition / construction | Appendix A, source L11034-L11040 | For an irreducible $p\in R$, the maximal $p$-primary submodule is $T_p=\{x\in T:p^n x=0\text{ for some }n\}$, and a finite torsion module has the Sylow decomposition $T=\bigoplus_pT_p$ with each $T_p$ decomposed into homogeneous summands $R/p^eR$. | — |

Note that the above isomorphism for $T _ { p }$ is not canonically determined by $T$ as demonstrated in Example A.1.4.2 below. | — |
| `FC11-A-U008` | Construction / comparison | Appendix A, source L11042 | The invariant-factor and primary decompositions are related by factoring each elementary divisor into prime powers and applying the Chinese remainder theorem. | — |
| `FC11-A-U009` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix A, source L11044 | We frequently use the concept of length: the length $\ell ( G )$ of a finite abelian torsion $R$ -module $G$ is equal to the minimal number of generators (by definition). In particular, the length of $T _ { p }$ is at most $s$ , and for primes diving $d _ { 1 }$ the length equals $s$ . | — |
| `FC11-A-U010` | Definition / construction | Appendix A, source L11044-L11046 | The length $\ell(G)$ of a finite torsion module is its minimal number of generators; for a $p$-primary module it is the total number of homogeneous summands, and the length of a finite torsion module is the maximum of its primary lengths. | — |

| `FC11-A-U013` | Example / comparison | Appendix A, source L11052-L11056 | We assume $R = \mathbb { Z }$ . Suppose the elementary divisors of $T$ are $( 4 , 1 2 , 4 8 )$ . Then $T _ { 2 } \simeq \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z } \oplus \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z } \oplus \mathbb { Z } / 1 6 \mathbb { Z }$ , $T _ { 3 } \simeq \mathbb { Z } / 3 \mathbb { Z } \oplus \mathbb { Z } / 3 \mathbb { Z }$ and $\ell ( T ) = 3$ . Suppose $\{ e _ { 1 } , e _ { 2 } , e _ { 3 } \}$ is an ordered basis of $T _ { 2 }$ , i.e., a basis reflecting the above isomorphism. Then $\{ f _ { 1 } = e _ { 1 } + 2 e _ { 2 } + 4 e _ { 3 } , f _ { 2 } = e _ { 2 } + 4 e _ { 3 } , f _ { 3 } = 3 e _ { 3 } \}$ is also an ordered basis of $T _ { 2 }$ and so the isomorphism $T _ { 2 } \simeq \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z } \oplus \mathbb { Z } / 4 \mathbb { Z } \oplus \mathbb { Z } / 1 6 \mathbb { Z }$ is not unique. We finish this section by recalling some modular arithmetic. The multiplicative group of units $R ^ { \times }$ of the ring $R = \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z }$ has order $\varphi ( m )$ , the number of elements modulo $m$ that are coprime with $m$ . In what follows elements $r$ of $R$ are viewed as additive classes of integers modulo $m$ and we write $r \ = \ \bar { x }$ , where $x \in \mathbb { Z }$ . If the group $( \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times }$ is cyclic and $r$ is a generator, one says that $r$ is a primitive generator modulo $m$ . We denote the (multiplicative) cyclic group of order $n$ by $C _ { n }$ (in contrast to the additive version $\mathbb { Z } / n \mathbb { Z }$ ). The next result can be found in elementary textbooks on number theory, e.g. [113]. | — |
| `FC11-A-U014` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix A, source L11054 | We finish this section by recalling some modular arithmetic. The multiplicative group of units $R ^ { \times }$ of the ring $R = \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z }$ has order $\varphi ( m )$ , the number of elements modulo $m$ that are coprime with $m$ . In what follows elements $r$ of $R$ are viewed as additive classes of integers modulo $m$ and we write $r \ = \ \bar { x }$ , where $x \in \mathbb { Z }$ . If the group $( \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times }$ is cyclic and $r$ is a generator, one says that $r$ is a primitive generator modulo $m$ . We denote the (multiplicative) cyclic group of order $n$ by $C _ { n }$ (in contrast to the additive version $\mathbb { Z } / n \mathbb { Z }$ ). | — |
| `FC11-A-U015` | Lemma A.1.5 | Appendix A, source L11058-L11084 | Lemma A.1.5. 1. The units of the finite cyclic groups have the following structure: (a) For $p$ odd the groups $( \mathbb { Z } / p ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times }$ and $( \mathbb { Z } / 2 p ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times }$ are cyclic of order $p ^ { k } - p ^ { k - 1 }$ . For $p = 2$ we have $$ ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times } = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { 1 } & { i f k = 1 } \\ { C _ { 2 } ~ g e n e r a t e d ~ b y } { \overline { { - 1 } } } \right.} & { i f k = 2 } \\ { C _ { 2 } \times C _ { 2 ^ { k - 2 } ~ } g e n e r a t e d ~ b y } { ( \overline { { - 1 } } } , { \overline { { \pm 3 } } } ) & { i f k \geq 3 . } \end{array} $$ (b) In general, writing $m = 2 ^ { k } \Pi _ { p } p ^ { k _ { p } }$ , where the product is over odd primes $p$ , we have $$ ( \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times } \simeq ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times } \times \prod _ { p } ( \mathbb { Z } / p ^ { k _ { p } } \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times } . $$ In particular, only for $m = 2 , 4 , p ^ { k }$ and $2 p ^ { k }$ there exist primitive generators modulo $m$ . 2. The group $\sf D ( \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z } )$ of units of $\mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z }$ modulo squares has the following structure: (a) In the cases $m = p ^ { k }$ and $2 p ^ { k }$ the group $\sf D ( \mathbb { Z } / m \mathbb { Z } )$ is cyclic of order 2 and generated by a non-square modulo $p ^ { k }$ : D(ℤ/????ℤ) = {1, ?? mod ????} ≃ ??2, $p$ an odd prime and ?? a non-square modulo $p ^ { k }$ (b) For $p = 2$ we have: $$ { \mathsf { D } } ( \mathbb { Z } / 2 ^ { k } \mathbb { Z } ) = { \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { \{ { \bar { 1 } } \} } & { f o r k = 1 , } \\ { \{ { \bar { 1 } } , { \bar { 3 } } \} \simeq C _ { 2 } } & { f o r k = 2 , } \\ { \{ { \bar { 1 } } , { \bar { 3 } } , - { \bar { 3 } } , - { \bar { 1 } } \} \simeq C _ { 2 } \times C _ { 2 } } & { i f k \geq 3 . } \end{array} \right. } $$ | — |
| `FC11-A-U016` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix A, source L11090-L11094 | The $p$ -adic valuation and $\mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ . The field of $p$ -adic numbers $\mathbb { Q } _ { p }$ is the completion of $\mathbb { Q }$ with respect to the $p$ -adic valuation $v _ { p }$ on $\mathbb { Q }$ which is defined as follows: first, one sets $v _ { p } ( 0 ) = \infty$ . Next, for a non-zero number $\begin{array} { r } { x = p ^ { k } \frac { u } { v } } \end{array}$ with $\operatorname* { g c d } ( u , p ) =$ $\operatorname* { g c d } ( v , p ) = 1$ , one sets $$ v _ { p } ( x ) = k , \quad \\| x \\| _ { p } = p ^ { - k } . $$ | — |
| `FC11-A-U017` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix A, source L11108-L11112 | By definition of a completion, we have a natural embedding $$ \iota _ { p } : \mathbb { Q } \hookrightarrow \mathbb { Q } _ { p } . $$ | — |
| `FC11-A-U018` | Theorem A.2.1 | Appendix A, source L11137 | Theorem A.2.1. 1. Suppose $p$ is an odd prime. Then $x \in \mathbb { Q } _ { p } ^ { \times }$ can be written uniquely as $x = u p ^ { k }$ with $u$ a unit in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ . It is a square if and only if $k$ is even and $u \equiv 1$ mod $p$ . The group $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { p } )$ is isomorphic to the Klein group with representatives $1 , p$ , $u , u p$ , where $u$ is a unit in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p }$ such that $u$ mod $p$ is a non-square. The group $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { p } )$ is cyclic of order two, generated by a non-square in $\mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { \times }$ . 2. A dyadic number $x \in \mathbb { Q } _ { 2 }$ can be uniquely written as $x = u \cdot 2 ^ { k }$ , $u$ a unit in $\mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ . It is a square if and only if $k$ is even and $u \equiv 1$ mod 8. The group $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Q } _ { 2 } )$ is isomorphic to $C _ { 2 } \times C _ { 2 } \times C _ { 2 }$ with generators 2, 3, 5 mod 8. $A$ unit $u \in \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 }$ is a square if and only if $u$ mod 8 is a square, and $\mathsf { D } ( \mathbb { Z } _ { 2 } )$ is isomorphic to the Klein group $( \mathbb { Z } / 8 \mathbb { Z } ) ^ { \times } = C _ { 2 } \times C _ { 2 }$ with generators $3 , 5$ mod 8. | — |

| `FC11-A-U025` | Corollary A.3.4 | Appendix A, source L11170-L11171 | Corollary A.3.4. Let $( V , q )$ be a (non-degenerate) quadratic $\mathbb { Q }$ -vector space of dimension $\geq ~ 2$ . | `FC11-A-U030` | Lemma A.4.1 | Appendix A, source L11216 | Lemma A.4.1 (Hensel’s lemma). Let $f ( X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { n } ) \in \mathbb { Z } _ { p } [ X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { n } ]$ and suppose that $\pmb { a } = ( a _ { 1 } , \ldots , a _ { n } ) \in \mathbb { F } _ { p } ^ { n }$ is a simple zero of $f$ mod $p$ , that is, $f ( { \pmb a } ) \equiv 0$ mod $p$ and the gradient $\nabla f$ at $\mathbf { a }$ is not identically zero mod $p$ . Then this zero can be lifted to $\mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { n }$ . In other words, there exists $\pmb { b } \in \mathbb { Z } _ { p } ^ { n }$ with $f ( \pmb { b } ) = 0$ and ${ \pmb a } \equiv { \pmb b }$ mod $p$ . | — |
| `FC11-A-U031` | Example / comparison | Appendix A, source L11218-L11224 | Example A.4.2. For odd $p$, the equation $ax^2+by^2=c$ over $\mathbf F_p$, with $a,b\ne0$, always has a solution. If $a,b,c$ lift to $p$-adic units, a solution lifts by Hensel; in particular $(a,b)_p=1$ after taking $c=1$. | — |

| `FC11-B-U002` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11484-L11488 | The metric is K¨ahler if $\kappa$ is a closed form, which then is called the associated K¨ahler form. Prominent examples are smooth complex projective varieties, and complex tori. Using the K¨ahler form $\kappa$ , we shall make use of a general positivity condition related to complex submanifolds $Y \subset X$ , namely the inequality $$ \int _ { Y } \underbrace { \kappa \wedge \cdot \cdot \cdot \wedge \kappa } _ { k } > 0 , \quad k = \dim Y . $$ | — |

| `FC11-B-U004` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11496-L11500 | For a compact Kähler manifold, $H^{p,q}(X)$ is the subspace of complex de Rham cohomology represented by closed forms of type $(p,q)$, and the Hodge decomposition is $H^k(X,\mathbf C)=\bigoplus_{p+q=k}H^{p,q}(X)$ with $\overline{H^{p,q}(X)}=H^{q,p}(X)$. | `FC11-B-U002` |

| `FC11-B-U024` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11606 | 1. The most basic examples of surfaces are $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ and $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ . These are birationally equivalent to each other (see e.g. Example B.5.1) and any surface birational to $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ is called a rational surface. Apart from the just mentioned surfaces also the Hirzebruch surfaces $F _ { n } , n \in \mathbb { N }$ , belong to this class. The surface $F _ { n }$ is the total space of the $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ -bundle over $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ possessing a unique section $C _ { n }$ with self-intersection $- n$ . The Hirzebruch surface $F _ { 0 }$ is just $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ and only $\pmb { F } _ { 1 }$ has an exceptional curve. These surfaces are all simply connected and $b _ { 2 } ( \mathbb { P } ^ { 2 } ) = h ^ { \scriptscriptstyle \mathrm { 1 , 1 } } ( \mathbb { P } ^ { \scriptscriptstyle 2 } ) = 1$ while $b _ { 2 } ( { \pmb F } _ { n } ) = h ^ { 1 , 1 } ( { \pmb F } _ { n } ) = 2$ . | — |

| `FC11-B-U027` | Example / comparison | Appendix B, source L11620-L11625 | A K3 surface is a simply connected surface with trivial canonical bundle; standard examples include Kummer surfaces, smooth quartics in $\mathbf P^3$, and smooth complete intersections of three quadrics in $\mathbf P^5$. | — |
| `FC11-B-U028` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11656 | An Enriques surface is a surface with $p_g=b_1=0$ and canonical bundle of order $2$. | — |
| `FC11-B-U029` | Example / comparison | Appendix B, source L11658-L11664 | Example ( [19, 4.18]). Let $( x _ { 1 } : y _ { 1 } : z _ { 1 } : x _ { 2 } : y _ { 2 } : z _ { 2 } )$ be projective coordinates in $\mathbb { P } ^ { 5 }$ and define $$ \iota ( x _ { 1 } : y _ { 1 } : z _ { 1 } : x _ { 2 } : , y _ { 2 } : z _ { 2 } ) = ( x _ { 1 } : y _ { 1 } : z _ { 1 } : - x _ { 2 } : - y _ { 2 } ) $$ An invariant quadric is of the form $Q ^ { \prime } ( x _ { 1 } , y _ { 1 } , z _ { 1 } ) + Q ^ { \prime \prime } ( x _ { 2 } , y _ { 2 } , z _ { 2 } )$ . For a generic choice of three such quadrics, the intersection is a smooth surface and the calculation rules for complete intersections tell us that this is a K3 surface. Note that the fixed point set of $\iota$ consists of the two planes $x _ { i } = y _ { i } = z _ { i } = 0$ , $i = 1 , 2$ , and three invariant quadrics cut out three conics on each of these planes and so their intersection is empty for a generic choice of the quadrics. If this is the case, the involution acts on the intersection without fixed points. By what we just said, the quotient is an Enriques surface. All Enriques surfaces can be shown to either arise in this way or they are, in a technical sense, “limits” of such surfaces; in particular they are all algebraic. | — |

| `FC11-B-U031` | Definition B.4.1 | Appendix B, source L11690-L11704 | Definition B.4.1. 1. A Hodge structure of weight 2 on a free $\mathbb { Z }$ -module $L$ of finite rank consists of a decomposition $$ { \cal L } _ { \mathbb { C } } = { \cal L } ^ { 2 , 0 } \oplus { \cal L } ^ { 1 , 1 } \oplus { \cal L } ^ { 0 , 2 } , $$ such that $L ^ { 2 , 0 }$ is the complex conjugate of $L ^ { 0 , 2 }$ and $L ^ { 1 , 1 }$ is self-conjugate. This implies that there exist subspaces $L$ and $L ^ { \prime \prime }$ of $L _ { \mathbb { R } }$ such that $$ { \cal L } _ { \mathbb { R } } = { \cal L } ^ { \prime } \oplus { \cal L } ^ { \prime \prime } , \quad { \cal L } _ { \mathbb { C } } ^ { \prime } = { \cal L } ^ { 1 , 1 } , { \cal L } _ { \mathbb { C } } ^ { \prime \prime } = { \cal L } ^ { 2 , 0 } + { \cal L } ^ { 0 , 2 } . $$ 2. If $^ { b }$ is a non-degenerate integral form on $L$ we say that $L$ is polarized by $b$ , if the two Riemann bilinear relations (B.8) and (B.9) hold. 5 3. A polarized weight 2 Hodge structure is said to be of $\kappa 3$ type if $\dim L ^ { 2 , 0 } = 1$ and $L ^ { \prime } \subset L _ { \mathbb { R } }$ is of Lorentzian type, that is, has signature $( 1 , \mathrm { d i m } L ^ { \prime } - 1 )$ . | — |
| `FC11-B-U032` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11706-L11716 | Polarized weight-$2$ Hodge structures on $(L,b)$ with fixed $h^{2,0}$ are parametrized by the period domain of $b_\mathbf C$-isotropic $h^{2,0}$-planes $P$ satisfying $b_\mathbf C(u,\bar u)>0$ for $u\ne0$; for K3 type these are isotropic positive projective lines. | `FC11-B-U031` |
| `FC11-B-U033` | Construction / comparison | Appendix B, source L11718-L11724 | The period domain is the homogeneous space $O(r_+,r_-)/(SO(r_+-1)\times O(1,r_-))$, equivalently the corresponding special-orthogonal quotient, and it is connected. | `FC11-B-U032` |
| `FC11-B-U034` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11726 | A marking $\varphi:H_X\xrightarrow{\sim}L$ transports the polarized Hodge structure to $L$ and determines its period point in $D(L)$ by the subspace $\varphi(H^{2,0}(X))\subset L_\mathbf C$. | `FC11-B-U031` |
| `FC11-B-U035` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11730-L11734 | Minimal models. Classification of surfaces begins with a reduction to minimal surfaces. To explain this, we need the concept of a $( - 1 )$ -curve6 $E$ on a complex algebraic surface; by definition $E$ is a smooth rational curve with $E \cdot E = - 1$ . Such a curve arises under the process of blowing up a surface at a point. To describe this, we assume that we have chosen coordinates $( u , v )$ in an open subset $U$ on the surface such that the point $p$ is the origin. Now consider $$ \widetilde { U } : = \{ ( ( u , v ) , ( U : V ) ) , \in U \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \mid ( u : v ) = ( U : V ) \} $$ | — |
| `FC11-B-U036` | Example / comparison | Appendix B, source L11738 | Example B.5.1. Let $p , q \in \mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ be two distinct points and let $L$ be the line connecting $p$ and $q$ . Blowing up $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ in $p$ and $q$ transforms the line $L$ in an exceptional curve.7 Blowing down this curve yields $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ and this procedure shows that $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ and $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ are two distinct minimal models within the same birationality class. | — |
| `FC11-B-U037` | Lemma B.5.2 | Appendix B, source L11742-L11746 | Lemma B.5.2. Let $X$ be a compact complex surface, $\sigma : { \tilde { X } } X$ the blow-up in a point $p$ and $E = \sigma ^ { - 1 } p$ the exceptional curve. Recalling (B.2) for the notation, we have the following cohomological results: 1. $\sigma ^ { * } : H ^ { 2 } ( X , \mathbb { Z } ) \to H ^ { 2 } ( { \widetilde { X } } , \mathbb { Z } )$ is an injection; 2. ${ \mathsf { H } } _ { \widetilde { X } } \simeq { \mathsf { H } } _ { X } \oplus \langle - 1 \rangle$ , where the second summand is spanned by the class of $E$ . | — |
| `FC11-B-U038` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11748-L11752 | Intermezzo on the Kodaira dimension. In this subsection we broaden our scope and change notation accordingly: $X$ stands for a compact K¨ahler variety and $L$ for a holomorphic line bundle on $X$ . Assuming $L$ has holomorphic sections, a choice of a basis $\{ s _ { 0 } , s _ { 1 } , \ldots , s _ { N } \}$ for the vector space of holomorphic sections defines the meromorphic map $$ \begin{array} { r l } & { f _ { L } : X \to \mathbb { P } ^ { N } } \\ & { \qquad x \mapsto ( s _ { 0 } ( x ) : s _ { 1 } ( x ) : \cdot \cdot \cdot : s _ { N } ( x s ) ) . } \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC11-B-U039` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11754 | This is not defined at points where all sections of $L$ vanish. | `FC11-B-U038` |
| `FC11-B-U040` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11756 | If $f _ { L }$ is everywhere defined and is an embedding, $L$ is called very ample and likewise for a divisor $D$ with $L = \odot _ { X } ( D )$ . Lastly, $L$ (or $D$ ) is called ample if for some positive $m$ the bundle $L ^ { \otimes m }$ (or the divisor $m D$ ) is very ample. This implies that $L \cdot C > 0$ for all curves $C \subset X$ , since $L \cdot ( m C )$ is the degree of the curve $c$ as embedded in $\mathbb { P } ^ { N }$ by means of $f _ { L ^ { \otimes m } }$ . Similarly, if $X$ has dimension $d$ , we have $L \cdots L > 0$ . We conclude that if $d = 2$ , for $D$ ample, we have $D \cdot C > 0$ and $D ^ { 2 } > 0$ . $d$ times | — |
| `FC11-B-U041` | Criterion / construction | Appendix B, source L11758 | Kleiman's criterion in the stated surface form says that a divisor with positive self-intersection and positive intersection with every curve is ample. | — |
| `FC11-B-U042` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix B, source L11760-L11764 | The canonical bundle $K _ { X }$ of $X$ is the line bundle associated to the sheaf $\Omega _ { X } ^ { n }$ of holomorphic $n$ -forms where $n = \dim X$ . A divisor whose line bundle is $K _ { X }$ is called a canonical divisor, also denoted by $K _ { X }$ . The $m$ -th tensor power of $K _ { X }$ is the $m$ -th pluricanonical bundle and we set $$ P _ { m } ( X ) = h ^ { 0 } ( K _ { X } ^ { \otimes m } ) , \quad \mathrm { t h e } m { \cdot } \mathrm { t h p l u r i g e n u s ~ o f } X . $$ | — |
| `FC11-B-U043` | Definition B.5.3 | Appendix B, source L11768-L11772 | Definition B.5.3. Let $X$ be a compact complex variety. The Kodaira dimension of $X$ is the number $$ \begin{array} { r } { \kappa ( X ) = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { - \infty } & { \mathrm { ~ i f ~ } P _ { m } ( X ) = 0 \mathrm { ~ f o r ~ a l l ~ } m \in \mathbb N } \\ { \operatorname* { m a x } _ { m \in \mathbb Z _ { > 0 } } \dim f _ { K _ { X } ^ { \otimes m } } ( X ) } & { \mathrm { ~ o t h e r w i s e } . } \end{array} \right. } \end{array} $$ | — |
| `FC11-B-U044` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11776 | A bielliptic surface is a surface with $b_2=2$ admitting a holomorphic locally trivial elliptic-curve bundle over an elliptic curve. | — |
| `FC11-B-U045` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11778 | A properly elliptic surface is a surface of Kodaira dimension $1$ admitting an elliptic fibration over a curve. | `FC11-B-U043` |
| `FC11-B-U046` | Definition / construction | Appendix B, source L11780 | A surface of general type is a surface with Kodaira dimension $2$; its minimal model is characterized here as non-rational with $c_1^2>0$. | `FC11-B-U043` |
| `FC11-B-U047` | Theorem B.5.4 | Appendix B, source L11784 | Theorem B.5.4 (Enriques--Kodaira classification). Every minimal compact Kähler surface belongs to exactly one class, ordered by Kodaira dimension: minimal rational or ruled surfaces ($\kappa=-\infty$); two-dimensional tori, K3, Enriques, or bielliptic surfaces ($\kappa=0$); minimal properly elliptic surfaces ($\kappa=1$); or surfaces of general type ($\kappa=2$). | `FC11-B-U043` |
| `FC11-C-U001` | Definition / construction / notation | Appendix C, source L11798 | For a symmetric form $b$ on an $R$-module $V$, the correlation map $b_V:V\to V^*$ sends $x$ to $[y\mapsto b(x,y)]$; non-degeneracy means injectivity and unimodularity means bijectivity. | — |
| `FC11-C-U002` | Lemma C.1.1 | Appendix C, source L11802 | Lemma C.1.1. Let $\{ w _ { 1 } , \ldots , w _ { r } \}$ be a basis of $W$ .
